The constant now points at the real domain, but the real lesson is in the README: a domain move needs all THREE of keeping the old name served through a transition, publishing a new APK, and only then retiring the old name. A phone in the field can also be repaired by hand — the pairing screen edits the address — which is why that field exists and why it must never be removed.
283. **The driver app is handed out as a public link, not out of a CI artifact** (owner: "driver appni gitni ichidan emas 1 link orqali skachat qilib oladgan qilib ber"). Reaching an Actions artifact needs a GitHub account, a zip and a file transfer — three steps a warehouse cannot do with a truck waiting, and a driver can do none of them. `/driver` is public because a driver is not a user of this system and never will be: no login exists to give them. Nothing there is a secret — the APK carries no credentials, and a phone is inert until somebody pairs it with a six-character code minted on the batch screen, which is what actually authorises a trip. The page carries the install warning Android is about to show, written out before it appears, and a QR of itself so the warehouse can put it on a screen instead of dictating a URL.
284. **Storage is the record for the published build; there is no table and no settings row.** One APK under one key, its details in a JSON object beside it. There is exactly one current build, replacing it IS the operation, and a second place to remember it is a second place to disagree. What gets published is validated by CONTENT rather than by filename — the first four bytes must be a ZIP header — because `.apk` on the end of a name is a claim by whoever uploaded it, and this is the one file the company tells its drivers to trust enough to switch off "unknown sources" for. The version string is typed by a person and lands inside a `Content-Disposition` filename, so it is filtered to a safe set rather than escaped.
285. **A batch that never left is CANCELLED, not deleted — and `cancelled` was already the answer.** The owner needed to clear test batches and plans he had made in production before go-live ("endi shularni o'chira olmayabman"). `cancelled` has been in the `batches_status_check` constraint since M3, the board already files it under the archive drawer (`batches/page.tsx:16`), the batch card already hides its controls, the tracking service already refuses it (`tracking/devices.ts:65`), and every report filters on an explicit status list that excludes it. The state was understood everywhere and simply unreachable: there was no action that could set it. This is the missing door, not a new room.
286. **Hard-deleting a batch would silently orphan history that has no foreign key to protect it.** A survey of the live database found seven real FKs into `batches` (all `NO ACTION`, two of them `NOT NULL` on the driver tables — so a delete would be refused anyway), and beneath them the ones that matter: **10,588 `box_movements` rows** carry `ref_type='batch'` + `ref_id` with NO constraint at all, plus 2,130 `audit_log` and 2,305 `events` rows keyed the same way. Nothing in the database would stop a delete from cutting the ground out from under them, and nothing would complain afterwards — a box's history would simply point at a batch that no longer exists. A soft cancel keeps every one of those references true, which is the same reason receipts, boxes and costs are voided rather than deleted.
287. **What cancelling refuses is the whole design.** The button sits on the same board as real trucks, so: departed or later is refused outright (the code is on the customs papers and the cargo is between two countries); a live cost entry or a live client charge is refused (somebody has already accounted for it); a member box in any state other than `planned`/`loading` is refused rather than guessed at, because "give them back to stock" would then be a lie about where the cargo is; and a reason is required, like every other void here. A VOIDED cost deliberately does not block — otherwise one mistaken cost entry would pin a junk batch to the board for ever, which is asserted directly. Released boxes get the same `box_movements` shape a short-loaded box gets (`finishLoading`), the plan is cancelled with the batch so it cannot claim a batch that no longer forms, and any paired driver phone is revoked so it stops reporting against a dead trip.
288. **A plan is cancellable only before approval, and that is a one-door rule.** After approval the plan owns a batch holding really-reserved boxes, so the way out is `cancelBatch`, which gives the cargo back and takes the plan down with it. Two doors into the same room would be two chances to leave boxes reserved against a plan nobody can see any more. Gated on `plans.manage`; cancelling a BATCH is gated on `batches.depart_close` rather than the loader's `scan.load` — departure is the loader's call because he is standing at the truck, but retiring the trip is a manager's.
289. **A revoked driver phone was told 401, and 401 means "try again for ever".** Found by an adversarial pass over `cancelBatch` before it shipped. `ingestPositions` refused a revoked device with `unauthorized` → HTTP 401, which the Android client treats as a server hiccup: it keeps its queue and retries on every tick, into a SQLite table with no ceiling. Only `trip_finished` → 410 makes the handset call `clearTrip()` and stop the service — and `Store.clearTrip`'s own comment says "trip finished OR LINK REVOKED", so the phone was always meant to stop here and nothing ever produced the answer that makes it. Now a revoked device is `trip_finished`; an unknown token stays `unauthorized`. This also required KEEPING `token_hash` on revoke — a device the ingest cannot find at all is indistinguishable from a bogus token and cannot be told anything. The existing test asserted `unauthorized`, i.e. it encoded the bug; it is rewritten with the reason in it. Latent since the driver app shipped, and `cancelBatch` is what would have made it common.
290. **Cancelling now closes the open work on the record, and the report stops counting it.** Two more from the same pass. `cancelTasksFor(tx, entityType, ids)` is step 8 of the house void idiom and was missing, so a task raised on a batch stayed open on somebody's day with a link to a trip that is over. And `batchRegister` (`reports/queries.ts:304`) had NO status filter at all — cancelled batches would sit in the register and its XLSX for ever, which is precisely the list the owner was clearing. The distinction the codebase keeps: a record's OWN list dims a retired row (you went looking for it), a REPORT filters it out (it is an aggregate, and a trip that never happened is not a row). Watch the spelling when following this: tasks and custom fields say `'batch'`/`'plan'`, the audit log and event stream say `'batch'`/`'load_plan'`.
291. **The first real APK upload failed twice over, and the second failure had already written the file.** (a) The publish form was a server action, and server actions cap their request body at **1 MB** by default — the APK is 2.3 MB. Raising that cap would have raised it for every form in the app, so the upload moved to a route handler instead, where the only ceiling is the 150 MB one this module states itself. (b) Behind it sat a worse one: `audit_log.entity_id` is a `uuid` column and the audit call passed the literal `'current'`, so Postgres refused the row — AFTER `publishDriverApk` had already written the APK to storage. The screen said "error", the file was live, and the two disagreed. There is only ever one published build, so it has no id of its own: `DRIVER_APP_AUDIT_ID`, a fixed uuid, keeps every publish on one auditable timeline. Verified by actually posting 2.3 MB through the running server and downloading it back byte-for-byte — the unit tests could not have caught either, because both live in the framework and the column type, not in the module's logic.
292. **The client conversation comes in through a hand-run script that keeps no session, and the privacy promise is enforced by the SCHEMA.** Owner: 95 % of client contact happens on Telegram, from managers' personal accounts, and it leaves with the manager. He chose the user-account route with the risks stated and accepted. Phase 1 imports history only: `scripts/tg-import.ts` logs in with a code the manager types, reads, and exits — the session lives in memory for the run and is never written anywhere, so the server holds no credential to anybody's personal Telegram. There is no code path in it that SENDS, because sending is what gets accounts limited. Only conversations whose phone is already in the client book are stored; a manager's family, friends and other business are read past in silence — not stored, not counted by name, not logged. That promise is kept by `tg_messages.client_id NOT NULL`: there is nowhere to put a message belonging to nobody.
293. **Everything the import DECIDES lives away from anything that talks to Telegram.** `crm/telegram-import.ts` holds two pure functions — which dialogs are ours, and what one message becomes — and the script is a thin shell around them, converting gramjs objects into a four-field shape before any decision is made. That is what makes the interesting parts testable with no account, no network and no phone: the impostor case (a peer whose display name is a client code but whose number is not), the contacts problem (Telegram reveals a phone only to contacts, so `no_phone` is reported separately from `not_a_client` — one is fixable by saving contacts, the other is somebody else's chat), and Telegram stamping `date` in SECONDS, which read as milliseconds files every conversation in January 1970 and sorts correctly against itself.
294. **`telegram` (GramJS) is pinned and archived on purpose.** npm marks it deprecated in favour of `teleproto`, a fork with one maintainer created in 2025; `@mtcute/node` was the other candidate and installs `better-sqlite3`, a native module the `node:22-slim` image cannot compile — it would have broken the owner's Docker build, not a test. GramJS is pure JS, is the most widely exercised of the three, and pinned exactly in the lockfile it can never publish a surprise. This dependency will hold session access to twelve personal accounts if phase 2 ships, so the trade taken is "no future security fixes" over "a new maintainer's account is now in that path"; if it must be replaced, `teleproto` is API-compatible.
295. **A board that moves a card optimistically satisfies an assertion the server never saw.** The CRM funnel spec moved a lead to another stage, asserted it was on screen, and navigated on. The kanban places a card the moment it is tapped and reconciles afterwards, so that assertion passes on client state alone — the next navigation then raced the write, and on a slower runner the reload showed the lead still in its old stage. Green here for weeks, red in CI. The step now RELOADS and re-asserts before moving on, which is what asks the server, and it is the pattern the same spec already used ten lines earlier for `move-next`. The second half of the same fix: after `?scope=all` the spec names the stage instead of trusting the one the funnel opens on — that default is "the first stage that has anything in it", which depends on what every other lead in the database is doing, and under `scope=all` that includes leads the spec never created. Same family as #154, but timing and scope rather than leftovers.
296. **The conversation list is one row per client, and the useful fact on it is who spoke last.** Phase 2 of the client chat. `DISTINCT ON (client_id) … ORDER BY client_id, sent_at DESC` walks the `(client_id, sent_at)` index once and takes the top of each group, rather than the obvious "latest message per client" shapes that sort the whole table (#152's lesson, applied to a table that only grows). `waitingOnUs` — the client spoke last and nobody answered — is computed in the query rather than left for the eye to work out from a name, because an unanswered customer is the one thing on that screen that costs money. The list orders newest-first AFTER the distinct, since `DISTINCT ON` must sort by the grouping column; the thread reads oldest-first because that screen IS the conversation, while the client card's panel is a reference ("what did we last say") and stays newest-first.
297. **Reading a client's conversation is gated, not merely hidden from the menu.** `/suhbatlar` and the thread each carry their own check (#198): these are things customers said in confidence to their manager, and a warehouse operator has no business in them. Asserted by an e2e that logs in as one.
298. **Three of my own phase-1 tests only worked on a FRESH database.** They wrote `tg_messages` with hard-coded peer and message ids, so a second run of the same suite hit the unique index it was written to praise — and a fourth asserted its client was first in the whole list, which a leftover from a previous run can legitimately outrank. CI is always fresh, so all four were green and would have stayed green while being wrong. They now stamp ids per run and assert RELATIVE order, and the suite was run three times over the same database to prove it. The rule that keeps catching this codebase out is not "clean up after yourself" — it is **assert only what your own fixture establishes**.
299. **The conversation panel gates ITSELF, because the deal card is not a sales-only screen.** Owner: "bitim va crm bo'limida telefon raqamli kartochkalar bor u yerda ham tursin chat." A deal card is open to `ved.docs` as well as sales (`DEAL_WRITE_PERMISSIONS`), so an ungated panel dropped onto it would have quietly handed the customs manager every private sales conversation in the company — a widening nobody would have noticed, because the people who could see it are the people who would not think it odd. The check therefore travels WITH the component rather than living in whichever page remembers to add it: a component meant for any card has to be safe on any card. The same deal card now shows different things to different people, deliberately, and that is what the e2e pins.
300. **A lead's conversation is resolved, never guessed.** `tg_messages.client_id` is NOT NULL, so a brand-new prospect has no thread and the panel does not render — correct, since their chat was never imported. Two cases do resolve: a converted lead (`clientId` set), and an EXISTING client typed into the funnel as a fresh lead, which is how most of this funnel fills up. The second is matched by phone through the same helper the cabinet uses, and ONLY when the number resolves to exactly one client. One phone with several codes is normal here (777, 555, 444 in one pair of hands); on a client card that is fine because you opened a specific code, but on a lead it is a guess, and a wrong guess opens somebody's private conversation. Silence beats a plausible mismatch.
301. **A test that skips itself is worse than one that fails.** The deal-card e2e opened `/bitimlar`, found nothing and called `test.skip` — the board defaults to MINE and the deal an earlier spec leaves belongs to somebody else. It reported green for two full runs while asserting nothing. It now opens `?scope=all` and asserts the card is visible before clicking, so the absence of a deal is a failure rather than a shrug.
302. **A chat is read in the order it happened, and it opens at the newest message — including on the card.** The owner reported both halves in one sentence ("mijoz kartochkasidagi chat teskari bo'lib qolgan … chatlar ro'yxatidan chatni tanlab ko'rsang focus bugunga qaratilmagan"), and both are the same fix, which supersedes the last clause of #296: the card panel's newest-first order was a theory about panels being references rather than conversations, and nobody reads a chat backwards. `conversationFor` returns rows NEWEST first and both screens render them inside a `flex-col-reverse` scroll box. That gives reading order on screen AND a first painted frame that is already at the bottom — no scroll-after-paint, so no jump, and no JS at all. Taking the newest `limit` rows rather than the oldest is the other half: the recent end is the only part anyone reads. The thread screen's height is now spelled out from the shell it sits in (`h-14` header + `p-4` top + the `pb-28`/`md:pb-8` that clears the tab bar) instead of guessed — the first cut was one nav bar too tall, so the newest message sat UNDER the tab bar, which looks exactly like the bug it was meant to fix.
303. **A colour class the theme has no token for is silent everywhere, and four had shipped.** `bg-surface-200` was written on both screens that show a conversation; the surface scale has never had numbers (DEFAULT / raised / sunken), so Tailwind emitted nothing and every message a CLIENT sent rendered with no bubble while ours had one — the screen read as "our messages are highlighted", not as a bug. It does not fail typecheck, lint, build or the browser console; the page just looks nearly right, which is why it survives review. `tests/unit/tokens.test.ts` now walks `src/` and fails on any colour class the config cannot produce, naming the file. It found two more on its first run: `bg-surface-100` in the public layout (since `/driver` shipped) and `stroke-brand-400` on the tracking map — the brand scale is 50/100/200/500/600/700/800, so a truck's planned route was a polyline with `fill="none"` and no stroke, i.e. drawn and invisible. The palette is deliberately small (#180) so a wrong shade is a mistake rather than a choice; this is what makes that enforceable. Related to the Tailwind footgun already in CLAUDE.md but the opposite direction: there the class is built at runtime and cannot be seen, here it is perfectly visible and means nothing.
304. **The bubble is one component now, because it was wrong twice in the same way.** Both copies asked for the same colour that does not exist, and both would have had to be fixed twice. `TelegramBubble` takes its labels as props so it stays a plain presentational component with no `getTranslations` of its own, which is what lets the card panel (a server component that does its own permission check) and the full thread screen share it.
305. **Live receiving stores a session, and that is the whole of what changes.** Phase 3. The privacy rule is not relaxed by going live: `decideIncoming` calls the SAME `classifyDialog` the import calls, so a message is kept only when the sender's number is already in the client book, and a refusal carries no id, no name and no number — the listener may count what it passed over, it may not be able to name it. What genuinely changes is that the server now holds a credential to a personal Telegram account, where phase 1 deliberately held none (#292). Everything on this page follows from that one difference.
306. **The first reversible secret in the system, so it is encrypted rather than hashed.** Login passwords, browser sessions and driver pairing tokens are all one-way, because nothing ever needs the original back; a Telegram session string IS the credential and must be handed to the library on every reconnect. AES-256-GCM, authenticated so a row edited in the database fails to open instead of decrypting to something. The key is `TG_SESSION_KEY` and is deliberately NOT derived from `SESSION_SECRET`: rotating the web session secret must not lock twelve people out of their own Telegram, and leaking one must not hand over the other. The owning user id is bound in as additional authenticated data, so a blob lifted from one manager's row into another's will not open — without it, write access to one table would be read access to somebody else's private conversations. `sessionKey()` checks the DECODED length, because node would accept sixteen bytes and quietly give AES-128.
307. **One listener per account, held by a RESERVED postgres connection.** Two live connections on one personal Telegram account is the thing that gets it limited, and "did I leave one running on the other machine" is not answerable at the time. An advisory lock dies with its connection — including when the process dies badly, which a "who is running" table would not survive. It is taken BEFORE connecting to Telegram, since connecting first would already have done the thing the lock exists to prevent. It reserves a connection rather than borrowing from the pool, because a pooled connection can be closed underneath and would take the lock with it silently. The integration test then found the other half: `release()` returns a connection to the pool WITHOUT closing it, so the lock rode along on an idle connection and the account stayed locked after a clean shutdown. Releasing now unlocks first.
308. **A row in `tg_accounts` is configuration; a heartbeat is a connection.** The failure this design is aimed at is silent: the bridge is a process on a server, and when it stops nothing errors, no page breaks, and the conversation list simply stops growing — the first anyone knows is a client asking why nobody answered. So the status is a heartbeat written every 30 s, the screen calls anything inside 90 s live, and `signed_out`/`stopped` outrank the clock so a session Telegram itself ended does not read as "stale" and send somebody to restart a process that cannot start. `keyOpens` is separate again: a rotated `TG_SESSION_KEY` and a dead session both look like "not connected" and have completely different answers. `accountStatuses` never returns session material, and it survives a missing key on purpose — the status screen is where somebody would find out the key is missing, so it must not be the screen that crashes because of it.
309. **The client book is cached, and a stranger is what invalidates it.** A listener runs for days and the book changes underneath it — new clients are the business. Re-reading per message is a query per message for data that changes a few times a day; never re-reading means a client given a code this morning stays a stranger until somebody restarts the process. So: a ten-minute refresh, PLUS a forced reload the first time an unknown number writes, rate-limited to one a minute. The trigger is exactly the event that suspects staleness, and the cooldown is what stops an evening of family messages becoming a query loop.
310. **The bridge status renders one of five strings chosen at RUNTIME, so the key is a map and not a template.** `t(\`bridge_${state}\`)` is invisible to every check there is: a missing key throws while rendering, in every locale, and comparing bundles to each other cannot see a string that is absent from all four (#163). `BRIDGE_LABELS` is the source of truth outside the bundles and `locales.test.ts` reads it — the same shape as the settings-descriptions anchor, for the same reason. The string most likely to be missing is the one that only appears when something has already gone wrong.
311. **A person's answer beats the automatic rule, in both directions.** Owner: "shunda feature qo'sh — qaysi chatlarni qo'shish kerak va qaysini qo'shmaslik." The phone match stays and still does most of the work; what it cannot know is that Telegram reveals a number only to CONTACTS, so a real client the manager never saved is invisible to it — 122 of those in the first import, and "go and save 122 contacts" was the only fix on offer. Nor can it know that a number IN the book belongs to a chat nobody wants kept. `classifyWithRules` checks the written rule FIRST, before even `isPrivate`/`isBot`: those are guesses about what a peer is, and a person who has looked at the chat knows better. `pending` decides nothing — a question a scan wrote must never quietly become a yes. One function, consulted by both the import and the live listener, so a decision cannot apply to one path and not the other.
312. **The scan writes down a name, never a message.** `pnpm tg-scan` is run BY the manager against their OWN account, and that is what makes it acceptable at all: it records the display name, the id and the visible number of every chat the automatic rule did not take — which for most managers is mostly their family and their friends — so that they can be ruled out. There is no code path in it that opens a conversation. Groups, channels and bots are never offered, and that is a limit rather than an oversight: a group holds people who never agreed to anything, and one manager does not speak for them. A re-scan refreshes labels (a person renames themselves, and deciding from a stale name is deciding about the wrong person) and never touches `decision`.
313. **Answering is gated TIGHTER than reading, and scoped to your own chats.** `/suhbatlar` opens to `crm.leads` OR `clients.manage`; `/suhbatlar/qaysi` demands `clients.manage`. Reading a client's thread is the sales job; deciding what the company starts KEEPING about a person is not, and the sales manager e2e pins exactly that difference. On top of it, a manager sees only rows from their own account — only `admin.settings.manage` (the owner) sees everybody's, because somebody has to be able to finish the job. The server action re-checks the same scoping rather than trusting the screen: a screen is a view, an action is a door, and a manager who can see only their own list must not be able to answer somebody else's by posting an id.
314. **`include` demands a client, in the schema and not only in the action.** `tg_messages.client_id` is NOT NULL, so a rule that says "keep this chat" without naming a client would promise a message a home it does not have — and it would fail much later, at insert time, inside a listener. `CHECK (decision <> 'include' OR client_id IS NOT NULL)` makes it unrepresentable. Undo clears the client with the decision, so a reverted row cannot keep pointing at somebody. Every answer is reversible in one press on purpose: it is a judgement made from a display name, and being wrong is ordinary.
315. **An outgoing private message has no SENDER, and reading the sender dropped every reply we sent.** Found while designing phase 4, in code shipped an hour earlier. GramJS sets `senderId` only when `post || (!out && peerId instanceof PeerUser)` (`tl/custom/message.js:56`), so a message the manager SENDS, in a private chat, with no `fromId`, has none at all. The listener asked `getSender()`, got null, concluded "not a private chat", and passed it over. The symptom is the worst kind available: the bridge says connected, the client's messages keep arriving, and only OUR half of every conversation is quietly missing — noticed weeks later by somebody reading a thread and wondering what we answered. The peer now comes from the CHAT (`peerFromChat`), which is the other party on an incoming message and the recipient on an outgoing one — the same person either way, and the same thing the import iterates, so both paths reduce a conversation identically. `telegram-peer.test.ts` pins it against the real library by constructing `Api.Message` objects: the one test here that imports `telegram`, and it earns it, because no hand-written fake could have shown this.
316. **Phase 4 sends, so the safety argument had to be rebuilt out of rules.** Phases 1-3 rested on "there is no code path here that sends", and that sentence was the whole protection — sending is what gets a personal account limited, and these are twelve people's own accounts. `crm/telegram-send.ts` now holds every rule, pure and tested, with the shell obeying: **never message somebody who has not written to us first** (unsolicited outbound is what "report spam" exists for, and it is also the only thing this business never needs), a company-wide switch defaulting to OFF so deploying the code cannot by itself make an account start sending, 12/minute · 200/day · 4/chat/minute, and FLOOD_WAIT obeyed with a second of slack and capped at an hour. The caps are set where a HUMAN cannot reach them but a bug can — a loop that re-queues, a retry that never gives up — because their purpose is to make a software fault cost one account nothing.
317. **The web app never sends; it writes a row.** Exactly one process may hold a connection to an account (#307), and the Next.js server is not it. So a reply is a row in `tg_outbox` and the listener drains it, one message per three-second tick and never a batch: a queue that empties as fast as it can is precisely the burst that gets an account flagged. The consequence is that a QUEUED ROW IS NOT A DELIVERED MESSAGE, and the screens must never blur that — the thread draws a queued reply dashed and unsent, and `canQueue` refuses outright when the bridge is down rather than accepting something that might go tomorrow. A manager who saw their answer accepted believes the client has it; a client waiting on an answer nobody sent is the worst thing this feature can produce.
318. **Claiming a job is one statement, and a message in flight is never guessed at.** `UPDATE … WHERE id = (SELECT … FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED) RETURNING` — a read followed by a write is two statements with a gap, and the gap is where the same message goes out twice. (`FOR UPDATE` in a lone `SELECT` would do nothing here: postgres.js runs it in its own transaction and releases the lock immediately, which is the same trap as #307's `release()`.) A row left in `sending` means the listener died mid-send, and nobody can tell from here whether Telegram received it: re-queueing may double it, dropping it loses a reply. Startup marks those FAILED with the reason spelled out, which puts it in front of the one person who can open their own Telegram and look.
319. **You may only reply through your OWN account, and the owner is not exempt.** The message leaves a manager's personal Telegram and arrives under that manager's name and photograph, so replying "as" a colleague is not a permission question — it is impersonating them to a customer. `replyAccountFor` returns an account only when the actor is the manager whose conversation it is. Reading somebody else's thread stays allowed (that is what the CRM is for); speaking in it does not. The screen says which, rather than showing a disabled box: "why can't I type" is a question somebody answers by restarting a server.
320. **`connectionRetries: -1` meant ZERO attempts, so the listener never connected at all.** Found by an adversarial pass over phases 1-4 and confirmed in the library: GramJS uses the value as a LOOP BOUND — `for (attempt = 0; attempt < this._retries; attempt++)` (`network/MTProtoSender.js:149`, fed from `telegramBaseClient.js:314`) — so `-1` runs the body no times. `connect()` then reports failure by RETURNING FALSE rather than throwing, and the code ignored the result, registered its handlers and started writing heartbeats for a socket that was never opened. The bridge would have said "connected" for ever while receiving nothing. `Infinity` is both the correct value and the library's own default, and the result of `connect()` is now checked. Pinned by a test that reads the source, because the whole failure lives in a number.
321. **`status` was a one-way street, and it made the whole of phase 4 unusable after one restart.** A graceful stop wrote `stopped`; nothing wrote `active` again except a fresh `pnpm tg-login`. So the first ordinary restart latched the bridge as stopped for ever — and because queueing a reply refuses when the bridge is not live (#317), replying silently became impossible until somebody logged in again. `markAccountActive` runs at startup. The neighbouring half: the heartbeat was written unconditionally on a timer, which proves the PROCESS is alive rather than the LINK — a listener sitting on a dead socket read as connected. It now beats only while `client.connected` is true.
322. **Saved Messages would have been filed as a customer's conversation.** A manager's chat with themselves is a private chat, is not a bot, and carries their OWN phone number — and an owner's number is usually somewhere in the client book. Their private notes would have been stored against that client and shown to everyone who can read them. Refused before the book is consulted, and refused ahead of an explicit rule too: nobody should be able to opt into it by pressing a button on a list.
323. **`tg_phone` had no UNIQUE, and that is the identity the design is actually about.** 0035 enforced one login per PERSON (`manager_user_id`), but the invariant is one listener per TELEGRAM ACCOUNT — a second connection is what gets an account limited. Two rows for one number would mean two account ids, two advisory locks (the lock is taken per ROW, so it cannot help), and two listeners on one account. Migration 0038 adds it.
324. **One phone with several client codes filed under whichever row postgres returned first.** `classifyDialog` takes the first match and `clientBook()` had no `ORDER BY`, so the same conversation could land under 777 today and 444 tomorrow. It now orders by client code: the answer may still be the wrong one of three, but a STABLE wrong answer is one a person can correct with a rule, and an unstable one is not. This is the owner's documented reality (777/555/444 in one pair of hands), not an edge case.
325. **The compose box belonged on every card, not only on «Suhbatlar».** Owner: "crm kartochkalarda, bitim kartochkalarda va chatda sms jo'natish ko'rinmayapti." It was on the thread screen and nowhere else — my omission, and he is right that it is the wrong place to stop: somebody reading a client's chat on the client card wants to answer it there, not navigate away first. `TelegramReply` is now one server component doing all the gating, used by the thread screen and by the card panel, so the two can never drift apart on who may speak. It gates ITSELF for the same reason the panel does (#299). The queued-but-unsent replies are shown on the card too — a manager who answered from that panel must see the answer still waiting, or the panel looks exactly as it did before they typed.
326. **When a reply cannot go, the screen says which reason.** Not a disabled box, not silence. There are seven distinct ways a reply can be refused — the company switch is off, the client never wrote, the bridge is down, three rate limits, the conversation is on somebody else's account — and they have completely different answers, from "turn on a setting" to "ask Siroj". Silence sends somebody to restart a server; a disabled box with no label does the same thing more slowly. The e2e asserts the SENTENCE is there on an unconfigured system, not merely that the box is absent.
327. **Nothing caught up after a disconnect, so every deploy lost messages silently.** The comment claimed "Telegram replays a little history on reconnect" and nothing implemented it: GramJS's `catchUp()` is an empty stub (`client/updates.js:65` — literally `{ // TODO }`) and the package never calls `updates.GetDifference`, so a reconnect resumes from wherever the new session starts. `docker compose up -d --build` takes the listener down for a minute or two, and this company deploys — a client writing in that window was delivered to nobody, ever, and the conversation screen would not even mark them as waiting. The listener now resumes each chat from the highest message id already stored for it (`resumePoints`, one grouped query), through the SAME decide-and-store path a live message takes; the unique index is what makes running it on every start free. Bounded per chat, because a restart must not become a full re-import, and never fatal — a bridge that refuses to start because it could not backfill is worse than one that starts a little behind.
328. **The exclude half of «Qaysi chatlar» was unreachable, and the reason was the scan.** `scanVerdict` returns `'auto'` for anything the automatic rule already keeps, the scan records only `'ask'` verdicts, and `recordCandidates` was the sole writer of `tg_chat_rules` — so no route existed by which a MATCHED chat could ever be given an exclude. Half the owner's feature ("qaysini qo'shmaslik") shipped as an ornament. It is now reachable from the conversation itself, which is the better place anyway: you are looking at the chat you are deciding about instead of judging a name on a list. Future-only and said so before the press — deleting a client's conversation is a separate decision with its own consequences, and this button must not quietly be the one that takes it.
329. **The most important alarm in the system reached the owner's phone as the word "BackupFailed".** Found by a survey for the photo backup, in code that has been live for weeks. `renderTelegramText` has a case for `RestoreTestFailed` and none for `BackupFailed`, so it fell to the default — `${type}\n${appUrl}` — and `payload.error`, the one thing that says WHAT broke, was dropped on the floor. These are the messages that only ever arrive on a bad day, which is precisely why nobody noticed they were useless. The locale test now walks EVERY alarm in every language and asserts it is not the bare event name and does carry the reason; it was shown to fail without the case. The general lesson: a default branch that degrades gracefully is right for a notification nobody has written yet and wrong for an alarm, because the alarm is never exercised in normal use.
330. **The photo backup groundwork, and what the design review changed.** Three independent designs were scored and none won outright (23/40 each), so the parts taken are the ones all three agreed on plus the corrections the judges made. Already built: `uploadBuffer` (photos come out of MinIO as a Buffer, and writing them to a temp file first would put a disk that can run out between the object store and the backup) and `driveQuota` reading `about?fields=storageQuota`. The quota read is the load-bearing one: `usage` is the WHOLE Google account — the owner's Gmail and his own photographs share the same 15 GB — so a sync that measured only its own folder would happily consume the last gigabyte the DATABASE dump needs, turning a nice-to-have into a catastrophe. Corrections the review produced, to apply when the sync is built: photographs go in their OWN Drive folder (the nightly prune pages the dump folder, and mixing thousands of photos into it makes that walk the lot); `md5Checksum` belongs in the `fields` list, because the bytes are already in memory and a hash comparison costs nothing over the size-only check; and a restore CANNOT use `WHERE thumb_200_key IS NULL` to find work, because restored rows have those columns populated while the MinIO objects are gone — the obvious predicate finds nothing and every image stays broken.
331. **The «lenta»: one client, everything that happened, in one column.** Owner: "amocrm bitrixlardek katta polyada ketma-ketlikda ko'rinib tursa yaxshi edi … chatga o'xshab qachon nima bo'lgani 1 joyda ko'rinar edi." He is describing the screen every CRM is actually used through, and the insight in it is worth stating: a Telegram message, a consignment arriving, a payment landing and a note somebody left are the same KIND of thing — something that happened, at a time, done by a person — and once they are drawn that way, "what is going on with this client" has one answer instead of five panels to hunt through. It also dissolves his other complaint rather than answering it ("crm bitimlarda chat yo'q"): the conversation panel rendered nothing for a client who had never written, while a timeline always has content, because cargo and money happen whether or not anybody writes.
332. **Five sources, chosen because each carries `client_id` directly and each is already indexed.** `tg_messages`, `tg_outbox`, `crm_activities`, `receipts`, `client_transactions` — one `UNION ALL` of narrow selects, sorted once, with the LIMIT applied to the whole thing and paging by a timestamp that needs no source to know about the others. Deliberately NOT `box_movements`: 10,588 rows carrying `ref_type`/`ref_id` with no foreign key, reachable from a client only through boxes → lots → receipts, and every box of every consignment would be a row. That is not a timeline, it is a log. Cargo enters the feed at the moment a person cares about, which is the receipt. A voided charge stays on the timeline dimmed and labelled, because it happened and then somebody undid it, and both are part of the story.
333. **Two composers, not one control with a mode switch.** amoCRM puts "message the client" and "internal note" behind one box and a toggle, and the accident that follows is somebody typing a private remark about a client and sending it TO the client. Two boxes, each labelled — one says «mijoz KO'RMAYDI» in the placeholder itself — cannot make that mistake. The note goes to `crm_activities`, the table notes already lived in, so a note left on the old contact-history panel and one left on the timeline are the same note rather than two half-histories.
334. **The deal-card gate test kept passing while proving nothing.** Swapping `TelegramThread` for `ClientFeed` left the spec asserting on a `data-testid` the page no longer rendered: the owner's count was zero, VED's count was zero, and the assertion that VED must not see what the owner sees was satisfied by both seeing nothing. Same family as #301, and the same lesson from the other end — there the test skipped itself, here it compared two absences. It now asserts on `client-feed` AND that the owner's count is greater than zero, so the relation is proved against something that exists.
335. **The timeline was missing the middle of the journey, and for a cargo company that IS the story.** The first cut had the cargo ARRIVING and then nothing until the money — a survey of every event source, with the volumes measured on the owner's own data, showed what was absent: the truck leaving, the arrival in Uzbekistan, the handover, the crate, a lost box, a cancelled batch. "Qachon nima bo'lgani" for this business means keldi → yashik → yo'lga chiqdi → yetib keldi → topshirildi, and a feed that skips four of those five is a feed about invoices.
336. **`box_movements` belongs in the feed GROUPED, not excluded.** I had ruled it out as "a log, not a timeline", and that was the right instinct with the wrong conclusion. It is one row per BOX — 10,920 rows for 4,401 consignments — so raw it would be a line per carton. Grouped by the batch (`ref_id`) it collapses 2,082 departure rows to 1,198: one line per truck per client, which is exactly what a person remembers. The three-hop path (receipts → lots → boxes → movements) has no index of its own but walks three that exist, and measures 4.3 ms warm on the busiest client. The trap inside it: `marked_lost` and `inventory_missing` are written with `ref_id` NULL, so grouping THOSE by `ref_id` would collapse every box a client ever lost into one row dated whenever the last one happened — they group by day instead.
337. **Read the fact, do not infer it.** Three places where the obvious shortcut is wrong: whether an arrival means "ready to collect" is decided per warehouse by `unload.ts`, so the feed reads `to_status` rather than assuming; `undocumented_transfer` counts as an arrival, because the box IS physically there and hiding it would leave the client's cargo invisible at the destination; and a handover carries `debt_ok`, meaning a manager overrode the debt gate to release the cargo — precisely the kind of thing the owner wants to find in one place rather than in an audit log.
338. **The bridge was started with `docker compose run`, which has no restart policy.** The owner's `pnpm tg-doctor` came back with everything green except the account, which read «hech qachon» — never a single heartbeat. A `run` container is one-shot: it does not come back after a reboot, after an OOM, or after the process falls over, and nothing on any screen breaks when it goes. It matters more than a normal daemon would, because a listener that is down loses every message sent while it is away — it catches up on start (#327), but only if it starts. `tg-listen` is now a compose SERVICE behind a `telegram` profile with `restart: unless-stopped`, taking its number from `TG_LISTEN_PHONE`. One account per container, because two listeners on one Telegram account is the thing the advisory lock exists to refuse. The script backs off thirty seconds before exiting on a terminal failure, since `unless-stopped` would otherwise restart a signed-out session instantly and for ever — those conditions need a person, not a retry.
339. **The lenta died exactly where the CRM needs it most: on a lead that is not a client yet.** `ClientFeed` returned null when the client was unresolved — and a lead usually IS unresolved, that being what makes it a lead. So the CRM card had no timeline and no internal chat for most leads, and the owner reported both as never built ("qo'shilmadi"), which from where he sat was true: a panel that renders nothing did not ship in any sense that matters. The feed now takes an optional `leadId` and the lead's own activities join it; when `clientId` is null every client-keyed branch compares against NULL and yields nothing — the correct answer, not a special case. The note composer writes to the lead until a client exists. The deeper lesson repeats #183 from a new angle: a component that self-hides on missing data must be audited on the screen where that data is USUALLY missing, because that is the screen where "it gates itself" quietly becomes "it does not exist".
340. **The funnel e2e caught the duplication the swap created, by strict mode.** With lead activities now on the lenta, the old contact-history list on the lead card showed every note twice — and Playwright's strict `getByText` refused to pick one, which is the locator doing exactly what it is for. The LIST is gone; the activity FORM stays, because its kind and «keyingi qadam» date are what feed `/crm/today` and the lenta's quick note box has neither. One card, one history — "qachon nima bo'lgani 1 joyda" must not quietly turn back into two places. Activity kinds keep their icons on the feed row (a call is 📞, not 📝).
341. **The internal chat lives in Telegram; the record lives on the card.** Owner: "ichki chatni telegramda olib borishni belgila — CRM va BITIM uchun chatlar bo'lishi kerak ichki hodimlar bilan, va tasklarni telegramdan jo'natadigan qil, task linklari bilan." The split is the design: `crm_activities` stays the single record (a note on the lenta is the note), and writing one pings the right colleagues in Telegram with a deep link back to the card, so the conversation happens where everybody already is and the trace stays where the client's history is. This deliberately does NOT build an in-app messenger — that stays cut — and it is the answer to the standing question of what "ichki hodimlar bilan chat" means here.
342. **WHO gets pinged is the thread-participants model, not a role broadcast.** Recipients of a note are the record's OWNER (the lead's or deal's carrier) plus everyone who has already written on that card, minus the author. A message to "all of sales" about every note on every card is a channel people mute in a week, and then the one note that mattered is muted with it; you join a card's conversation by speaking in it, which is how threads work everywhere else. The author is never pinged about their own note — that is how a whole team learns to mute the type.
343. **The deal chat is per JOB, not per client.** `crm_activities` gained `'deal'` (migration 0039, a CHECK widened — purely additive) and the deal card's note box writes to the DEAL. Two deals with one client are two conversations, and a price argument about one must not surface on the other — asserted by a feed test in both directions: the deal note shows on its own card merged with the client history, and does not leak onto the plain client card.
344. **Tasks reach their assignee's Telegram the moment they exist, with the link.** Create, reassign and complete all notify — assignee on the first two, the CREATOR on completion, with the result text, because "bajarildi" with no result is a message that starts a phone call. Never to yourself. The links come from one `CARD_PATHS` map (`notifications/links.ts`): routing knowledge in one place, `/bugun` as the fallback for a task about nothing in particular, and the trailing-slash join covered by a test because `APP_URL` is typed by a person. All three are pre-rendered text (#164) and mute-grouped with the task digest — someone silencing "tasks" means all of it.
345. **The per-role layout the owner asked for mostly existed; what it lacked was the messaging.** `PRIMARY_BY_ROLE` gives each job its own four thumb-tabs and `MENU_BY_ROLE` its own curated menu (#194) — receiving first for the warehouse, the funnel for sales, the money for the accountant. The genuinely missing piece of "ishlashga oson, tez va qulay" was that nothing PUSHED: work assigned or discussed still waited to be discovered. With tasks and notes arriving in Telegram carrying links, the app's screens are where you act and Telegram is where you find out — which is the shape his team already lives in.
346. **The skladchi's home screen is the day, drawn in the order the day happens.** Owner: "skladchi ekranidan boshla" — the first role of the per-role layout pass. Receiving keeps the big coloured button (it is 90 % of the work), and under it the remaining steps as rows in work order — what is coming, loading, handover — each carrying the live number that says whether it needs attention NOW: trucks on the road here plus open promises (with the late ones flagged), batches forming or loading at this origin, boxes a client could collect. A row that reads «Yuklash · 2» is an instruction; a bare tile is a door. The screen is keyed on `actor.warehouseScoped` — the same fact the data scope reads — so an invented warehouse role gets it exactly when it gets warehouse-scoped data, and the counts obey the three-answer scope rule (`rbac/scope.ts`): scoped with no warehouse means zeros, never the whole company. Every count is one indexed slice (`batches_origin_status_idx`, `boxes_wh_status_idx`; `expected_arrivals` stays small by nature) because this is the most-opened page in the app. The zero badge is deliberately absent — a `0` pill on every quiet row teaches the eye to skip the pills — and the four workflow steps leave the tile grid rather than appearing twice. The e2e proves the number MOVES: a promise recorded for the operator's warehouse raises his arrivals count by one, cancelling it takes the count back down, and the owner — not warehouse-scoped — keeps the tile home.
347. **Light cargo wore the danger colour, and the fix is the owner's own scale.** The density badge's lightest band was dressed in the BRAND colour on three screens — and the brand is red, so the best cargo in a volume business looked like a warning ("yengil yuklar qizil belgilanib qolyabti"). His rule now, stated by him in numbers: green to 150 kg/m³, yellow to 250, light red to 450, dark red above. One `DENSITY_CLASS` map and one `DensityBadge` (`components/density-badge.tsx`) replace three hand-rolled colour ladders, the thresholds stay a SETTING, and migration 0040 moves a stored row off the old default only if it still IS the old default — an edited value is his and untouched.
348. **Administration is a hub, not a corridor.** «Boshqaruv» landed on the warehouse list with ten other sections in a strip that scrolls off a phone; a person who did not know a section existed never met it. `/admin` is now a page of permission-gated buttons — every section, one screen — and the tile points there. One door → walked through it; zero doors → home.
349. **The shell learns two small manners.** The desktop sidebar folds to an icon rail (per-browser, localStorage, `title` names the icons) because the funnel and the stock table want the width more than the menu does. And the phone's ••• sheet closes on route change: it lives in the LAYOUT, which survives navigation, so any navigation that was not the link's own onClick left it standing over the new page (owner's report; the admin tab strip was one such path).
350. **The scanner was not slow, it was half blind — and the server was innocent.** The owner suspected Germany; decoding never leaves the phone. The actual chain on the Yiwu iPhone: Safari has no BarcodeDetector, so the zxing fallback ran on a camera stream Safari defaults to 640×480, of which the guide crop leaves ~350 px for a 100 mm label at arm's length. The stream now asks for 1080p (`ideal`, degrades gracefully), the crop canvas grew to 640, and a torch button appears when the track's capabilities offer one — warehouse aisles in the evening are where scanning actually dies. Not testable in this container (no camera); verified by build + the unchanged HID path.
351. **Expense types get a door.** They were data since M6 — three seeded rows — but with no screen, so the owner had to ask whether they were hard-coded. `/admin/cost-types`: add and rename by NAME only (codes are our plumbing — 'crating' and 'unload' are matched by services, a new type is matched by nothing), hide instead of delete because history points at them.
352. **The two sales boards carry doors to each other.** Lead funnel and deal board are one story told twice ("bir-biriga chambarchas"); each header now links to the other, and a converted lead's card offers «Bitim ochish» — `/bitimlar/new` with the client pre-picked, because the moment a lead is won and coded, opening the job is the only next act.
353. **A promise learns kub and kilo, and «Qabul qilish» finishes the errand it starts.** (Owner, item 9.) `expected_arrivals` gains `weight_kg` / `volume_m3` (migration 0041, additive). The button on a promise row now opens `/receive?arrival=<id>`: the wizard starts from the promise — client chosen, count and totals in a 'mixed' lot the operator corrects against real boxes — and the tap DELIBERATELY beats any half-draft in localStorage, because the tap is the intent. The receipt carries the promise id, so the confirm closes EXACTLY that promise inside the same transaction; the old unambiguous-match heuristic stays for receipts that name none. Closing across warehouses is refused quietly — a stale link must not close another warehouse's promise, and a planning aid must never fail a receipt. When the numbers differ, the promise's AUTHOR is told in Telegram, outside the transaction: boxes on any difference (they are counted), kg/m³ above 5 % (they are estimates against a warehouse scale, and messaging every 2 % teaches the manager to mute the type — the #342 law again). Never to the receiver: they are looking at the boxes.
354. **The card takes the amoCRM shape the owner named, and the board stops scrolling the page.** (Owner, item 3: "kartani ichiga kirganda … chatlar, tasklar, notelar bir tarafda, mijoz infolar glavniyda; kanban column pastgacha davom etardi, pastidan scroll chiqib qolmasdi".) One `CardCols` component gives the deal, lead and client cards the same skeleton: the LENTA is the working surface and takes the width on the left, tall; the record's facts — compare panel, edit, lines, receipts, money, tasks, custom fields, history — live in a 24rem rail on the right that sticks and scrolls inside itself. DOM order is rail-then-main and the desktop grid swaps them with `order`, so the phone keeps its old facts-first stacking from the same markup, no window measuring. The kanban desktop board now owns the remaining viewport (`100dvh` minus the chrome above it): columns are full-height sunken lanes whose CARD LISTS scroll, so the page under the board never grows and the bottom scrollbar the owner kept hitting is gone; the pointer-events drag needed no change and its e2e passed untouched.
355. **The dock: chat and tasks ride on every page.** (Owner, items 5+7, restated mid-build: "chatni butun sistemadan kirsa bo'ladigan joyga qo'y".) One button in the app bar opens a right-side drawer (bottom sheet on a phone) with two tabs — the /suhbatlar list-and-thread with replies typed in place, and the /bugun list with one-tap finish. Everything loads WHEN the drawer opens, never on page load: the dock rides on every page, so its cost must be zero until reached for. A card that is about a client declares it with a hidden `data-dock-client` marker, and the drawer opens straight into that conversation — how a client component learns what a server page was about without a second fetch. The chat tab exists only behind the conversation gate; the warehouse gets a tasks-only dock. The drawer is a PORTAL to document.body: the app bar's backdrop-filter makes the header a containing block for fixed descendants, and the "full screen" overlay was pinned inside a 56-pixel strip until it escaped (caught by the dock's own e2e, "element is outside of the viewport").
356. **Notes take files, and the contact log folds into the rail.** (Owner: "Записать контакт lentaning pastida emas, yon tarafdagi menyuda tursin, collapsible bo'lib; zametkaga fayllar qo'shish".) The note box uploads against a client-generated activity id BEFORE the note exists — the receipt wizard's pre-binding pattern, now with 'crm_activity' in the upload route's allow-list — and the save claims that id, making the files the note's own. The lenta renders them: pictures in the lightbox, the rest as named downloads, via a per-note jsonb aggregate in the feed's activities branch. The lead card's ActivityForm moved into a folded Panel in the rail; its kind and «keyingi qadam» date still feed /crm/today, which is why it did not merge into the note box.
357. **The map answers where it was tapped.** (Owner, item 12.) The warehouse and truck detail cards moved from BELOW the map — where fullscreen buried them — to one overlay panel inside the canvas, shared by both renderers (Leaflet and the SVG schematic), fullscreen included: per-client cargo chips, the truck's live-fix/estimate line and progress, and the door to the full page. No new data plumbing: the popup's numbers were already computed by the map page and shipped to the client. The Leaflet count badge also stopped floating 15 px off its icon — it was positioned against the 72 px LABEL wrapper, not the 26 px icon.
358. **The cabinet links by the number Telegram itself vouches for.** (Owner, item 13, his own idea accepted with the security stated: a typed number would let anyone probe the client book, an SMS code needs an SMS contract — Telegram's `request_contact` needs neither, because Telegram only ever sends the SENDER'S verified number.) A client who opens the bot with no link is offered the share-number button; the contact handler's no-pending branch matches the phone against the client book with the same tested last-9-digits helper and the same forwarded-contact guard as the code flow, links EVERY code under the number, and answers "ask your manager" — naming nobody — on a miss. `client_telegram_links.created_by` becomes nullable (migration 0042): a self-service link honestly has no staff author, and the two staff-notification call sites no-op on null. The staff-minted link flow stays untouched as the fallback for a client whose Telegram number differs from the one in the book.
359. **The photograph itself, where the business already looks.** (Owner, item 15.) The listener downloads an incoming photo the moment its message row is NEW — the unique-index replay null is what makes a reconnect never re-download — and binds it as an attachment `entity_type='tg_message'`, so the thread, the card lenta and the dock all show the picture instead of «📎 media». Three refusals stated up front, in a pure tested plan function: photos only (hasMedia is also link previews, stickers, voice notes), size read from the MESSAGE OBJECT before any network I/O (10 MiB cap; unknown size refuses — pulling blind is how a 2 GB "photo as file" gets in), and thumbnails made INLINE with sharp because `enqueue()` starts the full pg-boss worker fleet — nightly backup included — in the calling container, which is #253-261 all over again (`saveAttachment` grew a `thumbnails:'skip'` option for exactly this caller). History backfill via tg-import and SENDING photos are deliberately not in this cut: both spend the personal account's rate budget, and the outbox is body-only by CHECK. Named to the owner: chat photos join the ~1.5 GB unbacked MinIO pool — the Drive photo sync is next; and `/api/attachments/[id]` is gated on login only, weaker than the thread's own gate, which is the already-queued attachment-authorization work.
360. **A voided cost leaves every report, not just some.** The revenue and opex sides of the P&L excluded their voided rows from day one; direct cargo costs never did, so every voided `cost_entries` row went on shrinking the profit for ever — in the monthly P&L, the cash flow's `cargoCosts` line, and `profitByBatch`. All four aggregates now filter `voided_at IS NULL`. `profitByClient` gets it belt-and-braces: voiding deletes the entry's allocations, but the update and the delete are two statements, not one transaction, so the join now also refuses an allocation whose entry is voided — a crash between the two must not be counted for ever. The tests assert DELTAS (add 111 → all three move by 111 → void → all three return exactly), because today's month is shared with whatever else lives in the database.
361. **A payment names the cash box it landed in — but only from now on.** `client_transactions.account_id` has existed since migration 0020 and `addTransaction` accepted it all along; the ledger form simply never offered it, so every client payment fell into the void while expenses dutifully named their account. The form now carries an optional account select on the payment side. Optional on purpose, twice over: thousands of historical rows have no account and backfilling them would be invention, and the cash-flow report already treats an unassigned payment as "received but not yet placed" — a true statement about both the past and a hurried operator. No migration.
362. **The handover act paginates; it never truncates.** The act drew on exactly one A4 page: rows past ~33 were silently discarded, and both sides signed an incomplete list — with no hint at all between 34 and 45 boxes, because the "… и ещё N" marker only fired past a separate 45-row cap. The renderer now adds pages the way the label renderer always has, keeping one 17pt row of headroom above the 120pt signature floor. The test pins page count (50 rows physically cannot fit on one page), and the fix was eyeballed for the subtle failure the page count alone cannot catch: rows must land ON the new page, which requires `line` to close over a mutable `page` binding, not a snapshot.
363. **Customs documents read what DEPARTED, not the live pointer.** The invoice, packing list and manifest selected boxes by `current_batch_id` — which unloading clears box by box, so the papers regenerated at the border or after arrival came out EMPTY at the one moment the export agent needed them. All three now go through `batchMemberFilter` (current pointer OR the `batch_departed` movement), the same predicate costing and the batch card have used since #121/#152. The test unloads a batch fully, confirms no live pointers remain, and regenerates both workbooks.
364. **A receipt is voidable only while every box is still on the shelf.** `voidReceipt` used to void whatever state the boxes were in: cargo on a departed truck became unscannable at unload, and cargo already HANDED to the client was un-happened on paper — while the movement history said otherwise. The box rows are now locked (`FOR UPDATE`) and one box past `in_stock` refuses the whole void, in the same transaction. Anything that left the shelf is resolved by the load/unload/issue machinery, not by void — the same line moveReceipt drew. The action catches `VoidError` rather than letting it become a 500.
365. **The wire shape of a ticked checkbox is `['off','on']`, and the test must send THAT.** The checkbox custom field posted a hidden `off` alongside the browser's `on`, and the parser read the FIRST value — so a ticked box saved "no", always. Parsing is now last-value-wins, and the hidden input obeys #171 (disabled when the field is read-only, so a locked "yes" is not overwritten by the sentinel). The old unit test fed a bare `'on'` — a shape the form never produces — and passed against the broken parser; it now feeds the real wire shapes, which is #166 all over again: a test that restates the code proves nothing, a test that speaks the protocol catches the bug.
366. **/pipeline is gated on the permission that already means "this screen".** The menu showed the sales pipeline only to sales, but the URL was open to any logged-in session — client-by-client cargo counts included. The page now requires `reports.own_clients`, the same grant the dashboard treats as the ticket to per-client reporting, so access stays editable data in Admin → Roles rather than a hard-coded role list.
367. **/api/health may only claim what it probed.** The endpoint answered `{db, storage, jobs}` while running a single `select 1` — MinIO down and the pg-boss fleet dead both reported "ok" for months, and the fleet DID die once (#252) with the health check smiling through it. Each word is now its own probe: the db select; a real `HeadBucket` with a 1.5 s timeout — deliberately NOT through `ensureBucket`, whose once-only latch would make every later ping a no-op and return the endpoint to lying; and jobs = the in-process latch (observed via `isBossStarted`, never by CALLING `startBoss` — a health check that boots the worker fleet lies the other way) plus `pgboss.version.maintained_on` younger than ten minutes, five missed maintenance cycles. Degraded answers 503 with the per-probe breakdown.
368. **Postgres is tuned in compose, not by migration and not by hand.** Production ran the stock image's compiled-in defaults — shared_buffers=128MB and a spinning-disk random_page_cost=4, the same planner bias behind the #152 class of scans. The tuning lives as `-c` flags in docker-compose.yml: a migration cannot do it (ALTER SYSTEM refuses to run in a transaction, and this repo's runner wraps all pending migrations in one), and hand-edits on the server are invisible config that dies with the volume. Command-line flags also carry the highest precedence, so the repo governs even over a stray postgresql.auto.conf. Values are sized for a ~4 GB VPS shared with the app and MinIO (512MB/1536MB/16MB/128MB, wal 2GB, rpc 1.1; halve the first four on a 2 GB box). A unit test pins the block — including that the command's first token is `postgres`, because compose `command` REPLACES the image CMD and omitting the binary keeps the whole stack down. Applies only on container RECREATE: `docker compose up -d`, off-hours, backup first.
369. **Attachment reads are authorized in log-only mode first.** GET /api/attachments/[id] authenticated and served — any staff session could fetch ANY file by uuid, including Telegram chat photos and CRM note files, strictly weaker than every screen that renders them (#274, #359 both said so). The mapping now exists (`wms/attachments/access.ts` — wms, not platform: it queries receipts/crates/handovers/crm_activities/tg_messages, and platform must never import wms) and MIRRORS the rendering screens: receipts are login+warehouse-scope with no permission code (the TNVED editor's traffic must not log), tg photos take the /suhbatlar gate, a deal note admits `ved.docs` (#299-301), custom-field groups follow their record's write list, the uploader always reads their own, and pre-allowlist entity types land in an `unmapped` branch that IS the inventory of them. But the route still serves: a would-deny writes one greppable `[attachment-authz]` line to stdout — not to the audit log, which is the immutable business record and would take ~30 rows per photo-heavy card view. The flip to a cabinet-style 404 comes only after the logs have been read on real traffic; a wrong mapping enforced blind would blank screens people use daily.
370. **The phone board swipes; the buttons stay.** The mobile funnel already showed one stage at a time with a chip strip and one-tap moves (built for the owner's earlier "mobileda kanban" ask) — what the amoCRM feel was missing was the THUMB: stages are now full-width snap columns in a horizontal carousel, each with the desktop board's sunken full-height shape (cards scroll inside the column, the page does not), and the chip strip is sticky under the app bar so the funnel map never scrolls away. The scroll position is the truth for the active chip — whichever column's centre is nearest owns it — with a short guard while a chip tap's own smooth scroll is in flight, or the tap and the thumb fight for the highlight. Touch drag-and-drop was considered and refused again: on a phone the same gesture IS scrolling, and the move-next button plus the stage sheet are one tap where a drag is a hold, a wobble and a miss. One file changed (`components/kanban.tsx`); both boards inherit it because the board was generic from the start. The e2e now asks the COLUMNS whether a card moved — every stage is in the DOM, so "gone from the board" stopped being an observable.
371. **One keyboard contract for every box that talks to a customer.** The dock sent on Enter and the thread composer did not — the same person, two behaviors — and on a phone there is no Shift+Enter, so Enter-send made a multi-line message impossible and fired half-typed sends at real clients. The rule is Telegram's own, in one helper (`components/composer.ts`): a physical keyboard sends on Enter, a finger gets a newline and only the button sends, decided by `(pointer: coarse)` through `useSyncExternalStore` (the server cannot know, so the server snapshot is the desktop rule for one frame nobody can type in). All three composers autogrow to their max-height instead of carrying a resize handle that does not exist on touch. The NOTE box keeps no-Enter-send on purpose — a note is multi-line prose. E2e caution recorded in the helper: the mobile Playwright project emulates touch, so specs must press the send button. `viewportFit: 'cover'` finally makes `pb-safe` real in the installed-PWA case, and the four focus-mode action bars (receive, issue, plan, crate) took `pb-safe` in the same change — cover without it would have sat them on the iPhone home indicator.
372. **A reply may carry ONE photo, and the queue owns the whole story.** Migration 0043, additive: `tg_outbox.attachment_id` plus the body CHECK relaxed to "text OR photo" — a photo with no caption is a real message, the same principle the import applies to incoming. The composer pre-binds the upload to a minted `tg_outbox` group id (the note-files pattern); `queueReply` refuses anything that is not the sender's OWN photo under 10 MiB (the incoming cap, so we never send what we would not accept) and a caption over Telegram's 1024 — refused, never truncated, and never split into a second send, because a second send is a second rate-limit slot. The listener sends via `sendFile`, and then writes the ECHO row itself and claims the photo onto it: Telegram's own echo becomes a no-op replay on the unique index, and `wasSentWithPhoto` stops the event handler from downloading back the bytes we just uploaded. Missing bytes at send time are a PERMANENT failure ("photo_missing") — retrying cannot conjure a deleted file. `deleteAttachment` maps the new FK to an 'in_use' refusal instead of a 500; a cancelled reply keeps its photo row (the audit-friendly orphan every pre-binding flow pays). One photo, no albums: media is the heavier, more spam-scored send, and every rule that protects twelve personal accounts — the switch, never-initiate, all three rate limits — holds unchanged, proven by the unit suite. The `tg_outbox` authz branch landed in the SAME commit as the upload allowlist entry, or every pending photo would have logged `unmapped` and 404ed on enforcement day.
373. **Every working role wakes up to its own day; the owner keeps the map.** Round 11 gave the skladchi a workflow home; sales, logist and accountant now get theirs, and WHO gets WHICH is one resolver (`wms/home/role-flows.ts` `buildHomeFlow`), ordered from the narrowest job to the broadest — the same rule the tab bar has always used (PRIMARY_BY_ROLE). Warehouse scope wins outright, because scope is a fact about the data, not the menu: a sales manager who is also a warehouse operator lives the warehouse day. super_admin/admin deliberately match nothing — a person who runs everything needs the overview, not somebody else's queue. Each home is a hero plus rows built from counts its own screens already pay for on every open (followUps, openLeadCount, managedClients, warehouseFlowCounts reused unscoped, moneySnapshot, costMissingBatches) plus three new indexed counts: plans awaiting a verdict, THIS month's cash-box-less payments (month-bounded on purpose — the years of pre-accounts NULLs are history, not work), and recurring templates not yet posted (mirroring generateRecurring's own idempotence predicate, voided postings NOT counting — proven by test). Every row's target passes that role's own page gate, not just the menu: the accountant's cost-missing row opens /dashboard because /batches would bounce him. The suppressed-tile list rides on the flow itself, so each home hides exactly the tiles it redraws.
374. **The batch card holds its shape but learns the card grid.** The daily actions were never the problem — open-loading, depart, unload all lived in the first card — the problem was the tail: eight equal-weight blocks in one narrow column on any screen size. The card now keeps its header block (code h1 FIRST — a spec reads `h1.first()`; status chip now wearing a literal per-status colour map; pair code; the stage actions untouched) above a `CardCols` grid: the working surface (contents, loaded boxes, costs — the widest table on the page) takes the wide column, the facts (VED docs, vehicle, driver phone, tracking pins, pricing, tasks, fields) sit in the sticky rail. The header must stay ABOVE the grid: CardCols renders its rail first on a phone, and the loading button must never sit under six folded panels. Zero testid, action or gating changes — every m3/m4/m5/m9/m9m assertion passed untouched, which is the proof this was a re-arrangement and not a rewrite.
375. **A mention is plain text plus a promise, and the promise is one message to the named person.** Phase 4 wanted @mentions in notes without inventing a markup format the composer, the feed, Telegram and the export would all have to agree on. The note STAYS plain text; the dropdown (`MentionTextarea`, offered on `@` + a prefix) inserts the colleague's canonical full name, and the server re-finds names in the saved text (`crm/mentions.ts` — longest-name-first span claiming, so «Aziz Karimov» is never half-matched by a colleague named «Aziz»; a word boundary on both sides, so an email address mentions nobody; two staff with the SAME name both get pinged, because the text genuinely names both). A hand-typed partial matches nobody — the contract is the dropdown's canonical form, and a fuzzy server guess would ping strangers. The named person receives `MentionedInNote` INSTEAD of the thread's `InternalNote` copy — one message, the personal one — and the contact-log path (which never broadcast) now pings exactly the mentioned. Everyone may mention everyone (the task-assignment precedent: «hamma hammaga»); self-mentions are dropped; delivery is Telegram-only, so a colleague who never linked the bot is named in the text but hears nothing — stated to the owner rather than half-built as an in-app bell. Both new types joined `MUTE_GROUPS` (the mention beside InternalNote), because the mutes tripwire reads `buildRecipients` itself and a type without a group is unmutable by construction.
376. **Permission to issue to a debtor becomes a record, and the gate re-checks everything at read time.** Phase 6, docs/DEALS.md's own criticism of the checkbox: «leaves no reason and no end date». `issue_approvals` (migration 0044, additive) carries who asked, who decided, why, until when — and the debt SNAPSHOT the decider actually saw, which is a CEILING: a debt that grew past it is a different debt, blocked again, because the person who approved $100 never saw $150. Validity is never a status field alone — the gate demands `approved AND unexpired AND amount-covered` in one locked SELECT (`FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED`, so two counters cannot spend one permission), and consumption is stamped AFTER the handover row exists because the consumed row carries an FK to it — two steps, one transaction, still atomic; the first draft did it in one step and the FK itself refused, which is the test doing its job. One live request per (client, warehouse) so deciders never split attention across duplicates; a decision is single-shot like a plan verdict; TTL is a setting (`debt_approval_ttl_hours`, 24) read at DECISION time, so changing it never rewrites permissions already granted. Deciders are `usersWithPermission('finance.debt_override')` resolved from the editable grants — data, not ROLE_MATRIX — and the direct debtOk checkbox STAYS for holders at the counter: a person allowed to decide should not petition themselves. Both halves land in the audit log — the verdict on the approval row, and the handover naming WHICH approval it spent. The /approvals screen and the issue-screen banner are integration-proven through the same functions the buttons call; the browser half deliberately has no e2e of its own this round — building a debtor with live boxes through the UI would cost more than the pixel it proves.
377. **The deal's money grows its two missing limbs: the discount is written, the profit is read.** The discount columns had sat in the schema since migration 0030 with nothing able to write them — the card displayed a figure no form could produce. Now `setDealDiscount` (DEALS.md answer 3): amount + mandatory reason + author + date, audited with before/after, amount 0 removes a mistaken discount and is audited too; controlled inputs in the form because React resets a form when its action returns, and "typed the amount, forgot the reason" must not also swallow the amount. Gated on the DEAL-WRITE list, not finance.manage — a discount changes what the job should cost, which is re-pricing's power; collecting less still goes through finance. The ledger is deliberately untouched: a charge already posted at the old figure is corrected by void + re-post, both already audited, and the charge form's prefill now subtracts the discount so the NEXT charge is born right. Profit (`dealProfit`, answer 7: per deal, never per line) = non-void deal charges − the deal's boxes' `cost_allocations` shares (batch costs arrive pre-split, exactly as `profitByClient` splits them; `costEntries.voidedAt` filtered belt-and-braces per #360 — proven red without it). Revenue is what was CHARGED, not quote-minus-discount: the discount already flowed into the lower charge, and subtracting it again would count it twice. The one number that CANNOT be computed honestly is batch-priced money on trucks that carried this deal's boxes — a batch invoice spans deals, and pro-rating it would put invented figures in front of an accountant — so it is shown as its own labelled line instead of being merged or hidden. Panel gated `finance.reports`, the accounting reports' own gate. Migration 0045: the `client_transactions.deal_id` partial index the batch side has had since Phase 2.4.
378. **The 50-goods file: parsed by detective work, grouped by the assistant, decided by a person.** DEALS.md answer 6, with the owner's go-ahead to use AI for the customs arithmetic («rastamojkani hisoblash guruxlash uchun AI ishlatsang boladi»).
