The files are the CLIENTS' — every supplier formats their own — so the header is FOUND, not assumed: pure functions (`deals/goods-import.ts`) scan the first ten rows for a goods column in ru/uz/zh/en, claim quantity/unit/volume/weight/money columns around it (volume before weight so «вес, кг» survives a stray m3; the line TOTAL beats the unit price when a file carries both), drop «Итого» arithmetic rows, and degrade to first-text-cell-per-row when no header exists at all. Upload is a route handler (the server-action 1 MB cap, #291), content-validated by actually opening the workbook, capped at 5 MB and 500 rows. Codes come memory-first (Phase 1.5's `tnved_assignments`) before the AI is asked; `proposeGoodsGrouping` then proposes ~50 goods → ~N TNVED positions with per-group confidence, reasoning and an ESTIMATED duty rate — advisory, labelled «черновая подсказка», displayed with a total estimate when line amounts exist, and never persisted anywhere a customs document could inherit it. One bad code does not sink the proposal (blanked + demoted to low, the reviewer types the right one); a refusal or missing key lands in the same TnvedError the whole feature degrades through: the file still parses, every good becomes its own line, grouping is by hand — which is also exactly what the e2e proves, since neither this container nor CI holds a key, and the first live grouping should be watched on the server. NOTHING writes until the VED manager presses confirm; the confirmed draft rides the existing replace-all `saveLines`, and the lines form is now keyed by its content so a server-side replace actually shows.
379. **Phase 7: a rule is a row, the funnel is an event, and the engine cannot loop.** «Automation rules» had two words of agreed scope, so the v1 is the smallest thing that automates the owner's actual morning, amoCRM-shaped and stated to him as reversible: a rule = ONE trigger (a lead or deal ENTERING a chosen stage, or one of ten curated business events — the audit-trail-only types are deliberately not offered) → ONE action (open a task, or say something in Telegram). The funnel had to become audible first: neither moveLead nor moveDeal emitted anything, and the edit forms were second write paths — `LeadStageChanged`/`DealStageChanged` are now announced from EVERY path that changes a stage (move, edit, conversion), or a rule would fire only for the button that happened to be pressed. The engine runs inside the event worker per event, each rule fenced in its own try/catch — a broken rule must neither kill the notification fan-out nor leave the event unprocessed — and it structurally cannot cascade: the two actions emit no domain events, so there is nothing for a second rule to hear. A rule's task is authored BY the rule's author — the person who wrote «open a task» answers for the tasks it opens — and its Telegram message respects the recipient's mutes (`AutomationRule` in the operations group): the rule's author is not above your notification settings. The assignee is a strategy, not only a name: the record's responsible person (owner, falling back to the client's sales manager), the event's actor, or a fixed user — and a rule that resolves nobody logs and skips rather than inventing an assignee. Gated on `admin.settings.manage` (no new permission — the #170 seed-skip would leave a fresh code ungrantable on the owner's edited roles), stage ids deliberately un-FK'd (a rule may point at either funnel, and a deleted stage should orphan the rule, not survive because of it), and every rule shows its fire-count — a rule that never fires is a typo, and the counter is how the owner finds out. Deferred, stated: time triggers («stuck in stage N days»), conditions on the record, and {placeholders} in texts.
380. **The event worker drains; and a spec's leftovers are its own to clean.** Two things phase 7's tests flushed out. First: `processPendingEvents` handled 50 events per call — one call per minute — so any backlog deeper than a batch advanced at 50/minute and a burst meant an hour of silent notifications; it now drains in bounded batches (40×50 per run) until the backlog is empty. Second, the sharper lesson: `attachment-access` had been leaving a QUEUED `tg_outbox` row for the primary manager since round 13, and the outbox spec's `claimNext` — whose whole point is claiming the oldest queued job — would claim it as a real message. It never fired because vitest orders files by its duration cache, and the ordering happened to run outbox first every time; adding one new test file reshuffled the order and the latent leak surfaced as three failures in a file nobody had touched. The row is now deleted where it is made. The #154 rule («state a spec leaves behind is the next spec's input») was written about Playwright's LEXICAL order — the vitest half of the suite runs in an order that can change when any file is added, which makes leftover state strictly worse there: not a wrong assumption about order, but a bet that no future round will ever shuffle it.
381. **Phase 8: the owner's objects go through the same door as the shipped ones.** «Hech qanday biznes-obyekt hard-coded bo'lmasin» (#174) ends here: an object invented on /admin/entities is a `custom_entities` row (0047 adds `label`, `is_custom`, `write_permissions`), its records are `custom_records` (name + note + active), and its card at /o carries custom fields, tasks and history because those panels were generic from birth. The load-bearing decision is that `resolveEntity` is the ONE resolver — registry first (code, costs nothing), then the owner's active rows — and everything that used to consult the compiled registry (`saveField`, field actions, the fields/tasks panels, `createTask`, `aboutLabels`, automation's linkable check, attachment authorization) now asks it instead. The alternative — an `if (custom)` branch at each of those eight call sites — is how a second registry is born (#186's lesson, extended from arrays to rows). Codes are MINTED (`x_` + time36 + noise), never typed: a code is an FK target and a URL segment for the life of the database, and the reserved prefix means a future release can ship any code it likes without colliding with something the owner named in 2026. One wrinkle: `audit_log.entity_id` is uuid and an entity's key is text, so entity audit rows use uuidv5(code) under a fixed namespace — deterministic, so every rename/hide of the same object stays on one thread.
382. **Who may edit is four words, not a permission picker; and the seed must not kill what it never made.** The write gate on an invented object is a stored ANY-of list, offered as four choices (hamma / sotuv / moliya / adminlar) the way mute groups are offered instead of event types — and «hamma» is deliberately the empty list meaning any signed-in staff, because half the point of these reference lists is that everyone keeps them current. Reading is open to all staff by design; no new permission codes were minted (#170's seed-skip trap). Records deactivate and never delete — answers, tasks and audit rows hang off them. The near-miss worth the entry: `syncEntityRegistry` deactivates any `custom_entities` code that is not in ENTITY_SPECS, which is exactly what an owner-born row is — the nightly seed would have hidden his lists on every deploy. The WHERE now demands `is_custom = false`, and the integration test was run with the guard stripped to watch the seed do the killing before trusting the test at all (#166). Cut from v1, stated: lookup fields pointing AT an invented object, Telegram deep links to its records, a per-record chat.
383. **The read layer merged what the schema kept separate — one manager's Telegram was on every colleague's screen.** The owner's report (2026-07-29): chats imported from HIS account were visible from every staff login with the conversations permission. The agreement was always each-manager-their-own-account; `tg_messages.manager_user_id NOT NULL` said so («two managers are two conversations»), the reply path enforced it to the point of calling cross-account sending impersonation (#317-era rules) — and every READ ignored the column: `listConversations`, `conversationFor`, the lenta's two Telegram branches, the dock's both routes, the sales home's waiting-chats count, `pendingFor`, and the chat-photo authorization all answered for the whole company. Phase 2 had stated "they may read it (that is what the CRM is for)" — the owner has now overruled that model, and he is right that it was never what was agreed. The rule, one sentence: a Telegram conversation is PRIVATE to the manager whose account holds it; cargo, money and notes on the same lenta stay the company's shared record. `clientFeed` takes the viewer as a required second parameter — an option would eventually be forgotten by exactly one caller, which is how this bug was born. Nobody is exempt, including super_admin: the owner sees his own account's chats and no one else's, stated to him as reversible (a supervision view is one deliberate widening away, behind a decision, not an accident). The names of WHO holds a chat with a client (`conversationManagers`, the cards' honest Telegram status) deliberately stay visible — a colleague may know THAT Siroj talks to the client; they may not read WHAT was said. Proven per #166 by stripping each of the three guards — list/thread, lenta, photo authz — and watching the three new leak tests go red.
384. **The Telegram attachment branches stop logging and start refusing.** Round 13 shipped `decideAttachmentRead` LOG-ONLY everywhere, with the flip to 404 held for the owner's decision after reading real logs (#369) — the right caution for warehouse photos whose wrong mapping would blank daily screens. But the owner's instruction on chat privacy IS the decision for the two Telegram types: after the screens were scoped, a photo URL that still served would out-read the thread it came from. `AttachmentAccessDecision` gains `enforce`; only the `tg_message`/`tg_outbox` branches set it, the route 404s when it is set, and every other type keeps the WOULD-DENY log line and the served bytes until he flips the rest. The predicate itself narrowed from permission-only to permission AND own-account (outbox: the sending manager or whoever queued it), with the uploader short-circuit untouched — the person who put a file in the system always reads it back.
385. **The supervision view: the boss reads everything, and it is a ROLE, decided in one line.** Round 20 shipped chats strictly private per account with the widening stated as one deliberate step away; the owner took that step the next day («menga rahbar sifatida hamma yozishmalar korinsin, superadminga hamma account yozishmalari korinsin»). `tgViewerFor(actor)` is the single place the answer lives: `{ id, all }`, where `all` is true for the super_admin role — every Telegram read (list, thread, card panel, dock, queued replies, chat-photo authorization) takes the viewer object, so the rule cannot drift between screens. Deliberately the ROLE and not a permission code: #170's seed-skip trap makes a new permission a hazard, and granting an existing admin code would smuggle supervision to people nobody named. On the supervision list each row names the manager(s) whose account holds the thread — a company of merged threads with no names was exactly the «tushunarsiz» he complained about — and in the thread every outgoing bubble already carried its manager's name. Replying is unchanged: the boss reads everyone's chat and still answers only from his own account (impersonation was never on the table). Proven by stripping the `all` branch and watching the supervision assertions go red (#166).
386. **The chat left the lenta.** The owner, looking at the round-20 result: «lenta va chatlar alohida tursin, birbirini ichida tushunarsiz ko'rinishda turibti» — and he is right for a reason deeper than taste: after #383 the two halves have DIFFERENT audiences (the lenta is the company's shared record, the chat is one account's private conversation), so interleaving them meant one column whose lines answered to two different privacy rules. `clientFeed` dropped its two Telegram branches (and its viewer parameter with them — the lenta is shared again, simple); the conversation is its own panel, the long-dormant `TelegramThread`, now on the client, deal and lead cards under the lenta, self-gated and viewer-scoped like every other chat surface. The reply-to-client composer moved with it — a word to the CLIENT belongs in the chat, a word to COLLEAGUES (the note box, mentions and all) stays in the lenta. The feed tests now pin the absence: a client WITH a telegram row whose lenta must not show it.
387. **Connecting a Telegram account became a screen, and the listener became a supervisor.** The owner: «akkauntlarni sistemamizga ulashni osonlashtirishimiz kerak» — until now a new account meant the owner at a server terminal (`pnpm tg-login`) plus a compose edit plus a container start, per account. Now: «Suhbatlar → Telegram ulash», the manager's own phone → the code Telegram sends → the 2FA password when the account has one. The half-done login is a gramjs client held in THIS process's memory, per user, TTL ten minutes — never a database row, and a deploy mid-login costs one re-sent code. It is always the ACTOR'S OWN account (`beginTgLogin(actor.id, …)`): the CLI's `--user` flag, which could bind a session to the wrong person, has no web equivalent. The config check (API pair AND `TG_SESSION_KEY`) runs BEFORE Telegram is asked to send anything — the tg-login lesson about wasting a typed code, kept. The other half: `pnpm tg-listen` with no argument now supervises ALL stored accounts in one container — a scan a minute starts listeners for accounts it is not holding (per-account exclusivity still the advisory lock), `signed_out` accounts are skipped entirely (knocking on a dead session once a minute is how accounts get flagged), a failed start backs off five minutes. So a manager connects on the screen and receives within a minute, with nobody touching docker. Not tested against live Telegram from here (no network): the decisions — phone shape, error naming, TTL, config refusal — are pure and unit-tested, the browser proves the honest not-configured refusal, and the first real connect is watched in docker logs like every Telegram feature before it.
388. **The queued Telegram leftovers, cleared in one round.** Four items had waited since the phases that made them: (1) `tg_messages_manager_idx` (migration 0048) — round 20 turned every conversation read into WHERE manager_user_id = X and left it walking an index led by client_id; the new (manager_user_id, client_id, sent_at) index serves the scoped shape, the old one stays for the supervision view. (2) The PURGE `excludeChatForClient` refused to smuggle in (#its own comment: "a separate decision with its own consequences"): an excluded chat's stored rows and their photographs now delete on an explicit second press — «yozuvlarini o'chirish (n)» on the excluded row, confirm dialog, refused for anything not excluded, audited with the counts, storage objects removed best-effort AFTER the db rows (an orphaned MinIO object is waste; a row pointing at deleted bytes is a broken screen). `tg_outbox` deliberately stays: those are replies the company sent through its own screens — our record, not the excluded chat's. The refusal was red-proofed the hard way: the first cut of the test looked up an included rule and SKIPPED itself when none existed — proving nothing — so the rule it refuses on is now minted unconditionally in the test. (3) EDITS: the listener follows Telegram's EditedMessage into `applyEdit` — UPDATE-only by construction, so an edit in a chat we never kept cannot create a row, which is the privacy proof in one property; a client who corrects a price has changed the record the manager acts on. (4) `tg-import --media [n]`: photographs for kept history, planned by the pure `mediaBackfillPlan` — media rows lacking their attachment, newest first, capped per chat (default 50) — replay-safe because a row already holding its photo is skipped. Capped BECAUSE photos-to-Drive is still the owner's held decision and MinIO has no off-site copy: an uncapped backfill would be a storage incident wearing a feature's name.
389. **Warehouse scoping became a property of the ROLE — the deferred access item that was never actually blocked.** `isWarehouseScoped` consulted a compiled two-name array, so a role invented on /admin/roles was born UNSCOPED: hand it receiving permissions and its holder silently saw every warehouse in the company — the same silent-and-permissive failure class #199's scope helper exists to prevent, reintroduced by the role constructor. Migration 0049 adds `roles.warehouse_scoped`; `getActor` reads the COLUMN through the exported `loadUserRoles` (one testable home for "ANY scoped role scopes the user" — union for permissions, intersection for scope, unchanged). Two writers, deliberately split: the migration backfills a LIVE database's two shipped roles; the seed stamps the flag only when INSERTING a shipped role on a fresh database — after birth the screen owns it, because a seed that re-asserted the flag would betray an owner who deliberately unticked it (the grants_customised lesson applied to one boolean). The /admin/roles checkbox goes through `setRoleScoped` with the SAME own-role guardrail as grants: unticking scope on a role you hold widens your own reach over every warehouse, which is the self-promotion that editor already refuses. The compiled array stays as the seed's bootstrap list and the tripwire's anchor. Proven by stripping the column read from the loader and watching the invented-role test go red (#166).
390. **The heroes came off the home screens.** The owner, after living with them: «1 qatorda 1 button turgan narsalar o'xshamabti, qolgan buttonlar turaversin, iconkalarini tushunarli qil». Every full-width lone button goes — the brand-coloured FlowHero that led each role's flow (rounds 11/15), the tile home's first-action hero — and each becomes an ordinary row or tile among its peers, same testids, so no spec moved. The «Bugun» task strip stays: it is an alert that appears only when work is due, not a door that stands there all day. The icon pass fixed what made them «tushunarsiz» — DUPLICATES within one screen: receive and arrivals both wore the inbox tray (arrivals → clock, it is a waiting list), issue/approvals/deals were three handshakes (approvals → check), my-clients wore the whole client book's icon (→ single user), /o wore stock's boxes (→ list lines), pipeline wore dashboard's chart (→ report), and the flow rows got literal ones (transit → map, debtors/receivables/cost-missing → wallet, recurring → calendar). An icon is understandable exactly until the same one means two different doors on one screen.
391. **Round 25, the owner's four fixes.** (1) «Qo'shma» from the chat now excludes AND deletes in one press — his report «qo'shma desa hali ham sistemada turibti» overruled the two-step split (#388) for THIS path; the button's confirm states both consequences, the qaysi-screen purge stays for older excludes. (2) The queued-reply bubble outlived the send because nothing re-rendered the page: the queue empties on the SERVER, the screen is a server component, and the first evidence anybody saw was Telegram itself delivering while the UI said «navbatda». The thread screen now carries `AutoRefresh` — `router.refresh()` every 10 s while the tab is visible, which re-renders the server half and deliberately keeps client state, so the composer never loses a draft. Incoming messages appear on the same tick, which is what a screen calling itself a chat owed its users anyway. (3) One person, several GS codes on one number is the owner's NORMAL customer: the thread header now names every active code the client's phones answer to (`codesSharingPhones` — own code first), instead of the one code the import happened to file the chat under. (4) The kanban cards carry the chat: `chatBadges(viewer)` is one DISTINCT ON query for a whole board, each lead/deal card shows 💬 when the viewer holds a conversation with that client and a warn-marked 💬! when the client spoke last — scoped per viewer like every chat read since #383, so a board never advertises a conversation its viewer could not open.
392. **A deal follows its cargo as DATA on the stage, not code in the pipeline.** The owner's item 6 («yuk holati o'zgarishi bilan sdelka varonkasida etaplarga avtomatik o'tadigan qilsa bo'ladimi?» → «ha zor bo'lardi»): `deal_stages.cargo_trigger` (migration 0050, nullable, additive — the seeded funnel ships with no triggers and behaves exactly as before) names one of the five states the warehouse already announces as events: received / departed / arrived / ready / handed. The engine (`applyCargoTrigger`) moves FORWARD only, by `sort_order`: with several receipts on one job the same state arrives many times, and without the guard a second truck's departure would drag a deal already «tayyor» back to «yo'lda». Open deals only — won and lost are settled, and a lost stage may not carry a trigger at all: losing a deal requires a person's written reason (`moveDeal`), which an automatic move cannot give. Every automatic move goes THROUGH `moveDeal`, so the audit row, `DealStageChanged` and the owner's phase-7 rules see it exactly as they see a drag on the board. Deal resolution per event: `receiptId` reads `receipts.deal_id` directly; batch and handover events reach their deals through `box_movements` (a JOIN, never a subquery in a join predicate — #152), with `ReadyForPickup` additionally filtered by its client, because that event is per client of the batch. And `linkReceipt` itself counts as `received` when the receipt is confirmed — linking is where the owner said he starts, and by then the cargo is usually already sitting in China.
393. **`BatchUnloaded` was declared in M4, offered as a rule trigger in round 18 — and emitted NOWHERE.** Found because round 26 needed «mashina tushirildi» as a trigger and the event to hang it on did not actually exist. `finishUnload` now emits it (same transaction as the status write), which also means any automation rule the owner had already pointed at BatchUnloaded starts firing with this release — stated to him. The lesson for the next event: a type in `DomainEventType` and in the rules dropdown with no emit site is a lie told by the settings screen.
394. **The deal funnel got its first editor, and the platform hook crosses by dynamic import.** No editor existed — the eight seeded stages WERE the design, so the cargo trigger forced the screen into being (`/bitimlar/etaplar`). It mirrors the lead editor including the in-transaction needs_open/needs_won law, gated `crm.manage` like the lead settings (reshaping everyone's funnel is not the deal-write list; no new permission — #170). The event worker reaches the wms engine the way `startBoss` reaches wms workers — `await import('../../wms/deals/auto-stage')` — because platform never imports wms statically; fenced in its own try/catch beside the phase-7 rules so a stuck funnel cannot block the notification fan-out. Proven red three ways (#166, string-edit): forward-only guard stripped → the drag-back test fails; open-only guard stripped → the won-deal test fails; the hook stubbed → all five event-driven tests fail while the link-path test stays green, which is exactly the wiring the stub removed.
395. **«Qisman topshirildi» — the owner's own design, told back to the engine.** His words the day after round 26 shipped: the deal walks the stages by the FIRST part, and before «topshirildi» there is a place called «qisman topshirildi» where it waits «hammasi topshirilgungacha». So `handed` changed meaning: it now fires only when EVERY box of the deal's linked cargo is issued, and a sixth trigger value `handed_partial` (migration 0051 widens the CHECK) catches the in-between — the first handover of a split job parks the deal there. The denominator excludes lost and void boxes, the deferral's lesson retold: a box that will never be issued must not hold the deal at «qisman» for ever, and a deal whose entire cargo was voided is not "handed" (at least one real issue required). A single-shipment deal is fully issued by its one handover and skips the partial stop — forward-only already knew how to jump columns. A funnel with no partial stage simply waits at its current column until the last box goes out. Safe to redefine `handed` because round 26 was not yet deployed: no production stage carries any trigger. No event announces a partial handover — `handed_partial` is only ever a TARGET, chosen per deal from a `BoxIssued` event by the `dealFullyIssued` predicate; red-proven by stripping the split and watching the partial test fail.
396. **A deadline learned to carry a clock, and the clock learned whose wall it was on.** The owner's ask («zadachalar kunga emas, soatlargacha bo'lingan bo'lsa — butun proekt bo'yicha») cost no migration: `tasks.due_at` had been a timestamptz with an `all_day` flag since phase 3, and only the FORM refused to ask for a time. The form now takes an optional time beside the date — a date alone still means the whole day, because "sometime Friday" is a real answer — plus a hidden `Date.getTimezoneOffset()` field. That offset is the load-bearing part: a typed 14:00 is a WALL CLOCK, Tashkent's or Yiwu's, and the server sits in UTC — without knowing whose wall it was, every timed deadline would be stored five hours late, which on a 30-minute SLA is not a deadline at all. Timed tasks render on the reader's own clock and go red at their minute; all-day tasks keep the end-of-UTC-day convention untouched. Telegram texts print timed deadlines in Asia/Tashkent — a message cannot ask its reader where they sit, and the staff who live on these messages sit there.
397. **Hisoblash is a REQUEST with a clock, and the clock is stopped by the work.** The owner's design, from his own answers: the salesperson picks WHICH VED person (answer 2 — so `requestCalc` refuses an assignee outside the editable `ved.docs` grants), the deadline scales with the job («1 dona TNVED kod — yarim soat … maksimum 2 soat»: 30 minutes a line capped at 120), and the finish line is the calculation being SAVED — `saveLines` closes the request and its task in one move — because a button that says "done" measures who remembers buttons, not who calculates fast. A lead has no lines to save, so there the closed task is the only end there is; `completeTask` reaches the wms clock by dynamic import, the same crossing as everything else. One open request per card, held by a partial unique index against two simultaneous presses. Lateness is announced ONCE (an `overdue_notified_at` stamp), by a five-minute sweep rather than the morning digests — a 30-minute deadline flagged next morning is an anecdote — to the waiting salesperson and the owner, never to the assignee, whose /bugun is already red. The report (`/reports/hisoblash`, the staff-activity shelf and gate) is the owner's question as a table: done, average, worst, on-time share, and what is waiting right now. Red-proven by stripping the `saveLines` hook and watching the stops-the-clock test fail.
398. **The accountant's Excel was kept, not replaced.** Her sheet — a row per prixod, a column per expense (rastamojka, usluga, yo'lkira, sertifikat), one total — was the WORKFLOW, and the one-form-per-cost UI is why «rastamojkani yozishga uspet qilmayman». The grid on the batch card renders exactly her table over exactly the owner's cost-type dictionary and turns one save into ordinary receipt-scope cost entries through `addCostEntry` — audited, FX-frozen and allocated by the same engine, so the landed cost cannot tell a grid entry from a form entry, and nothing new had to be taught to any report. Two guards carry the weight: every already-entered sum is printed under its cell (a second session must SEE the customs bill before typing it twice), and the service re-proves each receipt's batch membership before writing anything — the grid only offers members, but a stale tab must not hang somebody else's prixod with our inspector's fee (red-proven by stripping the check). Found by m9's strict locator, not by eyes: the grid's currency select briefly wore the SAME `aria-label="currency"` as the cost panel above it, and the money spec refused to pick between them — an accessibility name is an API, and two controls on one page must not share it.
399. **The money went to where the person already stands; only the register was truly missing.** The complaint «hamma narsa har xil joyda tarqoq» decomposed into three different truths. (a) The mispriced internal legs were a WORKFLOW error, not a system gap: writing YW-001's 30 $/kub and GZ-001's 35 $/kub both onto KA-001 blends them over everyone's boxes — each leg's cost belongs on its own batch, and the landed-cost engine already stacks the journey per box; told to the owner as method, no code. (b) The pricing screen was two-thirds of the «partiya moliyasi» he asked for (cost/price/margin per client, batch totals) — it gained the per-client overall balance with a ledger link, labelled OVERALL honestly, because a payment is money on the account, not on a truck, and pretending to batch-scope it would invent a fact the ledger does not hold. (c) «Kimdan qancha pul olganimni qanday yozaman» split into write and read: the write always existed (client ledger, kassa select since #361); the READ — a period's payments row for row with their cash box, the thing she kept in a notebook — did not, and is now `/finance/reestr` + a payments XLSX beside the other accounting exports, gated `finance.view` like the screens whose facts it repeats, NOT `finance.reports`: who paid is not the company's margin.
400. **The tannarx opened up, and a long word zoomed the whole page out.** The owner's follow-ups the same day: the per-client cost on the batch-money screen now unfolds (a press, not a hover — phones have no hover) into its parts — «YW-001 · yo'lkira — $60» — via `batchClientCostBreakdown`, which is `boxLandedCost`'s rows folded per client, proven to sum to the whole; and the grid's columns he named (Rastamojka, Zatamojka, CCT — plus Yo'lkira) are seeded as cost types by fixed code, idempotently, with the admin screen owning them after birth. The grid itself got its Excel face: cell borders, zebra rows, an already-written totals row and a being-entered totals row. THE FIND of the day came from m9p refusing to click: the freshly seeded «Растаможка / Rastamojka» plus two buttons overflowed the 360-px viewport by 13 px on /admin/cost-types, and mobile Chrome answered by zooming the ENTIRE page out — every click coordinate on the screen silently shifted, and Playwright's own measurements disagreed with the page's by exactly the scale factor. Diagnosed with an elementsFromPoint probe after scroll-margins and centring did nothing, fixed with `flex-wrap` on the card header. The lesson generalises: a mobile layout that lets ANY row exceed the viewport does not overflow politely — it rescales the whole screen, and everything on it, by a factor nobody chose.
401. **Round 30 closed the section-3 list the owner pointed at.** (a) The VED manager — the last working role without a home — wakes to the hisoblash queue: the one queue in the company with MINUTES on it leads (`ved_flow-hero` → /bugun, red once anything is late), then the departed trucks whose papers never reached the agent (`sent_to_agent_at IS NULL` on in_transit/arrived), then goods lines on open deals with no TNVED code. Placed AFTER sales in `buildHomeFlow`: a person wearing both hats lives the funnel's day, and the calc clock still reaches them through /bugun. (b) The attachment log-only period (#369) ended: `decideAttachmentRead` now stamps `enforce` on every CODED deny — months of `[attachment-authz]` lines and the whole e2e suite exercising the serve path stand behind each branch — with one stated exception, `unmapped`: the legacy free-form entity types from before the upload allowlist are old real files, not private chats, and 404ing history the code never learned to name would break it for no security gain; the warn line stays their inventory. Red-proven by stripping the stamp (three refusal tests fail). (c) The deal-stage editor grew the lead editor's other two moves — reorder (order feeds the board AND the cargo engine's forward-only rule) and delete-with-move (a stage's deals go somewhere the operator picked, the open/won funnel law holding inside the transaction).
402. **Round 31: the yashik audit — the owner lifted the parking («unda shu yashikni ham tuzat») and a 20-candidate adversarial audit confirmed 11 defects, every fix red-proven.** The money first, because it was unanimous: `createCrate` inserted the crating cost and NOTHING ever ran — `amount_usd` stayed NULL, zero allocations, so the yashik fee reached no tannarx, no client share, no P&L, for every crate since M2. Now the entry is recomputed the moment the transaction commits, like every cost the forms enter. Its twin was quieter and worse: recompute read the LIVE `crate_id` pointer, which issue/dissolve/lost all clear, so the first FX sweep after the crate's life ended DELETED the allocations for good. Crate scope now reads the movement log (`crate_packed`) — membership is written once at packing and money follows what was paid for, not a pointer that dies. A crate cost also became correctable (scope `crate` joined `costEntrySchema`, CostPanel on the crate card, gate `costs.enter_receipt` at the crate's warehouse): a wrong fee had no void and no second entry.
403. **A scan that moves nothing is a duplicate, not a 500 — and drizzle's empty `values()` is a landmine next to every fan-out.** A stray-carrying crate scanned twice (operator habit, outbox retry, second phone) left `toLoad` empty and `insert(boxMovements).values([])` THREW; /api/scan/sync has no try/catch, so the phone's outbox retried the poisoned batch for ever and every scan queued behind it showed green while recording nothing — #221's failure mode, reintroduced by the fix's own plumbing. The empty case now answers `duplicate` (strays still named). The same class was then found in `dissolveCrate` — a crate emptied by receipt-void could never be dissolved — by the new test, not by the audit. RULE: any `values(list.map(…))` behind a filter needs the empty guard.
404. **«Qo'shib yuklandi» is a fact about a BOX, not about the scan — and journey flags die when the journey does.** Confirming a crate that carries strays used to re-record EVERY member as added-on-spot (scan events are what the batch register and the finish audit count), and the flag was never cleared, so a re-planned box printed last truck's ⚠ on the next truck's manifest. Now only boxes that actually joined off-plan get the event/flag/`loaded_on_spot` movement (`isSpot`, per box), scan events are written only for boxes the scan actually moved, landing retires the journey flags, and the manifest reads THIS batch's load events instead of live box state — so a reprint after handover keeps its crate column and its ⚠ marks tell the truth.
405. **The crate label vouches for the CRATE, not for every box its record lists — reality-wins is for a box somebody physically scanned.** A member short-loaded at the origin keeps its `crateId` (no per-box un-crate exists), and the unload fan-out used to "reality-wins" it into the destination: the client was told three boxes arrived while one sat on a shelf in Yiwu, origin stock lost it, and it was immediately issuable at Tashkent — a handover could be signed for a box that is not there. The unload crate scan now accepts only members that rode THIS batch (or already landed here); the left-behind are NAMED in the ack (`notArrived`), never moved. Single-box scans keep the auto-transfer rule — there somebody is holding the box. And the crate row itself finally travels: unloading moves `crates.warehouse_id` to the destination, so an arrived yashik can be planned onward and counted where it really stands (the origin inventory stops listing departed crates: members filtered to present-here, empty crates dropped).
406. **Guards the audit found missing, each refused at the door it belongs to:** a crate already promised to a live plan cannot enter a second one (`crate_on_another_plan` at submit — the old failure was the second VERDICT dying mid-transaction on a bare `insufficient_stock`, rolling back the agent's recorded approval); voiding boxes lets go of the crate (receipt void and lot-edit shrink both clear `crateId` — a void ghost member jammed dissolve and every whole-crate scan for ever); and changing the CLIENT of crated cargo is refused (`boxes_crated`, shown on the assign form via useActionState) — the crate row would go on naming the old client while its boxes answer to the new one. Everything in rounds 402-406 carries a test shown red with its guard string-stripped (#166); the audit itself ran as three find-lenses × per-finding adversarial verify, and its 9 rejections were either verifiers reading the fixes already in the tree, or claims with no wrong outcome (crate dims on the label only; «one place» is plan-granularity, not an invoice law).
407. **The card finds the thread under a phone-sibling code.** The owner's report («bitimda ham leadda ham ko'rinmayabti» while «Suhbatlar» plainly held the chats) had a structural cause, not a rendering one: one person holds several GS codes on one number, the import pinned each chat to whichever code the phone matched, and `TelegramThread` asked `conversationFor` for the CARD's exact client id — a deal filed under the sibling code got an empty panel. `threadClientFor(clientId, viewer)` now resolves the card to the thread: the exact client when their thread exists, otherwise the ONE phone-sibling that holds it (via `activeClientsByPhone`, the cabinet's own matcher), refusing on ambiguity — the lead resolver's rule, because the wrong person's private chat is worse than none. The panel names the sibling code in its header, links to that conversation, and the reply box posts onto the code that actually holds the chat. Viewer scoping is unchanged — the sibling search runs under the same own-account-or-supervision filter as every other tg read. Red-proven: sibling branch stripped → the new crm test fails.
408. **The supervision view widened to the vedchi and the admin — by his sentence, in one predicate.** Owner, 2026-07-31: «leaddagi chatlar hammaniki o'ziga ko'rinsin … vedchi va adminga hammaniki ko'rinsin, qaysi hodim qanday gaplashgani ko'rinsin». The rank-and-file rule is untouched (own account only), and the widening lives where round 21 promised it would — one line, now named `seesAllTg`: super_admin, the admin ROLE, and whoever's EDITABLE grants carry `ved.docs` (#170 — an invented customs role earns the view the moment it gets the grant, and loses it with it). The reason the vedchi qualifies is the calc flow: the client sends the goods files and photos into their chat with WHICHEVER manager, and the person doing the hisoblash has to read them where they landed. Every tg door asks the same predicate now — `canReadTg` on the screens (thread panel, /suhbatlar, both dock routes, the app-bar 💬), the nav item and the ved menu carry `/suhbatlar`, and the photo route's `seesAllTgChats` is an alias of the SAME function, so a pasted URL opens exactly as far as the screens do and no further. Replying stays own-account for everybody — supervision is eyes, not a mouth. Red-proven by stripping the two new clauses (predicate test + photo-authz test both fail).
409. **The card lists WHO talked, and the supervision view picks whose thread to read.** Owner's example was concrete: a lead/deal card for +99890… must show the chat AND «qaysi hodimlar gaplashganiga qarab spiskasi» — selectable. `threadManagers(clientId)` (id, name, message count) feeds a chips row on the card panel and on /suhbatlar/[clientId]; for a supervision viewer each chip links to `?hodim=<id>`, and `conversationFor` grew an optional manager filter that acts ONLY under `viewer.all` — for rank-and-file the own-account rule already fixes whose thread it is, and a foreign id pasted into the URL must not widen it (red-proven: honoring the param unconditionally fails the new test). Names without content stay shared knowledge (round 20's line): a salesperson sees WHO ELSE talked to their client, and reads only their own thread.
410. **The staff bot, batch A — the bot answers to the same rules as the screens, or it is a back door.** His four answers fixed the design (podklyuch = rastamojka+yo'lkira combined; the bot COLLECTS, staff quote the price; hisoblatish for everyone; the client button = the cabinet, nothing more). What shipped: (a) an unknown chat opening the bot is offered TWO DOORS — «Hodim» links by the Telegram-verified own number against the employee list (active users only, the cabinet's spoof-proof contact rule restated in platform because platform must never import wms), «Mijoz» is the existing cabinet flow untouched; a chat already held by a colleague is refused, never silently re-pointed. (b) A task notification carries «✅ Bajarildi» — the button asks for the result, the next message delivers it, and `completeTask` runs under the CHAT's honest identity so `canActOnTask` refuses a stranger exactly as the web does. (c) A debtor-issue request lands on the decider's phone with «Ruxsat / Yo'q»; the permission is checked IN THE BOT (`finance.debt_override` from the editable grants) because a chat id is not a session and the service trusts its callers. (d) The morning digest already existed (TasksDue); its body is now `composeMyDayText`, served on demand by the «📋 Bugun» keyboard button — the push and the pull say the same words. Buttons attach at SEND time by type (`buttonsFor`), so notification rows stay plain data and a bot-less deployment changes nothing. The grammy shell registers BEFORE the cabinet and calls next() for chats that are not its business — a customer's contact still reaches the client flow untouched. Red-proven: the permission gate and the chat-taken guard each stripped → their tests fail. NOT live-tested against Telegram (no network here): the first real button press should be watched in docker logs.
411. **Staff bot batch B — the bot may know only what the person already knows.** Three of his six items shipped together because they share one rule. (a) LOOKUP: a member of staff types a client code, a box label, a crate or a truck code and the bot answers where it is — `wms/bot/lookup.ts`, reached from platform by dynamic import, given a `botActorFor(chatId)` that mirrors `getActor` WITHOUT a session (permissions union, warehouse scope from the roles COLUMN per #199/0049, assigned warehouses). Every branch then asks the same questions the screens ask: `inScope` on the box's warehouse — or, for cargo in transit, on its batch's two ends, because a box between two countries belongs to nobody's floor and both ends legitimately care — and the balance line exists only for `finance.view`/`finance.manage`. A scoped hand asking about another warehouse's cargo is told so, honestly, instead of being shown it. Red-proven twice (money gate, scope check). (b) The WAITING CUSTOMER (migration 0053, additive): the conversations screen's most valuable fact only reached whoever opened it; now the manager whose account holds the chat is told, ONCE per silence — the crossing is remembered on the message row (`reminded_at`, partial index), because a reminder that repeats every sweep is a reminder people mute, and then the one that mattered is muted with it. The threshold is a setting (`unanswered_reminder_minutes`, 30; 0 switches it off) and the sweep runs every five minutes like the calc watch, for the same reason: the unit is minutes. Red-proven by stripping the mark filter. (c) The LOAD/UNLOAD SUMMARY: `finishLoading` and `finishUnload` tell whoever holds `plans.manage` how the truck actually went — loaded, short-loaded with codes, added on spot / accepted, missing with codes — AFTER the transaction and never to the person who pressed the button. Recipients come from the editable grants (#170), so a role the owner invents is included the day it gets the permission. Both types live in the `operations` mute group, not `alerts`: a real deviation already has its own alarm, and routine news filed as an alarm is how alarms stop being read.
412. **«Hisoblatish» — the bot COLLECTS, the staff member quotes.** His redesign of my proposal, and it is the better one: I had suggested the AI read a client's conversation and pick out the quote request, and he refused it in one sentence — «hato ishlash extimoli yuqori». Instead a member of staff opens the bot, presses «🧮 Hisoblatish», picks the section, names the customer, and sends exactly the material that belongs to this job. What is sent is what is analysed; nothing is inferred from a chat nobody pointed at. THREE SECTIONS, his: yo'lkira asks for the two ends of the road as well as the cargo facts, rastamojka asks only for the cargo (kg, kub, tovar nomi — customs does not care where the truck starts), and podklyuch is not a third service but the two ADDED TOGETHER, so it asks for everything. The completeness check is pure and tested as such, including the rule that a zero is a blank rather than a number; red-proven by making rastamojka demand a route, which collapses the sections into one. THE MODEL EXTRACTS AND EXPLAINS, IT DOES NOT PRICE — his answer 2, and a model guessing freight rates would be guessing with his money. What a person TYPED wins over what the model read (`intake-manual.ts` reads «250 kg», «5 kub», «Yiwu → Toshkent»): somebody looking at the material stated a fact, and a model that missed it must not erase it — which also means the whole feature still works on a server with no API key. A typed product list is deliberately NOT split into items: a wrong split reads as fact on the card. LANDING is his rule — a coded client's request joins their newest OPEN deal (or opens one), a stranger gets a lead, and a prospect who asks twice joins the card the first request made rather than sprouting a second beside it (matched on the last nine digits, the client book's rule). An ambiguous phone names NOBODY, red-proven: landing a quote on the wrong person's card is worse than asking. The AI's working — how it grouped, which TNVED code and why — goes on the card's lenta as a note, with the sent files attached to it, because that is what he asked to see. The calc clock is NOT started here: picking the VED person lives on the card, where the picker already is (round 28). Collection state is in memory with a 30-minute TTL, the round-35 pattern; the FILES go to storage as they arrive, pre-bound to the note id minted up front (#180). Stated cost: a bot restart mid-collection loses the typed text and the person starts again. LESSON, caught by a test and not by reading: JavaScript's `\b` is defined on ASCII, so «120кг» has no word boundary after it and the unit never matched — the parser uses a negative lookahead with the `u` flag.
413. **A receipt could be attached to the wrong job and never detached — the link only ever existed on the deal's side.** The owner asked how split shipments join a deal («bo'linib kelgan yuklarni biriktraman»), and answering it honestly turned up the hole: cargo is pointed at a job from two places — the receiving wizard's picker and the deal card's «Prixodni biriktirish» — and BOTH of them can only ever add. The deal card offers `unlinkedReceipts`, so the moment a receipt is on a job it stops being unlinked and disappears from every picker in the system; the receipt card said nothing about a deal at all. The service had `linkReceipt(receiptId, null)` from the day it was written and no button anywhere could call it. So a prixod filed under the wrong bitim stayed there, silently inflating one job's reality and starving another's — and the mistake is exactly the one that happens, because the receiving clerk picks the deal at four in the morning with the truck waiting. The panel now lives on the CARGO, where somebody looking at the wrong pairing already is: the current job as a link, a picker of the client's open deals, and an empty option that detaches. Two decisions inside it. The picker always contains the deal this receipt is ON, even when that deal has since been won or lost — the open list would hide the very mistake being corrected. And it is gated on the deal-write list (`canWriteDeal`), the same one the action enforces, not on a warehouse permission: re-filing a job's cargo moves money between jobs, and to somebody without the grant a deal code is a link into a screen they cannot open. Nothing about the service changed — `client_mismatch` still refuses another client's cargo, and a confirmed receipt still fires the 'received' trigger on the job it joins. Stated to the owner and true: moving a receipt AWAY from a deal does not move that deal's funnel stage back, because a stage is a person's record of where the job got to, not a derived value. Red-proven per #166 by deleting the panel from the page, rebuilding and watching m9h fail at `receipt-deal-pick` on a fresh database.
414. **A vacuous assertion hid a race for a whole round, and only a test that really needed the result found it.** The round-38 spec went green here and red on the runner, twice: the receipt link was not on the deal card when the new half of the test went looking for it. The cause was two lines above. After pressing «Prixodni biriktirish» the spec asserted `deal-compare` contains `/%/` — and the verdict line ALWAYS prints the threshold's own «(Porog: 25 %)», with or without cargo, so that assertion passed the instant the page existed and waited for nothing. The very next line reloaded the page, which aborts an in-flight server action; on a fast machine the action had landed first, on a loaded runner it had not. Nothing downstream had ever depended on the link actually being there, so the spec had been passing on a coin-flip since the deal feature shipped. The fix is the rule: **wait on server-rendered evidence of the thing you just caused, not on a locator that would be there anyway** — the receipt ROW appearing, and a SIGNED percentage (`/\+\d+\.\d %/`) that only the deviation prints. Two more of the same kind in the new half, each waiting on the panel's own deal link rather than on the `<select>`, whose value is local state and says nothing about what the server did. Also made retry-survivable: the picker now takes the FIRST unlinked receipt and demands only one, because Playwright's CI retry re-runs a test whose first attempt has already consumed one — which is how the retry reported a different failure than the run.
415. **The ledger had one counterparty — the client — and every other party we owe money to lived in somebody's head.** The owner's question about taking trucks on credit («transportlarni transport kompaniyasidan qarzga olamiz … ularni hisobini oladigan qilaolamizmi») turned out to name a hole with four sides. `cost_entries` recorded that a truck cost $3,200 and allocated it down to every box, so tannarx was right — and nowhere said we still owed it. Customs paid out of ANOTHER firm's account looked, if entered at all, like our cash leaving. The Chinese warehouses rented jointly with a transport company and the Chinese staff paid through it had no home but «Boshqa». And the two men who wire som into the company account and collect dollars from the till were a notebook. All four are one shape: **an account per counterparty**, so they are one table and not four features (migration 0054, additive: `partner_types`, `partners`, `partner_transactions`, plus a nullable `partner_id` on cost entries, expenses and client payments, and the customs firm on the batch). THE RULE the whole design rests on: **a cost and a debt are different facts.** Naming a partner on a cost does not move money — it writes a `charge` on that partner's account POINTING AT the cost row, so the P&L reads costs and the partner screen reads debts and neither counts the other; paying the partner later is money moving, not a second cost. Voiding either side takes the other with it, or a truck we stopped paying for goes on standing on the firm's account. Five row kinds, and `receipt` is the one that earns its place: money the partner puts INTO an account of ours, which is exactly the cash buyers' first leg — som lands, we owe dollars, and what is left after they collect is the rate we gained, visible rather than swallowed. The database enforces the pairing that keeps the cash-flow report honest (`CHECK ((type IN ('receipt','payment')) = (account_id IS NOT NULL))`) — money that moved must name its box, money that did not must not claim one. **The three-cornered settlement** is its own screen because it belongs to neither card: a client pays their debt to us into our supplier's account, two balances move and no till opens. Its two sides are DELIBERATELY separate amounts in separate currencies — his words, the client sends $100 and the firm then says at what rate it counted it and how much of our debt it closed — so the form asks for both and PRINTS the gap; averaging them would be inventing a rate. Proof is mandatory (his answer): a file or a written note, checked before anything is written, and the file must be the entering person's own upload or a stolen uuid would let one clerk decorate another's entry. Types are the owner's editable list including «Boshqa», never a compiled one; a counterparty may BE a client, one account per client, one card with two ledgers. Red-proven per #166 by stripping four guards — the cost→charge link, the expense's dropped cash box, the proof requirement, and the settlement's paired void — and watching each of its tests fail. Deliberately NOT built and stated: opening balances (he does not know the totals yet — «bizni sistemada faqat 1 ta partiya yurgizilyabti»), and any automatic posting of the rate difference, which stays a signed `adjust` a person presses.
416. **Round 39 opened two holes in the money reports the same day it closed the ledger's.** Partner `receipt` and `payment` rows move REAL cash boxes — a buyer wiring som into the company account, the till paying the transport firm — and `accountBalances()` knew nothing about either, so the very screen somebody counts notes against would have been wrong the first time the accountant used the new feature. In the other direction the cash-flow report over-reported: a client payment made into a PARTNER's account never touched a box of ours, and a truck the transport company is still owed for has cost us nothing in cash yet, but both were being counted as money that moved. Fixed on both sides — balances add partner receipts and subtract partner payments; cash flow excludes settlements (`client_transactions.partner_id IS NOT NULL`) and partner-settled costs and expenses, and adds the two partner cash rows as their own labelled lines. THE LESSON worth carrying: a feature that introduces a new way for money to move must be walked through every existing report that adds money up, not only the screens it ships with. Both directions red-proven (#166). **Balans** (`/accounting/balance`) becomes answerable for the first time in the same round: cash + what clients owe us + what counterparties owe us − what we owe = the net position. Deliberately management accounting and NOT a balance sheet — cargo sitting in the warehouse is not valued, because it is the client's goods and its money side is already in what they owe us; valuing it would double-count the same shipment and read as profit belonging to somebody else. Counterparties in front on their account are reported separately rather than netted against those we owe: one is a bill to pay and the other is money to chase, and one number hides which. Also his access fix: `/kontragentlar` joins the VED manager's curated menu (accountant had it, admin and super_admin are uncurated) — the grants were already right, only the menu list was short.
417. **`Date.now() + 7n` in one test is the plain `Date.now()` of a test that runs seven milliseconds later.** CI went red on `tg_messages_unique_idx` — (manager, peer, message) already existed — in a spec nothing in the round had touched. Every site in `crm.integration.test.ts` minted its own Telegram peer id from the millisecond clock, and two of them added small constants (`+ 7n`, `+ 8n`) to keep apart from their neighbours in the same test. That works only while the tests stay more than seven milliseconds apart, which is a property of the runner's mood and not of the code. One shared `nextPeer()` now multiplies the millisecond out and puts a counter in the low digits, so two calls cannot collide however fast they follow each other. The rule: a unique id derived from a CLOCK is unique only by luck; the collision-proof shape is clock × N + counter, and the constant-offset trick is the shape that hides the bug until a runner is quick.
418. **Three things the counterparty round got wrong the moment real people used it.** (1) The amount box was a third of a phone screen wide, sharing its row with the currency select — on the one form whose whole point is a number. It now takes its own labelled line at 2xl monospace, right-aligned, with the currency box narrowed beside it. (2) **The warehouse could not say who settled a cost.** The «who paid» picker shipped on the batch card only, and the warehouse enters most of the money on a PRIXOD — so a truck taken on credit and a cost paid from our own till looked identical to the person entering them. The picker now stands on the receipt and crate cards too, and every saved row PRINTS the payer's name: its absence was half the complaint, because even a correctly entered row said nothing afterwards. (3) **Customs is decided per prixod, not per truck** (migration 0055, additive). The batch-level firm shipped in round 39 is right for the usual case and stays as the truck's default, but the owner's real one breaks it: inside one truck some clients clear their own goods through their own firm and we clear the rest, and the money follows — a prixod the client cleared costs us nothing. A receipt now holds its own answer with THREE states, and the distinction between two of them is the whole feature: `null` is silence and follows the truck, `false` is an answer ("we clear this one") and must not fall back. Red-proven by collapsing `!== null` into `=== true` and watching the case go red. `batchCustomsRows` joins membership through `box_movements` (#152), never a subquery in a join predicate. Also caught in the same pass: the e2e retired its partner and navigated away in the same breath, racing the action — the third instance this week of *waiting on nothing after pressing a button*, and the reason the spec now waits for the server's own answer (the flipped hidden input) before it moves.
419. **`shrink-0` turned a cosmetic width bug into a broken form, and only a screenshot found it.** The owner reported the counterparty amount box "still the same" after a deploy that provably contained the fix — three rounds of chasing his cache followed, and the answer was in the picture he eventually sent: the amount field had collapsed to a sliver while the currency box took the whole row. `.input` carries `w-full`, and `.input` is declared outside a Tailwind layer, so it wins on source order over a plain `w-24`. Before this round that was merely ugly — the select was full-width but still SHRANK, so both boxes ended up sharing the row. Adding `shrink-0` to "keep it narrow" removed the only thing that was saving the layout: the select then claimed 100 % and refused to give any of it back, and `min-w-0` let the input go to nothing. The codebase already knew this: every other currency select in the app says `!w-24`. THE TEST THAT NOW GUARDS IT MEASURES THE RENDERED BOX (`boundingBox().width`) rather than asserting a class name — a class-list assertion would have passed on the broken markup, since the markup was exactly what I intended. Red-proven by putting `w-24` back and watching the width assertion fail at 
 the real viewport. TWO PROCESS LESSONS, both mine: a UI change shipped without ever being looked at in a browser is a change nobody has checked, and when a user says "not fixed" after the server is proven current, ASK FOR THE SCREENSHOT FIRST — I spent three exchanges on his cache while the evidence was one message away.

420. **A feature folded inside a folded panel is a feature nobody shipped.** The owner said «boshqa 2 tasiniyam korib chiq» — check the other two items too — and this time I opened them in a browser at 360×800 and looked at the pictures instead of asserting about the markup. Item 3, per-prixod customs, was invisible: `CustomsPerReceipt` was a `<details>` nested inside the collapsed «Документы ВЭД» `Panel`, so reaching it took two taps with nothing on the outside of either fold to say it was there. Rastamojka now has a `Panel` of its own on the batch rail whose BADGE names the firm and counts the prixods answering for themselves («— · +1»), so "who clears this truck" is answered with no tap at all, and the per-prixod list inside is no longer a fold of its own — the panel is the only one. The rule this settles: a collapsed panel with nothing on its face is invisible whatever it holds, and nesting one inside another doubles the invisibility. The badge is not decoration; it is the panel's reason to be opened.

421. **A select that shares its row with a label renders two characters wide.** The same screenshot pass caught three of these at once, all the same shape: a `flex` row where the text beside the control has no reason to be short. The customs picker read «Ка» for «Как у партии» — a choice nobody can read back, on the screen that decides whose bill a customs charge is. The cost form's allocation basis read «по», and «по весу», «по объёму» and «клиенту напрямую» all begin that way, so the field that decides how money is spread across a truck was unreadable. The attachments tile is `w-16` with «Прикрепить» inside it — 11 characters in a 64-pixel box, hanging outside the card and over the first photo on the receipt screen. Each is fixed the same way: the control gets its own line (or its own width), and what shares a row with it must be something that genuinely fits. Measured, not asserted: the new e2e reads `boundingBox().width` and demands the customs picker be wider than 200 px, the way #419's guard demands the amount box be wider than 180. A class list proves nothing about what a phone draws.

422. **The save button that is always there teaches nobody anything.** Both customs controls drew a permanently visible, permanently disabled `Сохранить` — one per prixod, so a truck with twelve receipts drew twelve pale buttons that did nothing. The button now appears only once the answer has actually been changed. It is a smaller screen, it says what state the row is in without a word, and it removes the question "did I already save this one?" that a wall of identical dead buttons invites. The e2e depends on this: `receipt-customs-save` exists only when the row is dirty, which is a stronger assertion than "the button is there".

423. **Items 2 and 3 were correct all along; only item 3 was reachable.** Worth writing down because it is the honest result. The payer picker («Оплатили сами» → a counterparty) renders on the receipt cost form, on the crate card, on the batch card and on the expense form; the warehouse operator gets it too (gate `costs.enter_receipt`, verified by logging in as one and reading the rendered page, not by reading the gate); a saved cost carries the payer's name as a chip on its row; and the money behind it was already right — a cost naming a partner writes a `charge` against that partner and the client's cash boxes never move. Item 3's per-prixod answer saved, survived a reload, and applied per receipt exactly as designed. What was broken was that a person could not find item 3 and could not read either picker. That distinction matters for the next round: the integration tests were not wrong, they were just not testing the thing that was failing.

424. **An adversarial audit of the counterparty rounds found seventeen defects, and every one of them was mine, from this week.** Four independent lenses over every file rounds 39–43 touched — CSS cascade at 360 px, UI that can be invisible, does-the-button-do-the-thing, and money correctness — each finding then refuted by a per-finding verifier before it counted. What that pass is FOR, stated because it is not obvious: the integration suite for these files was green and largely right, because it tests what I thought about. The audit tests what I did not. The heaviest three were invisible to it by construction: a table with no scroll container (nothing in a database says a page is 430 px wide), a void that guarded one direction of a two-sided fact (each direction has its own test and mine only had the one), and a currency branch whose own comment claimed the opposite of what it did.

425. **Voiding the client half of a three-cornered settlement left our debt to the supplier forgiven for ever.** `recordSettlement` writes the pair in one transaction and links them by `partner_transactions.client_tx_id`; `voidPartnerTx` has taken the client half with it since the day it shipped, with a comment explaining exactly why the halves must not diverge. `voidTransaction` — the one the accountant actually reaches, because the client ledger is the screen with the wrong client on it — had no such branch. GS100 pays $1,000 into the transport firm's China account, the accountant realises it was the wrong client and voids the payment: GS100 correctly owes $1,000 again, and the firm's balance stays $1,000 lower for ever, with `/accounting/balance` reading $1,000 healthier than the company is. Now mirrored, in one transaction, matched on the FK that DEFINES the pair rather than on the client row's own `partner_id` — only `recordSettlement` ever sets `client_tx_id`, so the match cannot catch an ordinary payment. Both halves get their own audit row. THE GENERAL RULE: when a fact has two sides, the guard belongs on both, and a test for one side is not a test for the other.

426. **A cash box in any currency but USD or UZS was worth exactly nothing.** `companyBalance` had a two-branch conversion whose own comment three lines above said "anything else is treated as dollars" — it was dropped instead, so the moment the owner opens the CNY till for Yiwu (the obvious thing to do once the Chinese costs are in CNY) a box holding ¥300,000 contributes $0 to «Kassa» and $0 to the net figure, while its own row prints the yuan beside the words «no rate» — mislabelling the reason, because a rate exists. Now every box converts through the same generic `rateFor` the rest of the system uses, one lookup per currency rather than per box; `null` finally means what the JSDoc always claimed, which is that no rate has been entered for that currency at all.

427. **Naming a payer on a cost in a currency with no rate wrote the firm's name and no debt.** `chargeForCost` returns silently when `amountUsd` is null, which is exactly what the recompute leaves when `rateFor` finds nothing — so the cost row afterwards showed the transport firm's name as a chip next to a «no rate» badge, stating that the firm had settled it, while that firm's account never heard of it. Entering the rate later did not fix it: `recomputeEntry` filled in the dollars and rebuilt the allocations and never revisited the charge. Two halves, and the second is the one that matters for rows already saved: entry now REFUSES (`fx_missing`, the same rule `addPartnerTx` and `addExpense` already enforce — a debt with no dollar figure is not a debt anybody can record), and `recomputeEntry` posts the charge once a dollar figure exists, so the /admin/fx recompute repairs every row entered before the refusal shipped. `chargeForCost` was already idempotent per cost, so the sweep can run for ever.

428. **Retiring a counterparty made two money screens disagree, and hid the door back.** `companyBalance` counts every partner row whether the account is retired or not — correctly, a debt is real either way — while /kontragentlar listed active accounts only and summed those. Hiding a firm we still owed $8,000 therefore dropped $8,000 off «jami qarzimiz» and changed nothing on /accounting/balance, which is the page that LINKS here; and since the hide button lives on the card and nothing then linked to that card, the account was reachable only by typing its uuid. Fixed on the READ side rather than by guarding the retire: a retired account with a live balance keeps its row, dimmed and saying so, and a retired account that is settled disappears — which is the tidying the button was for. A guard would have fought the card's own stated principle («retired, never deleted: the account it carries is a record»). Same shape one level up: grouping ran over ACTIVE partner types, so hiding a type on /admin/partner-types deleted its accounts from every section of the screen while their debt stayed in the total above. Grouping now runs over every type; only the form filters to the live ones, because that is what hiding a type means.

429. **Two more places where a screen said one thing and the row said another.** (a) An `adjust` is the one kind whose SIGN is the meaning — `balanceExpr` adds it signed — and the ledger row read it as a reduction, so a +50 correction printed «−$50.00» in green under a balance it had just pushed up, and the rows stopped adding to the total on the same screen. The predicate is now `raisesBalance`, EXPORTED and called by both, so the row and the sum cannot hold different opinions again. (b) The expense form offered a cash box and a payer at once and `addExpense` silently dropped the cash box; the till the accountant named never moved and nothing afterwards distinguished the row from one entered with no cash box at all. The picker now disappears with the choice, the way the counterparty form already hides its cash box for the kinds that move no money, and the already-translated, never-rendered `paidByHint` finally says why. (c) A settlement's client half is account-less BY CONSTRUCTION, and the accountant's home counted exactly those as "payments waiting to be placed" — one unfinishable chore per settlement until the month rolled over. `cashFlow` had learned this clause; the home count had not. The payments register keeps the row (the client really did pay) and names the firm instead of printing the red «no cash box», which is the honest fix rather than filtering money out of a register somebody is searching.

430. **Three tripwires, because three of these had shipped before under a different name.** (1) `tests/unit/style-cascade.test.ts`: `.input` is declared outside a Tailwind layer and carries `w-full`, so a plain `w-24` on the same element does nothing — it shipped three times (the counterparty amount box, the settlement amounts, the TN VED import row), and each time it was invisible to typecheck, to lint, to dev and to any assertion about markup, because the markup was exactly what was intended. The test is the grep-shaped rule: a width that names a SIZE on a class list that already owns its width must carry the `!`. `w-full`/`w-auto`/`w-0` are exempt and the comment says why. (2) `tests/unit/i18n-keys.test.ts`: the fourth instance of #163. `void-tx.tsx` shipped `tc('confirm')` with `common.confirm` in none of the four bundles, so the void button on the counterparty ledger read its own key path in every language — and no bundle-to-bundle comparison can see that, which is precisely why settings descriptions, bridge labels and feed labels each needed a bespoke test. This is the general case: every literal `t('…')` in the source is a key the page WILL ask for, so the source is the source of truth. Bindings are collected per FILE, not per scope, because `tc` is `crm` in one component of `page.tsx` and `calc` in another; a key counts as found under any namespace its name carries there. (3) A rule for the next reader, learned the hard way this round: **`git checkout` is not how a red proof is undone.** Restoring `finance/service.ts` that way reverted the uncommitted fix I was proving along with the strip, and only a grep caught it. String-edit out, string-edit back — #166 says so and now says why.

431. **The e2e that retires a partner had to change, and the new version is the truer one.** m9y retired a counterparty holding $2,000 and asserted the row vanished. Under #428 it no longer does, and it should not: a debt we still owe is not tidied away. The spec now SETTLES the account first — a payment for the full balance — and then retires it, which is both what the accountant would really do and a stronger demonstration than the old assertion was. Also added there, all measured rather than asserted from markup: the card must not rescale the page (`window.innerWidth === 360`, the round-29 signature), a positive `adjust` must print as a rise, the rename button must exist and work, and deleting the proof file must close the save gate again — `AttachmentsPanel` only ever announced uploads, so a caller gating on "is there a file" went stale the moment one was removed.

432. **The speed round started by measuring, and the measurement disagreed with everyone's guess.** The owner had said the system «qotib ishlayabti» and the standing theory was slow pages. Cloning his real database (26k box movements, 10.9k boxes, 4.4k receipts, 1.7k clients) and timing all 22 main screens said otherwise: worst server time 313 ms, median well under 150 ms, and NOT ONE query over 200 ms in the postgres log. What the log did show, once the threshold was dropped to zero and round trips were COUNTED per page, was `/accounting` issuing **1,564 queries for one screen** and `/accounting/balance` 1,611. Counting round trips, not timing them, is what found it — every one of those queries was under a millisecond, so no slow-query threshold on earth would have reported them.

433. **`accountBalances()` was six queries per cash box, and the owner keeps eighty-six of them.** 86 × 6 = 516 round trips, and three panels on the accounting screen each asked for the same list, so the page paid it three times over. Now five grouped aggregates and a join in JavaScript — the whole list for the price of one query per source — wrapped in React's `cache()`, the same per-request memo `getActor` has always used, so the three callers share one answer. Measured on his data: **/accounting 1,564 queries / 193 ms → 23 / 44 ms; /accounting/balance 1,611 / 186 ms → 31 / 53 ms.** The remaining pages issue 20–70 DISTINCT queries with nothing repeated and nothing over 30 ms, so after this the database is no longer the story. `log_min_duration_statement=200` now ships in the compose command block (pinned by the existing tuning tripwire) so the next one of these is found from the server rather than from a complaint.

434. **What was actually slow was nothing at all happening.** With the server answering in ~150 ms, a tap from Uzbekistan still takes about half a second — and App Router spends every millisecond of it showing the OLD screen, unchanged, with no spinner, no dimming, nothing. That is what «qotib qoldi» describes: not a slow app, a silent one. `NavProgress` is a two-pixel line that starts on the CLICK — before a request exists, which is the whole point — and ends when the URL changes. It stays invisible for the first 140 ms so a prefetched page draws nothing: a bar that flashes on every instant tap teaches people to ignore it, and it has to mean "this one is taking a moment".

435. **The indicator cost three rebuilds to a Next.js trap worth writing down: a `<Suspense>` boundary hydrates LATE.** The first version used `useSearchParams()` to detect arrival, which Next requires to sit under Suspense. Next renders that boundary as its fallback on the server, and the client hydrates it well after the page is interactive — so the component's `useEffect` had not attached its click listener yet, and the bar could not appear for exactly the taps it exists for. It rendered (the track was in the DOM) while its effects had not run, which is why reading the markup proved nothing and only a `console.log` inside the effect settled it. The fix was to drop BOTH navigation hooks: the component now watches `window.location.href` on the 90 ms tick it already runs, so it needs no Suspense, hydrates with the rest of the app shell, and its listener is there before anybody can tap. Second trap, same round: `useSearchParams()` returns a NEW object every render, so an effect keyed on it re-runs constantly — and that effect completed the bar, cancelling it a frame after it started. **Rule: never key an effect on `useSearchParams()` itself; key it on `.toString()`.**

436. **Two things about testing a navigation indicator, both learned by getting them wrong.** A Playwright `page.route('**/search')` matches no URL with a query string, and App Router asks for the next screen as `/search?_rsc=…` — so the "slow" navigation was never slowed and the test passed nothing. And aborting the prefetch to force a fresh request makes Next fall back to a FULL browser navigation, which tears down the React tree and takes the bar with it; the prefetch has to be allowed through and only the real navigation delayed. The spec now asserts both halves: a bar on a held-back navigation, and no bar at all on an instant one. Red-proven by rendering the component conditionally behind an env flag and watching the first assertion fail.

437. **CI went red on a test that borrowed a shared funnel column for its trigger.** The automation spec built its rule on `stages.find((s) => s.id !== from.id)` — a SEEDED deal stage, which every other test file's deals can also walk into. On the runner the rule fired twice and `fireCount` read 2. Two things made it invisible here: `listStages` for deals ordered by `sortOrder` with NO tiebreak, so which stage `find` returned was up to Postgres (the LEAD funnel has had a `name` tiebreak all along — a real if small defect on its own, since the board's columns could swap between page loads), and the local duration cache produced a different file order until it was deleted. Both trigger stages — deal and lead — are now MINTED by the file and deleted in `afterAll` (#183), so nothing but this file can pull them, and the spec carries the case as an assertion: a foreign deal moved through the shared funnel must leave `fireCount` at 1. Red-proven by restoring the borrowed stage and watching that line fail with the runner's exact message.

438. **The rule this one is worth stating: a test that asserts a COUNT must own everything that can increment it.** Not "clean up after yourself" — that was already done here, rules and tasks and stages were all deleted — but "do not build on a shared row in the first place". A seeded stage, a seeded cost type, a seeded warehouse: reading one is fine, hanging a counter off one is not, because the thing being counted is then everybody's business. Note also what did NOT fix it: the file already drained pending events in `beforeAll`, which handles the backlog and does nothing at all about a neighbour that is still to run.

439. **The fix for #437 introduced a second failure I could not see, because I was reading the wrong line.** Minting the trigger stages meant deleting them in `afterAll`, and a deal stage still under a deal cannot be dropped (`deals_stage_id_fkey`) — the very law `deleteDealStage` enforces on the screen with its move-first rule.
