So the predicate is ONE exported fragment per module (`leadTextWhere`, `dealTextWhere`) pushed into both, and the integration test asserts that the rows and the totals agree rather than asserting either alone — red-proven by unsharing it from the counts. The second half of the same rule: the filtering is in SQL, never over the fetched array. The open list is capped at 300 rows and the closed one at 20, so a JavaScript filter would search the newest twenty and answer «nothing found» about a database that has it — on the screen somebody opens to find a job. The needles are the global search's own `likeNeedle`/`parseQuery`, so typing a name into the board finds what typing it into ⌘K finds, phone numbers included. Adding the client code to the deal predicate meant `closedDealCounts` needed the clients join it had never had: a shared fragment brings its FROM clause with it.

514. **Four links on that screen were literal strings, and each of them silently threw the filter away.** The mine/all tabs and the «+N · show all» footer were hard-coded `/crm?scope=all` and `` `/crm?arxiv=1${mine ? '' : '&scope=all'}` ``. Ship a filter without rebuilding them and the first tap on any one drops what was typed AND loads four hundred unfiltered closed cards — which a person reads as the filter being broken, not as the link being wrong. `hrefWith(current, patch)` builds every board link from the live params instead. The picker has its own rule: `hodim` is read ONLY for somebody holding `crm.leads.view_all`, and ignored rather than obeyed otherwise, because the funnel's ownership rule is also the global search's and the bot's and a fourth door has to ask the same question. Its options come from `salesManagerOptions()`, never from the loaded rows — a list derived from what is on screen would collapse to one person the moment it was filtered to them, with no way back.

515. **The board's height is a viewport calculation, so anything put above it has to say what it cost.** `h-[calc(100dvh-19rem)]` exists so the page never grows below the board and its scrollbar disappears (#354); a filter row above it pushed the board 130 px down and the page grew the scrollbar back. The row now declares itself — `--board-extra`, one line or two depending on whether the colleague picker is offered — and the boards subtract it. Verified by MEASURING rather than by looking: the baseline was taken with the round's changes stashed, and all three cases (owner's funnel, owner's deals, a sales manager's funnel) came back to the same board bottom they had before, to the pixel. The same measurement found something that was NOT this round's: `/bitimlar` had a document 389 px wide inside a 360 px screen, because its three header buttons carry `shrink-0` — which refuses to narrow, so the box never reaches a width it could wrap at and the buttons simply run off the edge. That is the #400 mechanism, the one that makes mobile Chrome rescale the whole page and every tap land somewhere else. `flex-wrap` without `shrink-0`, plus the cross-board door reduced to its icon on a phone (round 60's precedent), and the header fits on one line again.

516. **A per-person view setting is not a permission, and the difference is who loses something.** Batch 4's last item lets a person choose which lines a board card carries. The obvious-looking design attaches `permission: 'finance.view'` to the deal AMOUNT so the money can be hidden properly — and it is wrong: `/bitimlar` is gated by `canWriteDeal` (crm.leads OR ved.docs OR clients.manage) with no finance gate anywhere, so a permission would TAKE the price away from every seller who reads it today. The owner was asked and answered plainly: the amount stays visible to everybody who can open the board, and the setting is show/hide per person. That is the same refusal round 63 made on the client card — adding a field to a read-only view is an access change wearing a layout change's clothes, and so is removing one. The card's IDENTITY is not on the list at all: a lead's name, a deal's code and title. Half of it is that a card with nothing on it is not a card; the other half is that most of the browser suite finds a board card by its text, so a hideable name would have put every one of those specs one cookie away from failing.

517. **The default has to be TODAY, which is neither «everything» nor «nothing».** `visibleCardFields(specs, null)` returns the set the board already renders, so a person who never opens the menu sees exactly the card that shipped — the property that makes this round safe to deploy on a live system and safe to run the existing e2e against. Cubic metres, the one line the owner asked for that the board never had, is therefore OFF by default: on, it would have changed the card for everybody who never chose anything. And an EMPTY choice has to survive a round trip distinctly from no choice at all: `lead=` in the cookie means every switchable line off, while an absent `lead` means the default set. Both are red-proven — make the default «everything» and the volume test goes red; drop the empty list on parse and the round-trip test goes red.

518. **The cookie, and why not the two obvious alternatives.** It follows the theme (`platform/theme/theme.ts`): a cookie is readable on the SERVER, so the first HTML already carries the right card and nothing rearranges itself once JavaScript boots. A `list_views` row was the tempting reuse — batch 1 already stores a per-user named query per screen — but it costs two queries on every render of the sales team's home board plus a redirect on a bare visit, which is round 45's whole lesson in reverse; and it would leave CONFIGURATION in the database for the next Playwright spec to inherit (#183), which is worse than data. `localStorage` is free and precedented here (the collapsed sidebar) but cannot be server-rendered, so the card would flash the old shape on every load. The cost that IS paid, stated rather than hidden: a cookie is per browser, so the same person's phone and desktop can differ — true of the theme and the sidebar too. The parser treats the value as what it is, a string a person can edit: an unknown board, a missing `=`, a truncated tail and an empty string all return something sane rather than throwing on a screen. The menu is a native `<details>` positioned absolute, so the CLOSED menu costs the board no height — which matters because round 65 had just made the board's height a viewport calculation that everything above it must pay for, and an e2e now measures that the board does not move when the menu opens.

519. **The sentences typed twenty times a day: two lists, one ownership column, and the filling happens where the client is known.** Batch 5's headline item. A canned reply is a `reply_templates` row whose `user_id` is either a person (mine, nobody else sees it) or NULL (the company's, offered to everybody) — `list_views`'s column exactly, for the same reason: deciding what every colleague is offered is a larger power than keeping a note for yourself, so publishing asks `admin.settings.manage` and no new permission code was minted (#170). The checkbox on the form is a REQUEST; the service checks the permission again, and editing or deleting somebody else's row is a refusal rather than a mistake to correct. Both halves red-proven. The placeholders are the design's real decision: `{ism}` and `{kod}` are filled on the SERVER, at the moment a composer is rendered for a known client — `templatesFor(userId, client)` — so the stored text stays a template and the browser is never told a customer's name in order to write a greeting. The dock's `/api/dock/thread` fills them against the id it RESOLVED (round 32's phone-sibling rule), so both composers offer the same words. A placeholder the caller said nothing about is left ALONE, which is not the same as one whose value is empty: «I have no name for this client» blanks itself, «I was not asked about names» must not quietly delete a word out of the middle of a message on its way to a customer. And the picker INSERTS rather than replaces — half a typed sentence plus a template means «and this too», which is #377, #419 and #463 wearing a fourth hat.

520. **A translated string that SHOWS a placeholder has to escape it, and the i18n tripwire structurally cannot see that.** The hint under the templates screen reads «write `{ism}` for the client's name» — and next-intl parses `{ism}` as an ICU argument, finds none supplied, and renders the KEY: `crm.templateHint` on screen, in all four locales. `tests/unit/i18n-keys.test.ts` was green throughout, because the key exists in every bundle; existence is what it checks and validity is a different question. The escape is ICU's own: `'{'ism'}'`. Caught by the browser spec that reads the hint, which is the second time this programme has proved that a screen nobody looked at is a screen nobody checked — and the reason every new screen here gets a 360×800 screenshot before it is called done.

521. **`.input` also carries `min-h-12`, so a bare `min-h-24` does nothing — the fourth costume of #419.** The template body wants to be six lines tall; `className="input min-h-24"` came back three, because `.input` is declared after `@tailwind utilities` and wins at equal specificity, exactly as its `w-full` does. The house idiom is `h-28` (a height, since `min-height` is only a floor and 3 rem is below the one asked for) or `!min-h-…`. `tests/unit/style-cascade.test.ts` now says so as a grep-shaped rule beside the width one, exempting `min-h-9`/`min-h-0` — those sit beside `max-h-…` on the autogrowing composers, where losing to `min-h-12` makes a box taller rather than unusable. Red-proven by putting the bare class back.

522. **A new control in a fixed row is a measurement, not a judgement — twice over.** Round 60's rule applied again: adding the ⚡ to the reply composer took the typing box from **128 px to 76 px** at 360 px, about eight characters, with no overflow and no test red. The word «Yuborish» is 62 px of that row and the button is the one control whose meaning survives as an icon, so it becomes ➤ below `sm` and keeps its word from there up (`aria-label` carries the name either way) — measured back to **138 px**, wider than before the picker existed. The second measurement was the panel: `absolute … right-0` hung off a button that sits ~110 px from the left edge puts a 256 px panel at −144 px, with half of it unreachable. That is #471's mistake and #491 repeated it; the rule is that a popover anchors where the ROOM is, and here the room is to the right (`left-0`, `max-w-[calc(100vw-7rem)]`, verified at 85…333 px inside a 360 px screen).

523. **A red proof that turns a REFUSAL into a success leaves the row it should have refused.** Half of this round's integration file asserts that something is forbidden, and red-proving one (#166) means running the file with the guard stripped — at which moment the call succeeds, the id is never recorded because the test expected a rejection, and `afterAll`'s delete-by-collected-ids misses it. Three template rows survived into the database that way, and the next browser spec found them: a locator that had been unique matched two. So a file whose subject is refusals sweeps by a run-scoped TITLE, not by the ids it managed to collect — and every title it writes, including the one a stripped guard would rename a row to, carries the run's suffix. Same family as #183: what a test leaves behind is the next test's input, and configuration is worse than data.

526. **The deals board asked its deviation question one deal at a time — the owner's «qotyabti», measured.** Round 45's method rerun on his data after his report that the CRM freezes, «ayniqsa bitim bilan»: /bitimlar was issuing ~120 statements per render, of which nearly all were `dealsNeedingAttention` calling `dealReality(id)` in a sequential loop — two aggregate queries per open deal plus a `findFirst` re-fetch of each priced deal for the one quote column `listDeals` did not return. Linear in his deal count, serial in its awaits, and blocking the whole board behind `Promise.all`: the cost grows with exactly the thing he is using more of. Now `dealRealitiesFor(dealIds)` answers for the whole list in ONE pair of `GROUP BY deal_id` aggregates (a deal with no receipts is absent from the rows and gets the zero object, which is what the per-deal query returned for it anyway), `listDeals` carries `quotedWeightKg` so nothing re-fetches a row it already holds, and the client card's deals panel — the same loop in a second costume, whose own comment argued «small N per client» — rides the same function. Measured: /bitimlar 120 → **17 statements**; the deal suites untouched and green. The rule this pins is #432's, restated for lists: **a per-row aggregate on a list screen must be one grouped query, because a list's length is the business growing.**

527. **The stock table was the real phone freeze: ~450 rows × ~22 nodes ≈ 10,000 DOM nodes, 3.3 s of main-thread time at a phone's speed.** The server was never the problem — 165 ms, 28 queries — and no test was red, because every locator found its row; only a CPU-throttled measurement (4×, a warehouse phone) showed `domInteractive` at **4.8 s** on the screen the warehouse lives in. The fix pages the RENDER, not the fetch: the query still brings the whole filtered set (≤500), `sortRows` still orders all of it, the Σ header and the XLSX export still describe everything the filter matched — only the `<tbody>` shows 120 rows, with prev/next as plain LINKS so paging walks with the back button. `page` joined `CONTROL_PARAMS`, because a saved view carrying `page=3` would greet its owner with the middle of a list. Measured after: **989 ms / 491 ms of long tasks / 2,696 nodes**. Deliberately NOT `content-visibility: auto` — it is ignored on table-internal boxes, so it would have looked like a fix and measured like nothing; and deliberately not SQL-side LIMIT/OFFSET, because the sort runs in JS over derived columns and paging the fetch would have sorted each page separately — the same lie #513 forbids, in pagination's clothes.

524. **An unordered read must never be indexed into — CI found one, and a four-lens sweep found four more.** `inline-edit.integration.test.ts` writes three `update` audit rows for one lead, reads them back with no `ORDER BY` and asserts on `rows.at(-1)`; the runner handed back the `company` row and round 67 went red on a file it had not touched. Postgres guarantees nothing about order without `ORDER BY`, and the usual excuse — «an append-only table comes back in insertion order» — is false here: `audit_log` takes no DELETEs but it takes plenty of ROLLED-BACK inserts, whose dead tuples autovacuum frees for a later row to land in. Measured rather than argued, on the local database: of the 1,166 entities holding more than one audit row, **210 already come back in an order that is not their insertion order**. This lead was simply one of the other 956, for four rounds. Two things this teaches about the local suite: it runs against a long-lived database whose physical layout is nothing like CI's freshly created one, and a test that reads back what it wrote is not automatically deterministic. A sweep over `src/` and `tests/` for the same shape (unordered read → positional access) raised seven candidates and confirmed four by adversarial verify. **Login** (#525). **`clientsForChat`** — a broker chat may hold several clients and `chatLocale` takes the FIRST with a language on file, so a chat whose clients had chosen different languages was answered in whichever one Postgres returned first, changing between two presses; ordered by the oldest link, the client the chat was opened for. Cosmetic — everything that could show wrong data (cargo, money, photo authorisation) consumes that list as a SET — but a stable arbitrary winner beats an unstable one. **`m3-planning`'s `makeLot`** — its callers read the array positionally («the planned ones» are `slice(0, 3)`, «boxes 3..6» are `slice(2)`) and what makes that true is that `recordVerdict` reserves the LOWEST `seq_in_lot`; a differently-ordered read would plan one box and scan another, and the gate would answer `not_on_plan` about a perfectly planned lot. Refuted and worth recording: the client-history `findFirst` (client_code is uniquely indexed and `[A-Z0-9]{2,10}` on every write path), `staffByPhone` (the JS predicate matches on `phone`, which is `notNull().unique()`), and the plan's crate-conflict read (multi-row, but consumed as a set — `taken.length` decides and `taken[0].code` only names one in the refusal).

525. **The OR of two unique columns is not unique, and the login box is made of one.** `loginAction` read `or(eq(phone, identifier), eq(username, identifier))` with `limit(1)` and no order, so when one row answers to a string by phone and a DIFFERENT row answers to it by username, the planner decided whose password hash got checked. It is fail-closed — a session is only ever created for the row whose hash validated — so the symptom is not somebody landing in the wrong account, it is a colleague refused with the right password, at random, with nothing on the screen or in the log to explain it. Reachable rather than theoretical: the admin form takes any string up to 50 characters as a username, phone-shaped included, and its duplicate check compares phones alone. Now `findUserByIdentifier` asks twice and states the precedence instead of inheriting it — **the phone wins**, because every account here is minted with one and no import has ever written a username. THE TEST LESSON, learned the hard way in the same hour: the first version of the red proof inserted the phone holder FIRST, and it passed with the defect still in place, because the broken read happened to reach that row first. A non-deterministic defect does not fail on demand — the fixture has to make the broken version deterministic, so the username holder is inserted first and the assertion is repeated ten times.

528. **The money audit, and what «is the bookkeeping correct» turned out to mean.** The owner asked in one sentence; the answer was a four-lens adversarial workflow over the four money subsystems (client ledger, partner ledger, costs/tannarx, reports), every candidate verified by an agent instructed to REFUTE it. Twenty-one candidates, twenty confirmed, one refuted — and the twenty clustered into ~15 distinct defects: five where a stored number or a gate was wrong, five where a report lied about correct storage, five edge-cases. The striking pattern: almost every live one was a PAIR RULE broken in exactly one direction. Cost→charge derivation existed, charge-void→cost did not (#529); the deferral netting existed, the column it joins on could never be written (#531); the settlement's paired void existed (#425), the cost-derived charge's did not. A rule enforced in one direction is a rule the next writer assumes holds in both — the audit's real lesson is that every "A implies B" in money code needs its converse stated, implemented, or explicitly refused in writing.

529. **Voiding a derived charge must take the payer off its cost, or the void undoes itself.** `voidPartnerTx` handled the settlement pair and ignored `costEntryId`/`expenseId`: the cost kept its `partner_id`, `chargeForCost`'s idempotence check sees only LIVE charges, and the next recompute — any FX save in the currency, any batch departure — re-derived the charge the accountant had just cancelled, under the ORIGINAL enterer's name in the audit log. Now the void clears the source fact's payer in the same transaction and the audit row names the unlinked cost. The drift half of the same pair: a recompute that re-prices a cost (the #427 repair path) now re-prices the LIVE derived charge too, audited as `cost_entry_reprice` — before, the P&L read the new number while the firm was settled for the old one. And `voidCostEntry` became ONE transaction instead of four autocommitted statements: a crash between the entry's void and the charge's used to leave a live debt whose backing cost was gone, unrepairable because re-voiding refuses `already_voided` and every sweep skips voided entries.

530. **Money first at the receipt void — the batch-cancel rule (#288) applied to the door that never had it.** `voidReceipt` voided the receipt and its boxes and left the costs standing: still in the P&L's direct costs, still allocated (and RE-allocated by any sweep, because the engine's receipt scope had no box-status filter) onto the void boxes, and a partner-named one still the firm's live debt for cargo that officially never existed. It now refuses with `receipt_has_costs` until finance voids the money — a cascade was considered and rejected, because a warehouse button must not be able to void money. Two adjacent fixes rode along: the allocation engine's receipt scope now excludes `void` boxes (a lot-edit shrink's phantom boxes kept their shares, which the batch pricing screen could not see — the owner priced trips below what was actually paid while profit-by-client counted the full amount), and the shrink triggers the recompute immediately rather than waiting for the next FX save. The void form also learned to SAY its refusals — both of them were silent, and a silent refusal reads as a broken button.

531. **A column only the service could write is a feature only the tests have.** #251's deferral netting joins payments to the deferred deal by `client_transactions.deal_id` — and the payment form never had a deal input, the action never parsed one, so on real data every payment landed NULL and the netting branch was dead code: a paid-off deferral went on excusing unrelated debt at the handover gate, the exact hole #251 was sold as closing. The pinning test passed throughout because it hand-crafted the row through the service — proving the service, not the system. Now the ledger form offers the client's open deals on a payment, the action parses `dealId`, the service refuses a deal that is not the client's (a select's value is a forged post until checked), and `tests/unit/payment-deal-wire.test.ts` pins BOTH halves of the wire source-shape, so the form and the parse cannot drift apart silently again. THE RULE, third appearance (m9x's refusal, the cabinet's serve-don't-race): an end-to-end feature is proven at the outermost layer a test can reach — a service-level test of a form-fed path proves nothing about the form.

532. **The report fixes, each a one-direction disagreement between two screens on the same hub.** (a) The round-29 grid's per-prixod customs landed in NO batch's profit row — receipt-scope, `batch_id` NULL — while the monthly P&L counted them by cost_date: the batch view overstated every truck's margin by exactly what was typed on its own xarajatlar page. The grid now STAMPS its entries with the batch (attribution, not scope — allocation still spreads over the receipt's boxes), so `profitByBatch` and «what this trip added» both see them; historical grid entries stay unattributed, stated. (b) `profitByClient` was keyed on revenue rows, so a client whose costs landed in a period whose price was agreed the month after vanished entirely — the union of both sides now, a cost-only client being a red row, which is the point of looking. (c) `companyBalance` counted only ACTIVE cash boxes: retiring a till with money in it deleted that money from the Balans — #428's partner lesson on the accounts side; a retired box with a non-zero balance now stays on the sheet, flagged ⚠. (d) The payments-register XLSX printed a settlement's kassa cell blank — indistinguishable from unplaced money — where its own screen says «→ firma»; and the cash-flow export printed `partnerIn`/`partnerOut` as raw keys. (e) «Shu reysgacha» read the FUTURE: membership is for-ever, so re-opening an internal leg's money screen after the export departed showed the export's customs in the internal leg's cost column, every last-month leg loss-making by money spent after it landed — `notLaterLeg` bounds both batch money reads to entries of this batch, no batch, or batches departed earlier.

533. **Three small gates that were comments instead of code.** A payment/expense/partner row naming a cash box in another currency was accepted — the till sums NATIVE amounts, so 500 USD in a som till read as 500 som and ~$500 vanished from the Balans while the drawer count could never reconcile; all three doors now refuse `account_currency_mismatch`, named on the forms. `generateRecurring`'s idempotence slot was (category, date, employee) — two warehouse rents in one category collided, the second «already posted» every month for ever with the home counter (which mirrors the predicate, and now mirrors the fix) saying nothing was due; the WAREHOUSE joined the slot. And `paymentsRegister` totalled a silently-truncated 2,000-row slice — the total is now aggregated over the whole period in SQL, and both the screen and the XLSX say «newest N of M» when the cap bites (the no-silent-caps rule). The grid's «already entered» hint stopped wearing an empty cell's face over unconverted money: «≈ $0 ⚠», because a hint that exists to prevent blind double entry must not itself be blind.

534. **The call round records on the phone, ships from the phone, and stores only the client book — on both ends.** Android hands no third-party app the call audio (since 10), and pretending otherwise is how call-recorder apps end up demanding accessibility hacks that die on every OS update. So the design accepts the platform: the PHONE's own recorder (Samsung, Xiaomi, Huawei, Vivo, Oppo all ship one; switching it on is a checklist step the app cannot do or verify — a manual tick like the auto-start one) writes the file, and `apps/calls-android` only reads the call REGISTER and picks the files up. The privacy line is the tg-import rule made structural twice over: `call_logs.client_id NOT NULL` on the server, and the app writes a local row ONLY for a call the server answered `matched: true` — an unmatched number is looked at once, answered about once, and stored NOWHERE, so a hodim's personal calls exist in no table on either end and their recordings never leave the phone. The owner's boundary «ornatilgan kundan boshlab» is a stored floor, not a convention: the 24 h re-read overlap (Android stamps a row with the call's START but writes it at the END, so a long call surfaces behind the watermark) is clamped to never cross the install-day midnight. iPhone is stated impossible (iOS exposes neither the log nor recordings) and stays in the plan by his word, not in the code.

535. **A call is read by the eyes that read the chats — and the audio URL answers to the same rule as the panel.** The owner's answer was «xuddi telegram kabi», so `callsFor(clientId, viewer)` takes the round-21 `TgViewer` and the round-33 supervision set verbatim: the taker sees their own calls, super_admin / admin / `ved.docs` holders see everything, and the `CallsPanel` carries `canReadTg` WITH it because the deal card is open to the customs manager (#299's lesson — a panel meant for any card must be safe on any card). Deliberately NOT the lenta: round 21 split the lenta as the company-wide record, and these rows are viewer-scoped, so they live beside the Telegram thread, not inside the shared feed. The `call_log` branch of the attachment authz ENFORCES from birth — unlike the legacy types still in log-only — because there is no legacy: no old bookmark can break, and a recording of a customer's voice is exactly what #383 was about. A copied `/api/attachments/<id>` link therefore cannot out-read the panel that showed it.

536. **The pairing is the driver contract with one inversion: the code binds a PERSON, so only that person can mint it.** `createCallDevice` takes no user id — the actor is the device's owner by construction, because a pair code is a signature: whatever the phone sends afterwards lands under that name, and an admin minting codes for colleagues would be signing their names. The rest is the proven driver-app shape reused deliberately: single-use display-alphabet code (no 0/O/1/I), sha256 token hash, revocation KEEPS the hash so the token can be answered **410** — the one status that stops a retry loop (#289) — and the app forgets everything on hearing it. Idempotence is heal-by-replay on both ends: the phone re-sends its recent register every cycle (a dead zone repeats a batch instead of losing it), the server's `(device, phone, started_at)` unique makes the replay a no-op, and the app counts a call as «sent» only when its local INSERT was genuinely new — the first draft counted every replay and would have inflated the status screen by ~96× a day.

537. **Audio travels second, matches conservatively, and is claimed exactly once.** The upload route accepts bytes only for a call the SERVER already knows, found device-scoped — a token cannot hang audio on a colleague's call, and an unmatched call (never stored) has nothing to hang audio on, so the storage can never hold a recording the database cannot name. Content is sniffed by magic (AMR/OGG/ID3/MP3-frame/RIFF/ftyp), never by filename; 25 MB cap on both ends. The claim is `SET attachment_id WHERE attachment_id IS NULL` — first write wins, and BOTH the already-claimed and the lost-race answer are `{ok, already:true}`, because the one answer that stops a phone re-sending megabytes for ever is «done». On the phone, matching is deliberately narrow: only the vendors' call-recording folders plus a MediaStore net for paths saying «call» — a generic recordings folder is NOT scanned, because a voice memo taped during the call window must never be uploadable by coincidence; the number's digits in the filename beat any bare time match; a file that has not appeared within 48 h never will and the row expires instead of scanning for ever.

538. **The ingest reads the client book once, in a stated order — #432 and #524 arriving together at a new door.** The first draft called `activeClientsByPhone` per call: 200 calls × a full active-client load, the deals-board shape rebuilt three rounds after it was measured away. And that helper has NO ORDER BY, so a person holding several GS codes on one number could land the same call on a different sibling code between two cycles — round 67b's unordered-first-match, this time scattering one conversation across sibling cards. Now the batch loads the book once and picks first-match over `createdAt, clientCode` — the OLDEST code wins, `clientsForChat`'s rule restated for phones. The APK itself is handed out AUTHED from /profile beside the pairing code — the driver's public `/driver` page and QR exist because a driver has no login; a staff member does, and copying the public door would have been copying a hole.

539. **A device is a pairing, not a phone — the first real phone proved it within the hour.** The calls dedup key was `(device, phone, started_at)`, and the owner did what every first user does: paired, revoked, paired again. The re-pair minted a new device id, the app re-read its register from the install-day floor, and every call of the day landed a SECOND time under the new id — four rows for two calls, on production, on day one. The fact was always the PERSON's call: migration 0061 rekeys the dedup to `(user, phone, started_at)` (cleaning existing duplicates — an audio-carrying row beats one without, then the earliest write), and `findCallForAudio` scopes by USER for the same reason: the call row keeps the FIRST pairing's device id, so a device-scoped find would 404 the new pairing's recording for ever, and the app — told 404 is final, correctly, to stop megabyte retries — would give the file up. The same report carried the stated deferral arriving on schedule: his call landed on the person's OLDEST code (ingest's rule) while the card he read belonged to a newer one, so `callsForCard` now widens the CARD's read to phone-siblings (`phonesOverlap`, the panel names the sibling code on the row) while the DATA stays where it landed and `callsFor` stays narrow — round 32's split between where a thing lives and where it is shown, restated for calls.

540. **An `optional` column is a rule about a narrow TABLE, and it had been allowed to decide what is in a FILE.** The owner: the client book's spreadsheet had lost its phone numbers. Round 57 handed the export the screen's own resolver, `visibleColumns`, whose no-view branch drops `optional` columns — a flag that exists so a 360 px table stays readable. A spreadsheet is read at a desk. The two questions are now two functions: `exportColumns` carries the chosen view when there is one and everything the person MAY SEE when there is not, and `visibleColumns` still answers for the screen. The permission filter is identical in both branches and asserted beside the relaxation, because that is the rule that must not bend — «an export that shows a column the page hides is a leak with a filename» is about what somebody may see, never about what makes a table tidy. This mattered more than a missing column usually would: that file is how he corrects the client book, and the outstanding chore list on it is nameless clients and truncated phones. Red-proven, and a source-shape tripwire says the route must ask the export question — the original defect was calling the right-looking helper, which no behaviour test can see and the file has no header test of its own.

541. **The name on a card is its title, and a tap on a title must not become an input.** The owner: «lead kartochkasida nomni ustiga bosib o'zgartirish kirgazilgan buni umuman olib tashla». Round 61 made four rail facts inline-editable and the name was one of them — while the same name is also the card's `<h1>`, directly above the rail. So it is removed as a control and kept as a fact: nothing is hidden, and renaming goes back through the ✏️ form, where changing what a record is CALLED is a deliberate act rather than a mis-tap. Removed from `INLINE_LEAD_FIELDS` as well as from the JSX, which is the difference between a removed feature and a hidden one — a control taken off a screen while the action still accepts the field is reachable by anything that can POST. Phone, company and note keep their inline edit: those are the corrections a salesperson makes with a phone against their ear, and he named the name.

542. **A tick is not board state, and treating it as board state cost 400 ms a press.** The owner: «umumiy belgilash … juda qotib ishlayabti, bosganda qotib qolyabti». Measured on a clone of his own data before touching anything, which is what round 45 bought: his funnel renders 298 open leads, and BOTH board shapes are mounted at once — `md:hidden` / `hidden md:block` is CSS, not a condition — so 596 card subtrees are live. The selection was `useState<Set<string>>` in each board's parent, so every tick minted a new Set, re-rendered the parent, and re-rendered all 596 cards to flip one checkbox: **135–400 ms of frozen main thread per tick** at a 4× CPU throttle, against a two-frame measurement floor of 33 ms. Ten cards is three seconds of dead screen, on the phone of somebody working a funnel.

543. **So the ticks moved out of React and into a store with per-id subscriptions.** `useSelection()` keeps the set in a ref and notifies only the listeners for the ids that changed: a checkbox subscribes to its OWN id via `useSyncExternalStore` (the pattern `components/composer.ts` already uses, and the supported way to read outside state without tearing), the bulk bar subscribes to the count, and the board is handed an object whose identity never changes — so a tick does not reach it at all. The bar reads the ids at PRESS time rather than holding them, which is why the parent needs no state either. Same measurement after: **35–66 ms**, i.e. at the floor. Nothing about the markup changed, so the bulk e2e passed untouched — which is exactly why the guard is a source-shape tripwire (`tests/unit/board-selection.test.ts`: neither board may hold `useState<Set<string>>`, the board may not take a `Set`, the bar must read count and ids from the store). Both versions WORK; the difference is only visible on a warehouse phone, and the lesson generalises: **when one screen holds hundreds of live nodes, any per-item state belongs outside the component that renders them.**

544. **What the filter question needed before any code: numbers, and which of the asked-for fields exist at all.** He asked whether the boards could filter by price, client, date, kg, kub, source and comment «and whatever else the CRM holds», and whether that would work without freezing. Measured rather than guessed: 383 leads and 1,692 clients in his data, and a four-condition filter with a text search and a join over it runs in **0.99 ms**. The database is not the question and will not be for years. The question is the SCREEN, and the answer is the round above: the employee filter already works the right way (a `hodim` parameter in the address bar → one server query → a fresh page), so more filters cost the same and freeze nothing; it is only client-held state over hundreds of nodes that freezes. The finding worth telling him rather than quietly working around: **price, kg and kub exist on a DEAL and not on a lead** — a lead has no money on it by design — so the filter set cannot be the same on both boards, and that is a decision about his process, not a technical detail.

545. **The lead took money after all, because the owner named the moment it exists.** #108 kept price off a lead on purpose, and round 70's study restated it (#544). His answer moved the boundary: «tovarga hisoblatish bosqichidan keyin bizning serviceimiz narxi yozilishi kerak, va shu narx yutildimi yo'qmi etapiga o'tadi» — the SERVICE price exists as soon as hisoblatish has answered, before any deal, and the funnel's whole point is watching whether that number wins or loses. So migration 0062 adds `quoted_amount/currency/volume_m3/weight_kg` to `leads` — additive, nullable, the deal's own quote block one table earlier. Written through one `quoteValues` helper (`toFixed(2)`/`toFixed(3)`) so the stored string equals what postgres returns and `diffFields` never sees a phantom change (#503's lesson, one table over; the integration test re-saves the same numbers and asserts NO audit row). The currency defaults to USD only when a price is present — an unpriced lead stays all-NULL rather than carrying a currency for nothing. The card SHOWS the price (a filterable number nobody can see is a filter over invisible ink), which deliberately breaks the card-fields «null = yesterday's card» stillness (#517) once, stated in the test that guards it. And a won lead's «Bitim ochish» carries the quote into the deal form — but only when the lead's client matches the preset client, because a URL param is a forged post (#514) and `?lead=` is a URL param.

546. **A filter the counts cannot hear is a lie in the footer, so the predicate ships as ONE function per module.** The panel (source / dates / price / kub / kg / lenta) exists twice — `leadBoardWhere` and `dealBoardWhere` — and each is consumed by the rows AND by `closedLeadCounts`/`closedDealCounts`, which is #513 applied at design time instead of as a repair: unshare it and the closed column filters its cards while its «+N · hammasi» footer counts the whole year (red-proven exactly there). Everything out of the address bar passes `readBoardFilters` before SQL: a range is a finite non-negative number (decimal comma accepted — half the office types «1,5»), a date is `YYYY-MM-DD` or nothing, `manba` must look like a uuid. The lenta search is an EXISTS over `crm_activities` plus the record's own note — and deliberately NOT `tg_messages`: the lenta is the shared record, a Telegram thread is scoped to the manager whose account holds it (#383), and a board filter must not become a door through that fence. Because a filter is just query-string, every combination is savable as a round-57 view with zero new code — which is what the owner's «a» answer asked for.

547. **The e2e said «unpressable», the fix made it pressable, and only the screenshot said «still wrong».** The panel was first anchored under the filter row (`absolute top-full`) with `max-h-[65dvh]` — but 65dvh measures the viewport, not the space below the anchor, so on a 360×800 phone the panel ran past the bottom edge: the apply button sat below the fold AND under the fixed z-30 tab bar. Playwright refused the click (the tab bar intercepts), z-40 made the click land — the suite went green — and the 360×800 screenshot still showed a panel whose bottom third needs the PAGE scrolled to reach. Green plus wrong: Playwright scrolls anything into view; a thumb does not. So on phones the panel is a FIXED bottom sheet (`fixed inset-x-2 bottom-16`, the dock's shape) with the apply button resting just above the tab bar, and from `md` up — where the tab bar does not exist — it anchors to the row as a dropdown again. The rule this earns, third time paid for (#419, #400): a mobile layout claim is settled by a screenshot at 360 px, and an e2e that passes after a reachability fix proves clickability for a ROBOT, not reachability for a hand.

548. **The bulk bar is a fixed overlay, and a fixed overlay over a scroll container steals its last card.** Found by the merge-verification e2e run, not by either branch's own suite: once one card is ticked, the bulk bar (fixed, bottom, z-40) floats over the column's bottom edge — and a kanban column scrolls INTERNALLY, so the last visible card's checkbox can sit under the bar at maximum scroll, unreachable for a thumb and intercepted for Playwright. It surfaced now because round 71's ViewBar above the board made the columns shorter, moving the second test lead into the covered zone — the defect itself shipped with the bar in round 59. The fix is spare scroll, not logic: `pb-24` inside both column scrollers, so the column can always scroll its last card clear of the bar. Unconditional on purpose — ticks deliberately never re-render the board (#543 on this branch), so «pad only while selecting» would reintroduce the render the selection store exists to avoid.

549. **On scoped storage the File API answers «nothing» without saying no — read recordings through MediaStore or not at all.** v1.0's finder scanned the vendor folders with `listFiles()` and vetted every MediaStore hit with `File.isFile` — and on the owner's Android-13 Samsung both answered emptily while the files sat plainly in `Recordings/Call`: `READ_MEDIA_AUDIO` opens other apps' audio THROUGH the resolver, not through paths, and the FUSE view neither lists nor stats what it will happily stream. Zero uploads, no error anywhere — the server was proven innocent by replaying the APK's exact wire shape locally (pair → log → octet-stream multipart with the Samsung filename), which passed end-to-end on the first try. v1.1 carries a candidate as a content URI and streams it (`resolver.openInputStream`), keeps the direct scan only for pre-scoped-storage Android, and tightens the privacy fence to survive the wider net: a file counts as a call recording only when its FOLDER says «call» or its NAME carries the number's tail — never on time coincidence alone. The lesson that cost the day (#419's cousin, phone-side): the audio pass was SILENT — found/sent/refused counters now sit on the app's own screen, because a pipeline whose failure mode is «nothing happened, nothing said» cannot be debugged from a chat.

550. **The door widened to the funnel, at the owner's word — and the chat's one-target refusal stopped blanking a read-only log.** His third report of the evening was a REQUEST, not a bug: «nomer yozilib lead ochildimi, usha telefondan klient tel qilganda zapislari yozilib qolsin» — a prospect's calls are company business before the person has a code. Migration 0063 makes `call_logs.client_id` nullable, adds `lead_id`, and the CHECK (client OR lead, never neither) keeps the privacy rule structural in its widened form: the number must belong to the client book or an OPEN lead; anything else is still looked at once and stored nowhere. Matching order is stated: the client book first (oldest code), else the NEWEST open lead — the card being worked. `convertLead` re-keys the prospect's calls onto the minted code in the same breath (the pair rule, #528: a door that lets data in must have the door that carries it forward), keeping `lead_id` so the lead's own history never goes blank. And the lead CARD stopped asking `conversationClientForLead`: that resolver refuses two sibling codes because a REPLY needs one target — the right rule for a chat and the wrong one for a log, which is exactly why his lead card sat empty while the client card played the same recording. `callsForLeadCard` unions the lead's kept calls with every phone-sibling client's, each row naming its code.

551. **The board chrome collapsed to one row, because the owner refused the stack outright.** «Qidiruv va hodimlar bo'yicha filterni ham o'zing qilganni ichiga tiq … urg'u kanban view'ga berilsin … hamma buttonlarni boshqattdan o'ylab chiq» — and the measurement agreed with him: at 360×800 the first card sat ~1,100 px down, under a page header, a scope-tabs row, a view-chips row and a two-row filter card. Round 72 replaces all of it, on both boards, with ONE toolbar row — title, inline search (from `md` only), the door to the other board, the filter door with a count badge, a views button, a ⋯ menu, and the one primary «+» — plus a chips row when something is filtering. The search box, the Meniki/Hammasi choice and the colleague picker LIVE IN the filter panel now, exactly as he asked; the panel posts ONE GET form that re-posts all of them (#171). Three judge lenses reviewed the design before the build and changed it: desktop keeps a visible search input in the toolbar (the fold saves no height there while search is the most-pressed control — a phone keeps it in the panel, as asked); the cross-board door stays a VISIBLE icon, not a ⋯ entry (daily navigation does not belong behind an overflow, and m9p presses it directly); and scope=all shows as a removable chip, because an invisible widening is the hidden-filter confusion the chips exist to prevent. First card now at ~290 px — the board owns the screen.

552. **Two view UIs on purpose, one testid vocabulary as a contract, and one stated fold-depth exception.** The lists (/admin/clients, /stock, /o) KEEP `ViewBar` — a table scrolls, a chips row costs nothing there — while the boards get `ViewsMenu`, the same `list_views` engine behind one button whose label wears the active view's name. Both render the same testids (view-menu / view-chip / view-name / view-save / view-delete), which is now a CONTRACT: m9za proves the bar, m9zh proves the menu, and a rename in either breaks the other's spec. And the card-fields chooser inside the ⋯ menu is the suite's one fold-inside-a-fold: legal only because the ⋯ panel is absolutely positioned and the inline form expands IN FLOW inside it, so the closed board still costs nothing — written down here because the next author will otherwise read it as a precedent.

553. **The geometry fence — the first test that can read --board-extra — caught its first unpaid row within one run.** The board's height bases were re-derived (19→12 rem mobile, 17.5→10 rem desktop) and each page now states its own chrome: /bitimlar one value (toolbar + chips-when-on + attention-fold-when-on, the attention section itself folded to one line — eight warning cards were pushing the board below the fold); /crm pays for the SubNav too, which costs MORE on a phone than a desktop, hence literal responsive `[--board-extra:…] max-md:[--board-extra:…]` classes (Tailwind compiles only what it can see). No test had ever read the variable — a wrong constant was invisible to everything — so m9zh now measures the real thing: board bottom within [-2, +48] px of the tab bar, document never wider than 360. Its first full-suite run went red at −28 px: the scope chip had been added to the chips row after the review but not to `chipsOn`, so the row rendered unpaid. The fence caught in minutes what round 71 needed a screenshot to see.

554. **Small repairs the judges billed before shipping:** the panel's clear link now carries `scope=all` through (#171 — «clear» drops the FILTERS, not whose work a supervisor's board shows; for a mine-scope board it lands on the bare address, which for a default-view holder means «back to my default view», stated not accidental); the chips row is ONE sideways-scrolling line (SubNav's shape) because a wrapped second line would eat the board's bottom exactly when the owner filters hardest; the kimniki radios restyle themselves via `peer-checked` (a GET form gives no feedback until submit, so the tick itself must); and the three native `<details>` on one toolbar corner close each other through `PopoverRow`'s capture-phase toggle listener — native folds do not coordinate, and two stacked panels on one anchor read as breakage. The m9zg radio press goes through the visible styled label, not a force-check of the sr-only input: prove a thumb reaches the control, not that the DOM contains it (#547's rule).

555. **The tab strip left the funnel, because navigation must not stand on the board.** «Tepada bir qator bo'lib zvonki, nastroyka — menga yoqmayabti, yo'qot, halaqit qilmaydigan joyga o'tkaz.» The CRM SubNav now renders only inside a `(pages)` route group — today / dormant / people / settings, the screens it actually names, where a second row costs nothing because they scroll — and the funnel carries those doors in its ⋯ menu instead. The URLs did not move (a route group is invisible in the address bar), the gates stayed in the section layout, and /crm's --board-extra fell back to the deal board's constant. The lesson from round 72 holds: on a screen whose height is a viewport calculation, every row above the board must justify itself to the OWNER, not to the information architecture.

556. **Summa · kub · kg is one fact wearing one switch.** «Kartalarning ustiga kubi kg summasi ko'rinsin» — so both boards' cards print the composite line (present parts joined by « · ») under the existing money switch, and the deal board's separate opt-in `volume` checkbox RETIRED with it: a menu offering a switch for a line that always renders would be lying about what it controls. A cookie still naming `volume` is dropped, not honoured — a choice outlives a field (#517's parser rule doing its job). The card-fields default test and the m9zh switch test were rewritten to assert the absence deliberately.

557. **The comment boxes became composers — a shell that talks, not a form that files.** «Lenta, komment beradigan inputlarga e'tibor ber, UI/UX dizaynini o'zgartir.» One shape for both: a rounded shell that carries the focus ring for the whole group (`focus-within`), the textarea borderless inside it (`MentionTextarea` grew a `bare` variant), and the actions on a footer row where a messenger keeps them — 📎 left, send right. The contact log's KIND dropdown became four peer-checked chips (📞 💬 🤝 📝): the dropdown hid the answer behind a tap, and a GET-adjacent form must restyle on the tick itself. Names, actions and testids all unchanged — m8's activity flow passed untouched. Two things only a screenshot caught: the two-line placeholder clipped in a min-h-10 box (now min-h-14), and `border-brand-400` — a scale step that does not exist — was refused by the tokens tripwire before it could ship as silent nothing.

558. **Tap-to-edit came out of the Frappe study, shipped across three cards, and was removed whole — the second removal is the one that states the rule.** The owner took the lead's NAME off it first (round 70, «nomni ustiga bosib o'zgartirish … umuman olib tashla»), and then all of it: «contactlarni ustiga bosib o'zgartirish featureni qayerda qoygan bo'lsang hammasini olib tashla». Rounds 61-63 had reasoned that a fact should be correctable where it is read; the owner's reasoning is simpler and beats it on his own screens — a card is READ far more often than it is corrected, and a value that turns into an input under a thumb is a value nobody can read safely. What stays is the half that was genuinely missing before round 61: the facts are still ON the lead and deal cards, because their ✏️ forms are folded and reading a phone number used to mean opening an editor. The CLIENT card lost its block entirely in the same change — its form is always open and prints the same three fields, so once they stopped being editable the block above was printing every value twice; nobody had seen it because the two halves had looked different. Removed to the bone, per round 70's rule that a control removed while its action still accepts the field is HIDDEN and not removed: `InlineField`, `patchLead`/`patchDeal`/`patchClient` and their three actions are deleted, not merely unreferenced, and `tests/unit/card-facts.test.ts` (source-shape, like the chat-controls tripwire) says so — the removed feature WORKED, so no assertion about behaviour could ever state «this must not come back». The `revision` plumbing went with it: those inputs were keyed only because two writers shared them, and an unexplained key is the next round's mystery.

559. **The capacity question was answered by building the year and running it, and the answer was not «slow» — it was «lying».** The owner asked whether 50 users, 50 receipts, 100 leads and 100 hisoblatish a day would hold, and what VPS it needs. Measured on a clone of his data inflated to one year (36,383 leads, 22,401 receipts, 64,920 boxes, 93,680 movements, 236,765 audit rows — **192 MB**): every application query stayed under 30 ms, p50 **0.2 ms**, and the screens ran 100-500 ms. The database is not the constraint and will not be for years. The ceiling is the APP: Next standalone is ONE Node process, measured at 130 % CPU and 742 MB RSS, saturating at **~15 requests/second** — against a realistic peak of ~1.5. Thirty times the headroom, and adding vCPUs alone does not raise it. What DID break at that volume was the funnel, and it broke by rendering a confident wrong answer.

560. **A cap shared across columns is a cap on the FIRST column.** `listLeads` ended `.limit(300)` with `ORDER BY stage.sort_order, updated_at DESC`, so the 300 rows were taken left to right: at one year's volume the board showed «Yangi 300» and then five columns reading 0 and «Bo'sh», each of which actually held ~4,500 leads. Confirmed twice — by asking postgres which stages those 300 rows belong to (all of them the first), and by opening the board in a browser and screenshotting it. And the headers could not rescue it, because `hidden` was fed only by `closedLeadCounts` (`kind <> 'open'`), so open columns had no total of their own. The fix is the shape round 47 gave the closed half: a per-COLUMN slice (`row_number() OVER (PARTITION BY stage_id …) <= 40`, named in one extra query so the typed select below is untouched) plus `openLeadCounts`, the twin of `closedLeadCounts`, so every column prints what it holds while showing forty. The deal board carried the same defect one order of magnitude later (300 of 4,206 open, spread evenly because it sorts by date) and got the same two functions. **Raising 300 was refused**: 26,000 cards in the DOM is round 70's freeze with two more zeros.

561. **The urgency belonged to ONE view, and saying otherwise would have been the same mistake in the other direction.** The first reading of this was «the sales team sees an empty funnel in three days». The verification pass refuted it: the board's default scope is the actor's OWN leads, so a seller with ~6 new leads a day takes ~50 days to breach. It is «Hammasi» — one view, the owner's — that breaks first, and he was at **278 open leads against a cap of 300** when this was measured. Both halves are worth writing down: the defect was real and imminent for him, and the population it was claimed against was wrong by 15×.

562. **A correlated subquery inside a `DISTINCT ON` runs before the dedupe — once per ROW, not once per group.** `listConversations` carried `(SELECT count(*) FROM tg_messages n WHERE n.client_id = m.client_id)` in its projection, so at 140,250 messages postgres evaluated it 140,250 times: measured **901.8 ms**, on the screen the owner uses most («95 foiz telegramda gaplashamiz») and on the 💬 dock reachable from every page in the app. Split into the round-45 shape — the newest message per client, then ONE grouped count — and the pair measures **165 + 1.7 = 167 ms, 5.4× faster**. Two details carry most of that: the list is sorted and sliced to its ceiling BEFORE the count and the manager-name lookup run, so both ask about at most 200 clients instead of every client the company has ever written to; and the list has a ceiling at all, which it never did.

563. **A Σ that reduces the fetched array describes the fetch, not the warehouse.** `/stock` capped its GROUP BY at 500 rows and then computed the boxes/kg/m³ totals, the row count and the pager label from that array — while the XLSX export ran the same query at 10,000, so screen and file printed different numbers for the same filter. At 50 receipts a day the steady state is ~700 concurrent lot×warehouse rows (1.0 lots per receipt × a 14-day mean dwell), so the cap is breached on arrival at the new rate, silently, as a shrinking total. The Σ and the row count now come from one grouped aggregate over the SAME predicate, the fetch cap is stated (`STOCK_FETCH_CAP`) and raised to 2,000, and when the table IS a slice it says so on screen rather than leaving it to be discovered.

564. **A lead nobody owns was on nobody's board.** `owner_id` is nullable by design — the bot, an import and a cleared picker all produce one — but the personal scope asks `owner_id = me` and «Hammasi» needs `crm.leads.view_all`, which a seller does not have. On his real data 27 of 383 leads (7 %) are unowned; at 100 a day that is ~7 every day landing where nobody looks. «Meniki» now means mine OR unclaimed, which is how a shared inbox has to behave, and because both halves read `leadBoardWhere` the counts followed for free (#513 earning its keep). Red-proven: strip the `isNull` branch and the orphan vanishes from a colleague's board; the neighbouring test pins that a colleague's OWNED lead still does not appear.

565. **The disk failure is correlated, and the fix is an operator procedure, not a config flag.** `pgdata`, `miniodata` and `backups` share one volume, so a full disk stops postgres AND destroys the ability to take the backup needed to recover, in the same minute — and the growth is not the database (~0.5 GB/yr) but photos and call recordings (tens of GB/yr, and the calls APK has not even shipped yet). The tempting change — a `${MINIO_PATH:-…}` bind mount in compose — was written and then REVERTED: a bind mount to a path that does not exist refuses to start the container, and the machine where that bites is production on deploy morning (#472's lesson, one layer down). What shipped instead is the procedure in `docs/DEPLOY.md` — backup, `down` (never `-v`), rsync, rename the old volume, create the new one against the second disk, start, verify photos, and only then delete the old — plus a compose comment saying why it is deliberately not automated.

566. **Consecutive in the navigation list is not adjacent on the screen.** «Sotuv main ekranda bitim bn crmni ketma ket qoy» read like a request already satisfied — `/bitimlar` and `/crm` had been neighbours in `sectionSales` for rounds. Measuring the rendered page at both breakpoints is what found the real thing: the home tiles are `grid-cols-2 sm:grid-cols-3`, and at index 1 and 2 the two-column row broke exactly between them — «Mijozlar | Bitimlar» over «CRM | Suhbatlar» — while at three columns they were already side by side. **A phone-only defect, invisible on the machine it was written on.** The fix is to anchor the pair at index 0, and the reason to write down is NOT arithmetic about column counts (a pair at `(i, i+1)` shares a row at both 2 and 3 columns whenever `i` is even and `i mod 3 ≠ 2`, so 4 and 6 also work): it is that index 0 is the only position the per-viewer filter cannot move. The home screen drops items this person may not open and the ones their workflow rows already drew, so every later index shifts from role to role — an invented `crm.leads`-only role loses the client book and everything below it slides up one. Index 0 is invariant to filtering and NOT to insertion, which is precisely what `tests/unit/home-tiles.test.ts` exists to say, and the browser half in m9p measures the real boxes because the unit fence has to name the column counts and only the stylesheet knows them.

567. **A tile carries no number, so removing the tile and removing the row are different decisions.** «Bugun qo'ng'iroq kerak emas» — and the honest first move was to work out WHICH «Bugun qo'ng'iroq» he was looking at. `buildHomeFlow` returns null for `super_admin` (the owner deliberately keeps the tile overview, m9o's tripwire), so he has never seen `sales-flow-hero`: what he named is the tile, the third edit to his own tile grid. The NAV entry went, and with it the home tile, the sidebar row and the ••• sheet row in one deletion. The sales team's hero row STAYED, because `Tile` renders icon and label while `FlowRow` renders a count — that row is the only place in the app that says how many calls are waiting, and deleting it would have answered a complaint about his screen by taking his sellers' morning queue away. The route, its permissions, the funnel's ⋯ door and the CRM section's own links are untouched: a menu decision is not an access decision (round 47), and `/bugun` has always listed the same follow-ups. Stated to him rather than assumed, so the wider removal stays one sentence away.

568. **Moving the tile did not move the screen — he had to ask twice.** Round 10 put the client book in Sotuv at his request, and «adminstrativnoedagi klientini glavniga chiqaz» is the same complaint arriving again, because a tile is where you START and the screen is where you LAND. Three things were still calling it administration and only one had been noticed: the «← Boshqaruv» link the section layout draws on it, its tile on the `/admin` hub, and — the one no reading of the layout would have found — `isActive` prefix-matching, which lit «Boshqaruv» in the sidebar and the ••• sheet at the same time as «Mijozlar». One list, `NOT_ADMIN_SECTION`, now answers all three. **The route was deliberately NOT moved.** `/mijozlar` was designed and refused: `notifications/links.ts` bakes `/admin/clients` into Telegram messages that have already been delivered and cannot be rewritten, so the redirect would be permanent rather than transitional; ~11 `revalidatePath` targets fail SILENTLY when they miss (the save works, the screen does not refresh — round 52's shape); and the version of the plan that also deleted the layout's `canClients` branch contradicted itself, since the layout would have redirected a sales manager home before any redirect ran. Against 73 references in 37 files, the thing he can actually see is worth four files: `manifest.ts` is `display: 'standalone'`, so on his phone there is no address bar at all.

569. **Taking a door off a hub can turn a link into a link to itself.** `/admin` walks a one-door visitor straight through (`tiles.length === 1`), and the layout decided separately, from its own six booleans, whether to draw a way back — two answers to one question, which agreed until the client book left the hub and the logist dropped to a single door. He would have landed on truck presets with a «← Boshqaruv» that returns him to truck presets. Found by opening `/admin` as him rather than by reasoning about permissions. `hub-doors.ts` now holds the doors once and both callers ask it: the page renders them, the layout draws the link only when there is more than one. The first version of the tripwire for this asserted the layout's source `toContain('openDoors')` and stayed GREEN with the layout reverted, because the import line still matched — a test that passes while its subject is broken is worse than no test, and it is now anchored on the assignment.

570. **The fold answers the objection the code had written against it.** «Varonka kartasini ichidagi etaplar colapse bolsin» — `StageMover` drew one button per stage, and at his eight stages that is four rows and **170 px** at 360, sitting on top of everything a salesperson opens the card to do. Its own comment argued against exactly this change: «a dropdown would hide the funnel behind a click, and the whole point of a funnel is that you can see where the deal is and where it goes next». Both halves of that are now on the SUMMARY — the current stage as its chip, the forward move as the button the boards have had since round 14 — so only the jump to a non-adjacent stage folds away, and the comment was rewritten rather than left contradicting the code. Two details the shape needs: the forward button `preventDefault`s, or its click bubbles to the `<summary>` and the fold opens under the finger that just moved the lead; and the `<details>` is keyed on the current stage, because `moveLeadAction` revalidates SOFTLY and React would otherwise keep whatever `open` the reader left behind — remounting shuts the fold on the answer it was opened to give. Measured: 170 → **74 px**, one row, still a 44 px tap target.

571. **Nine rows where five say «—» teach the eye to skip the block.** «Umumiy inoflari mobileda boshqacharoq ihcham dizayda bolsin» — the lead's facts were 474 px of the phone's first screen. The obvious compact design, label and value on one line, was **measured and refused**: `ru` is the default locale and «СЛЕДУЮЩИЙ КОНТАКТ» wants 165 px of the 294 a phone has, so it saves ~24 px on a filled lead and truncates in the language most staff read. The height came from removing rows instead. Two of them printed what the screen already carried — the NAME is the page's `h1` a hundred pixels above, the STAGE is the chip on the fold, which is drawn whether that fold is open or shut. The empty ones cost one word each on a shared «To'ldirilmagan: …» line instead of a row apiece, and the PHONE is exempt from that rule and always printed, because a lead nobody can ring is the thing a seller has to notice. Measured at 360: facts **474 → 191 px** on a sparse lead and 341 on a full one; the lenta, which is what a card is opened for, moved from y=1131 to **752** (sparse) and 902 (filled), against a tab bar that starts at 741.

572. **The same component written out twice had been fixed in neither.** The lead card's `Fact` and the deal card's were identical character for character, so extracting them (`components/card-fact.tsx`) is how the round's real defect got fixed once rather than twice: the value had `whitespace-pre-wrap` and no `overflow-wrap`, and a token with **no break opportunity** — a URL without hyphens pasted into a note — pushed the card to **433 px** on a 360 px screen, which makes mobile Chrome zoom the whole page out and moves every tap target on it (#400). Worth recording HOW that was nearly missed: the first fixture for it was a hyphenated e-mail address, the red-proof stayed GREEN, and measuring the shapes directly showed why — a browser breaks at a hyphen, so that token is 360 px wide with the fix and without it. **A red proof that does not go red is evidence about the fixture, not about the fix.** Found on the way and fixed in the same commit: the card's stage buttons awaited `moveLeadAction`'s coded refusal and threw it away, so a refused move there was a tap that did nothing — for every round since #512 gave the two boards their words; `useMoveErrors` moved into its own module so the card can share it without dragging `DragBoard` into its bundle. Stated and NOT fixed: `updateLead` writes `stage_id` with no kind check and never clears `lost_reason`, so the ✏️ form is a second stage door that can drop a lead into «Yo'qotildi» without asking why.

573. **`gsr_dev`'s leads are e2e leftovers, and CLAUDE.md called them his real data.** Verified while checking a claim about how full a typical lead is: 383 leads reduce to **12 name shapes at exactly 27 rows each** («Dilshod #», «Ko'chadi #», «Tahrir # (yangi)»…), all created 2026-07-25..27, and `GS161`/`GS252` — the two clients this file's own chore list names from the real import — **do not exist there**. The 1,692 CLIENTS are real; the funnel is not. Consequence for every round that measures: a claim about "his data" has to say WHICH table, and a layout justified by how empty his leads look is justified by nothing. This round's numbers are the ones that hold either way — the fold's 170 → 74 and the two duplicate rows — with the empty-row saving stated as a range.

574. **A voice note is a message, and the listener was only ever taught to fetch pictures.** The owner, hours after connecting his staff's accounts: «audio habarlar bizni sistemada korinmayabti». Round 13 shipped media as PHOTOS ONLY and said so; what nobody had weighed is that a client explaining what they want out loud produces a `📎` in the CRM exactly where the useful part of the conversation is. `tgMediaPlan` extends the photo planner on its own terms — pure, structural, size read from the message BEFORE any network I/O, unknown size refused (pulling blind is how a 2 GB «audio» reaches the account the business runs on) — and returns voice notes and audio files with the mime and filename to store them under. Three details earned their tests: gramjs returns `document.size` as a **big-integer object**, and `Number()` on it is NaN — `NaN <= cap` is false, so the naive read would have silently refused every voice note; a client may label a voice note `application/octet-stream`, so the VOICE ATTRIBUTE decides and not the mime; and a video note also carries an audio attribute, so its own `DocumentAttributeVideo` is what excludes it. The read side needed the same care: `attachPhotos` swept EVERY attachment of a `tg_message`, so the first stored voice note would have rendered as an `<img>` pointing at an Ogg file — it is `attachMedia` now, splitting by kind, with a pdf landing in neither list. Authz needed nothing: the `tg_message` branch decides on the message's OWNER, so the fence is the conversation and not the file type.

575. **«Ocheretda turibti», twice, and the queue was right both times — three screens had three answers.** The owner reported a reply that reached the client while the CRM still called it queued, and after round 48 built a «stuck» label for exactly that state he reported it again. The state was never the bug: `/suhbatlar` asked `outboxLabel` and could say «stuck»; the card panel — where he works — carried its own `status === 'failed' ? ✕ : ◷ navbatda`, so a claimed row read «navbatda» for hours; the dock showed no pending rows at all, so pressing send made the words vanish until the echo landed. And nothing refreshed: round 25 gave the conversation screen `AutoRefresh` and the cards went four rounds without it, while `sendReplyAction` revalidated `/suhbatlar/<id>` and no card path — so a reply queued from a lead card left that card rendering the state from before the press. Now one `OutboxBubble` computes the label in one place for all three surfaces, both page surfaces refresh and the dock polls its open thread, and `revalidateChatSurfaces` takes the screen the person is actually on (a lead card's id is the LEAD's — no derivation from a client id could reach it). The tripwire says all of it, and its own first version was too weak to notice: `toContain('AutoRefresh')` passes on a file that imports the component and never renders it, which is #494's lesson arriving from the other side — the assertion is `<AutoRefresh` now, and only then did stripping it go red. One real hole closed underneath: `recordSent`'s failure was swallowed with a `console.error`, and that row is the ONLY record a text reply has (round 53) — a database blip meant the client had the message and the thread showed nothing. It is held and retried like `markSent`'s, because a message that has left cannot be un-sent by a local error.

576. **Who talked to this client, folded — and readable per person where the work happens.** The owner's other two items: «ulangan accountlar royxati … foldable qilib ber» and «1 ta mijoz bilan 2-3 ta sotuv manageri gaplashadi, qaysi biri qanday gaplashganini tanlaydigan qilib ber». The strip of names ate the top of every conversation, and the SELECTOR existed only on «Suhbatlar» — on the cards the chips merely linked away, so the screen he works on could not answer «what did this manager say». `ThreadManagers` is one native-`<details>` component for both surfaces: closed by default, and it opens ITSELF when a filter is on, because a closed fold hiding an active filter is how somebody reads a partial conversation and believes it is the whole one. The card pages now carry `?hodim=` and filter their panel in place. Selecting is offered only to the supervision view — not a UI preference: `conversationFor` honours `managerId` only under `viewer.all` (round 34), so for anyone else the chips would be buttons that change nothing; names stay visible to everybody, which is round 20's line between who talks and what was said. One consequence handled: a filter matching nothing must NOT make the panel vanish — the way back is the fold that would vanish with it.

577. **The loudest thing on a funnel card was a stage the card is not in — and on the won column that stage was «Yo'qotildi».** The owner: «kanbanimizdagi kartalarni UI UX ni qayta korib chiq, yomon korinishda … malumotlar tartibli, odam shunday korganida adashmaydigan tartibli qilib ber». Measured before anything was changed: at 360x800 a lead card is 136 px of which the footer control row is 65, and the largest text on it is the one-tap button's label — a stage NAME, printed at 15px/600 against the card's own 16px/600 name. That name is the THIRD printing of a stage on the same screen (the chip strip, the column header, then this) and the only one naming a stage the card is not in. `nextStage` is simply `stages[i + 1]`, and every seeded funnel puts LOST straight after WON, so **every card in the won column carried a big button reading «Yo'qotildi»** — the sharpest possible form of «adashadi», and one no test could see because the markup was exactly what was intended. Three changes, not one: the button is suppressed when the next stage is `lost` (that move demands a typed reason, so it was never one-tap — it belongs in the sheet that can ask); the destination is drawn as a **stage-coloured badge**, in the vocabulary the strip and the headers already use, so it reads as a place rather than a competing headline; and the label finally shrinks, which needs `!text-xs` because `.btn` declares its own font-size after the utilities (#578). The tick moved to the far end behind a divider: its only label is an `aria-label`, so a bare checkbox sitting against a stage name read as that name's caption. Red-proven twice — the source-shape rule and the browser half (m8 moves a lead to won and asserts `move-next` has count 0).

578. **One rank, one costume — and colour means exactly one thing per surface.** The old cards spent four different treatments on four facts of the same rank: on the lead card the source was a grey pill, the owner bare text, the client code bold GREEN and the chat an emoji; on the deal card «DM1786192267 Bekzod (Super admin)» ran two facts together separated by a single space. Now identity is the only semibold line, money is the only mono line, and every descriptive fact rides ONE muted 11px row joined by « · » (`components/board-meta.tsx`), with the separator travelling WITH the part that follows it so a wrap can never orphan a « · » at the start of a line. Green left both cards: `text-good` painted the client code, which discriminates on the funnel (only some leads have a code) and is decoration on the deal board (every card has one) — so the same token was signal on one board and noise on the other, while green/amber/red already belong to the STAGE here (`STAGE_CLASS`, the strip, both headers, the move sheet). The rule the boards now keep: **stage colour is a filled badge; card colour is coloured TEXT and only ever means urgency.** Which is why the follow-up date stopped being permanently amber — it is compared against a `today` the SERVER decides (so the first HTML and the first client render agree) and goes red only when it is late. Money is `font-mono … tabular-nums` on both cards and deliberately NOT `.num`, which forces `text-right`.

579. **The deal card's title slot held a job on some cards and a person on others.** `{deal.title || deal.clientName}` meant a TITLED deal named no client at all while an UNTITLED one named the client twice (once in the title slot, once as a code in the meta) and named no job — two card grammars in one column. The client is now always the identity line and the title is an optional muted line under it, with no «untitled» placeholder because the line above already says whose card it is. In the same slot audit: an unpriced deal stated the same fact TWICE, in two severities and two colours — `notQuoted` in warn and the `unpriced` alarm in bad — and not as an edge case, since the flag is set exactly when the quote is null. The money slot now carries a neutral «—» so a column still scans as a column of numbers, and the words belong to the alarm row alone. Cost, stated: on a deal whose owner typed a job description the phone card gains one line (156 -> 178 px on this data, where every seeded deal has a title; a title is human-typed, never generated, so the line only appears where somebody wrote something).

580. **Three things measurement refused, one of them my own first answer.** (a) The desktop controls were a `float-right`, which shortens only the LINE BOXES beside it — enough to break the deal code as «B-» / «000627» and the money as «200.00 USD» / «· 0.06 m³», with the separator dangling. The obvious fix, a two-column flex row, was built and **measured worse**: the control column is 52 px and it steals that from EVERY row, not just the first, so the meta line («DM1786192267» + an ordinary owner name = 201 px in 158) wrapped and the card grew 139 -> 159 px. It is kept only because the round then removed enough rows to pay for it, and because the controls must stay inside the anchor that carries `cardTestId`. (b) Chips for the meta facts, refused on a lens's measurement before any code: at the real 288 px card width the same four facts cost +7 px when they fit and +31 px when they wrap, on a card whose whole complaint was height — and `.chip` already exists in this codebase as a ONE-per-row status badge, so building a component would have been the fifth false «we lack X». (c) `text-xs` on a button is **dead CSS**: `.btn` declares `text-[0.9375rem]` after `@tailwind utilities`, so the label renders at 15 px — measured in the browser at 15 px with the class and 12 px with `!text-xs`. A sweep found **51** live class lists in that shape across `src/`, every one a button asking for a smaller label and not getting it. They are NOT fixed here and the tripwire is deliberately not armed, for a measured reason: `text-xs` also sets `line-height: 1rem` and that half DOES win, so neither deleting the class nor adding the `!` is the zero-pixel change it looks like — it moves real geometry on 51 screens and wants its own round with its own screenshots.

581. **The deal card was reading like a contact list when it is a cargo list.** The owner, after round 78: «GS code kattada yozilib klient ismi juda kichkinada yozilsa ham bo'ladi, umuman yozilmasa ham bo'ladi … klient kodi muhim, tovar nomi muhim va nechi kub nechi kg ekani, berilgan narx muhim».
