Round 78 had made the client NAME the identity line, which is the right answer for a CRM and the wrong one here: his people address cargo by code. So the card is re-ranked — client code big and mono, our own deal number small beside it, then **what the cargo is**, then money · kub · kg, and the name drops to the muted line where it disambiguates two similar codes without competing for the eye. «Tovar nomi» is a real field and had never been on a board: `listDeals` now carries the first `deal_lines.description` — the goods the VED files during hisoblash — falling back to the typed title, with «+N» when there are more, and it is **ONE grouped query for the whole board** (#432/#526: a description per card is a query per card). Deliberately kept: the title still shows when there are no lines, because before hisoblash it is the only place anyone has written what the cargo is.

582. **The system was quietly discounting, and the comment two files away said it never would.** «Kg kubda farq bo'ladigan bo'lsa, u bilan kelishilgan narxni o'zidan o'zi skidka bermasin — faqat tovar kg kubida tafovut bo'layotganini ogohlantirishi yetarli.» `deviation.ts` states plainly that `suggestedAmount` is «a STARTING POINT for the conversation, never a price the system sets by itself. Deliberately not written anywhere» — and that was true of the DATABASE and false of the SCREEN: the deal card computed `baseSuggested = deviation.exceeds ? suggestedAmount : quotedAmount` and handed it to the charge box as its default. Cargo that measured SMALLER than the quote therefore pre-filled a bill LOWER than the price the client agreed to, and whoever pressed save granted a discount nobody decided. The prefill is now the agreed price minus any recorded damage discount, full stop. The re-priced figure is off the screen entirely — not because it was wrong, but because a number labelled «suggestion» sitting beside the charge box holding the same value is read as the system's answer; the deviation warning and its percentage stay, loudly, which is all he asked for.

583. **A picker that names a code and a date asks the reader to remember which day which cargo arrived.** «Bitimda prixodni o'ziga olayotganda hozir YW-in-001 + date bo'lib ko'rinyabti, ammo bizga date kerak emas — shuni tovar nomi va kg kubini ko'rsatadigan qilsa bo'ladimi.» The receipt already knows: `receipt_lots` carries the product name in Chinese and Russian and the summed volume and weight. `unlinkedReceipts` returns them, joined in ONE grouped query over the fifty rows it already fetched, preferring the Russian name because the Uzbek office reads the picker and `product_name_zh` is the supplier's own label. Anything missing is simply left out, so a lot-less prixod still reads as its number rather than as «— · 0 m³ · 0 kg».

584. **Two sellers taking one enquiry is not a data problem, it is two people ringing one customer.** «CRM da lead dublicat bo'lmasligiga e'tibor ber — kimdir uje bor leadni kirgazsa, bu uje borligini ogohlantirsin.» `similarLeads` runs BEFORE the write and answers with NAMES: who it found, their phone, and whose lead it is — because «there is already one» is a shrug and «Aziz Karimov, +998…, Dilnoza's» is what stops the second call. The phone is the only strong match and is compared the way the whole app compares phones (last nine digits, so «+998 90 123 45 67» and «901234567» are one person); the name matches only when no phone was typed, since two «Aziz»es are common and a warning that cries wolf is one people learn to click past. **OPEN leads only** — a lost lead coming back IS a new enquiry and a won one is already a client. It never blocks: the same press again creates it, and any edit to the name or phone retires the warning, because the answer was about the old text. Red-proven twice (the open-stage filter, the phone branch).
585. **Two writing surfaces on one card is one too many.** «CRM kartada shu zapis yozish kerak emas, lenta bor» — the «Записать контакт» form and the lenta's note box both wrote to the same feed, and a person opening a lead had to decide which one they meant. The form is gone from the lead card. What it uniquely carried was checked before it went, not after: the «keyingi kontakt» pair that feeds `/crm/today` has ALWAYS also lived in the ✏️ form (`lead-form.tsx`), and the lenta's composer renders on a lead with no client at all (`ClientFeed` returns null only when client, lead and deal are all absent), so a brand-new lead can still be written on. m8 was rewritten to prove exactly that rather than to accommodate it: it now writes the note on the lenta, books the follow-up in ✏️, and still finds the lead on the call list — the removal's whole claim, executed in a browser. **What IS lost, and was told to him:** the four kinds (📞 call / 💬 message / 🤝 meeting / 📝 note). `crm_activities.kind` is not dead data — `client-feed.tsx` reads it to choose the bubble's mark — so from now on everything typed on a lead is a note. Putting the four chips on the lenta's own box is a sentence away if he wants them; it was not done unasked because the last two rounds were spent taking height OFF this card.

586. **The bottom of the rail is the middle of the page.** «Istoriya tarix mobileda eng pastda bo'lishi kerak, hozir o'rtada bo'lib qolgan.» The audit history sat last in the RAIL, which is right on a desktop — under the facts, in the sticky column — and wrong on a phone, where `CardCols` stacks rail-then-main and «last in the rail» lands ABOVE the lenta the card is opened to read. `CardCols` gained a third slot: DOM order is rail → main → tail, so the phone needs no rule at all, and the desktop grid places all three explicitly (`md:col-start-2 md:row-start-2`) so the history keeps its old place under the rail. One markup, no duplicated node for a locator to find twice (#509), no window measuring. Measured at 360: the lenta moved 752 → **628** and the history from the middle to y=865, last on the page; at 1280 nothing moved.

587. **The privacy fence was the reason new customers were invisible, and the way past it is to ask whose NUMBER it is.** «Telegramdan yangi klientlar ochsa smslar chatlar nega korinmayabti» — because `classifyDialog` kept a chat only when the peer's phone was already in the client book, so a customer writing in for the FIRST time is `not_a_client` by definition and produced nothing at all. The fence itself was right: a manager's Telegram is also their private life, and it was the only thing between that and the company database — his own import found 13 clients among 394 dialogs. So the fence is not removed, it is asked a better question. `tg_accounts.work_account` (0064, default FALSE, his design: «kalit qilib ber har kim ozi tanlasin») says whether a number is only ever used for clients; `unknownChatAction` reads it and answers **open_lead** on a work account and **ask** on a personal one. Exactly ONE refusal is reconsidered — `not_a_client` — and the test names the other five, because a work account must not turn a group, a bot or the manager's own Saved Messages into a customer, and `no_phone` must not mint a lead nobody can ring. The default is false because **the safe answer must be the one nobody has to choose**. Both directions count: the owner asked for the seller writing OUT to a stranger to open a lead too, and the listener has seen outgoing messages since #315 fixed their peer resolution.

588. **A hash is the only version of this feature that is safe to store.** He asked that creating a lead, a deal or a client look BACK into the connected accounts for that number and offer the existing chat. Answering that needs a list of every chat each account holds — which, stored the obvious way, is a copy of an employee's private address book living in the company database, and the tempting shortcut (keep the peer's NAME so the screen can say «Dilshod bilan chat bor») is exactly what turns this into surveillance. `tg_peer_index` holds `sha256(pepper + normalised last 9)` and **no name and no number**; the pepper is derived from `SESSION_SECRET` rather than a new variable, because a variable nobody sets is a pepper that is the empty string on the one machine that matters — and a bare sha256 of nine digits is not a secret at all, a laptop exhausts that keyspace in seconds. Rotating the secret makes every row unmatchable, which is safe rather than broken: the refresh rebuilds from the accounts. The last-nine rule is #111's, so `+998 90 175-78-00` and `901757800` are one person rather than two — red-proven by hashing the raw string and watching four spellings become four fingerprints. A match is NOT an authorisation: it names the MANAGER, and opening the conversation still goes through the per-manager fence (round 20). Stated to him: coverage is partial because Telegram hides most numbers, so the screen must say «topilmadi» and never «yo'q».

589. **Do not mint twice.** The rule that matters more than the minting: the same person messages, then rings, then messages again from a second Telegram account on the same number — and a funnel carrying three cards for one human being is worse than the silence it replaced. `leadForChat` looks the number up among OPEN leads before it writes anything (the calls matcher's precedent), ordered oldest-first because round 67b's lesson is that an unordered pick among duplicates is a different answer on different days — here it would move a conversation between cards. `convertLead` re-keys the chat onto the minted code through `rekeyLeadChats`, the deliberate twin of `rekeyLeadCalls`: both halves of «what do we have on this person» must follow the same person onto the same code, or the client card shows the calls and not the chat. Found by the typechecker rather than by a test, and worth the entry: making `client_id` nullable broke `tg-doctor`, which passed it straight into a `string` parameter — the diagnostic now says `isNotNull` in SQL as well, because it is about the reply window on a CLIENT and should not have been relying on a join to imply that.

590. **The two tests that broke were the right ones, and the fix was to sharpen the promise rather than drop it.** `telegram-live`'s «refuses a stranger and says nothing about them» and its integration twin encoded the fence in the strongest possible form: a refusal carries **no id, no name and no number**, so the caller cannot log a stranger even by accident. Round 79 breaks that on purpose for exactly one outcome — a tray cannot offer an anonymous row — and the honest response was to state the new line precisely instead of loosening the assertion. On a personal account a stranger now yields `{ ask: true, peerId, phone, title }` and the test asserts what it must NOT contain: **no `row`, and the message body nowhere in the serialised verdict**. Every other refusal still carries nothing, and the integration half now proves the silence against the TABLE rather than against the verdict, which is the stronger place to prove it anyway. A companion test was added for the work-account side, because a promise with a switch in it needs both positions pinned.

591. **Green lint, green build, 1135 green tests — and CI red on the one step none of them run.** Widening `LiveVerdict`'s `store: true` branch into a union (a known client, or a stranger on a work number) left five call sites in two TEST files narrowing with `if (!v.store) throw` and then reading `v.clientId`. Nothing local said a word: `next build` typechecks `src/` and does not reach `tests/`, and vitest transpiles without checking, so the assertions ran and passed on values that were there at runtime. CI's separate `pnpm typecheck` step is the only thing in the pipeline that types the test tree, and it is now step 6 of the ritual. The fix is the idiom the listener already uses — `'openLead' in v` — rather than a cast, because the narrowing is a real question about which verdict arrived and a cast would have hidden the next widening too. **A test file is source, and the only tool that reads it as source runs in CI.**

592. **The login fence had been open since it was built, and the ads round is what found it.** Reviewing a PUBLIC form's abuse surface sent a lens through the code that already answers strangers, and it came back with two live holes that have nothing to do with Instagram. (a) `requestMeta()` read `x-forwarded-for.split(',')[0]` — the LEFTMOST hop, which is whatever the CALLER wrote, because every proxy APPENDS to that list. (b) `isRateLimited` counted the **(identifier, ip) PAIR**, five failures per fifteen minutes. Together those are not a rate limit at all: rotating one header puts every guess in its own bucket, so any staff password on this system — ~20 people signing in with a phone number and a password, holding a thirty-day rolling session — could be brute-forced without limit. The same forged value was also being written into `sessions.ip` and into every audit row, so the «devices» list on /profile and the audit trail recorded whatever the attacker typed. Now `trustedIpFrom` takes the RIGHTMOST entry (the one our own Caddy appended, the only element nobody outside can dictate) and returns null when there is no header at all, and the limiter counts the **ACCOUNT** — the thing being attacked, which no header can change — with a wider twenty-per-address net kept as a second layer for spraying. The trade is stated in the code: five failures now lock ONE account for fifteen minutes whoever caused them, so somebody who knows a colleague's number can be a nuisance; that is recoverable in fifteen minutes and an unbounded password guess is not recoverable at all. Red-proven both ways — restore the pair and the rotating-address test goes red; restore `[0]` and the header test does.

593. **The same login form was also being served over plain HTTP, and had been since the first deploy.** `docker-compose.yml` published `3000:3000` on the app service, so besides `https://gsrwms.uz` through Caddy the identical app answered on `http://<vps>:3000` — outside TLS, outside the proxy, and therefore with an `X-Forwarded-For` that only the caller had written (which is what made #585's second half reachable from the open internet rather than from inside the network). The mapping is removed: Caddy reaches the app as `app:3000` on the internal docker network and never needed the host port. Stated in the file for whoever next wants to debug: bind it to loopback and use an SSH tunnel, never to `0.0.0.0`. This is a compose change, so it takes effect on `docker compose up -d` when the app container is RECREATED — and until he deploys it, the plaintext door is still open.

594. **Two drains, one event, two tasks — found because a new test file changed who vitest ran next to.** Adding `login-lockout.integration.test.ts` reshuffled the duration-ordered run (#380's rule: integration order is not even lexical), and `automation.integration.test.ts` began failing on `fireCount: expected 2 to be 1`. It is NOT caused by the new file and not by anything in this round: the same two files run together **on pristine `main`** fail identically, and `automation` alone passes in both trees — verified in a throwaway worktree at `origin/main`, which is the only way to tell «my change broke it» from «my change moved it». The mechanism is in the engine, not the tests: `processPendingEvents` claims work by polling `processed_at IS NULL` with **no `FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED`**, so two drains overlapping on one database can both pick up the same row. In the suite that is two files sharing `gsr_dev`; in production it is the per-minute pg-boss sweep overlapping the kick that every CRM action fires (`enqueue(JOB_PROCESS_EVENTS)` in the `run()` helpers), and the visible consequence is a phase-7 rule firing twice — **a duplicate task, or a duplicate Telegram message, from one stage move**. NOT fixed here, deliberately: the claim needs `FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED` plus a test that proves two concurrent drains split the work rather than duplicate it, and a change to the event engine at the end of a long session is how the next round's bug gets written. Recorded with its reproduction: `npx vitest run tests/integration/automation.integration.test.ts tests/integration/deal-auto-stage.integration.test.ts`.

595. **The tray could say «this is GS777» and «never», and a first-time customer is neither.** 0064 taught the LISTENER to open a lead by itself on a work number, and left the personal-number half — which is every connected account today — with a question it had no honest answer to. «Yangi lid» is that answer, and it is one press because that is its frequency: commoner than finding an existing code, rarer than «not my business». A rule may now point at a LEAD (**migration 0065**), which is the widening `tg_messages` took in 0064 and `call_logs` in 0063; the include CHECK moved rather than being dropped, so an included chat still names SOMEBODY. It goes through `leadForChat`, so #589's rule is inherited rather than restated — an open lead already carrying the number wins, and pressing twice does not mint twice — and the lead is owned by the manager whose ACCOUNT the chat is in, never by whoever pressed: the owner reading his staff's tray must not collect their leads. Both pointers can never be live at once, because the client door clears the lead one and the lead door clears the client one; the test that proves it was red-proven by deleting a single `leadId: null`. Refused where Telegram gave no number, and the button is not even drawn there — the same `no_phone` line `unknownChatAction` holds, because a lead nobody can ring is a row with a name in it. Stated: this attaches the chat FORWARD, exactly like «Bu mijoz» always has; reaching back through Telegram for what was said before the press is a separate decision and was not taken here.

596. **Where the lead branch is read is the whole of its design.** `classifyWithRules` answers «whose CLIENT chat is this», and that is the only question the history import can act on — widening its verdict would have rippled into `tg-import`, four test files and every consumer of `DialogVerdict`, to teach them a shape none of them can use. So the lead rule is read one level up, in `decideIncoming`, where the answer is already actionable: the lead exists, the message has somewhere to go. It must come BEFORE the classifier or a lead chat would fall through `not_a_client` and, on a personal account, be put back on the tray as a question somebody has already answered — and `isSelf` still comes before it, because Saved Messages is not a customer under any decision anybody could write down. Both orderings are red-proven. What the widening cost is worth recording: `store: true` is a union of three now, `if (!v.store)` narrows away none of it, and #591 was exactly this shape arriving on CI in files nobody had touched. **`isClientVerdict` is exported beside the type**, so the question is asked in one place and the fourth variant will not be five call sites again.

597. **A table nothing writes to answers nothing.** `tg_peer_index` shipped in 0064 with its hash, its pepper and its index — and no producer and no consumer, so «has anybody here talked to this number» was always no. Both ends are built now, and the producer had only one possible home: building the index needs a live Telegram connection, and the listener is the only process in this system that holds one — a pg-boss job cannot do it at any hour. It runs at start and then daily, and «not more often than daily» is enforced against `max(updated_at)` rather than trusted to the interval, because the listener restarts on every deploy and walking four hundred dialogs per boot is a burst with no purpose. The consumer is `TelegramLookback` on the lead, deal and client cards, and three rules are visible in its markup: it names the MANAGER (the index holds no name to give), a COLLEAGUE's match is a sentence and not a button (round 20 — knowing a conversation exists is not permission to open it), and it renders NOTHING when there is no match or when somebody has already answered about that chat, `include` or `exclude` alike. That last one is red-proven and is the difference between a hint and nagging: re-offering a refused chat on every card that carries the number turns a decision into a question that keeps coming back. The attach action never reads `managerUserId` from the form and re-derives the offer for the ACTOR before writing, so a hand-posted peer id cannot attach a colleague's conversation on their behalf. Measured at 360: the panel is 155 px with two rows, the document stays 360 wide, the tray's three buttons wrap to two rows of 36 px.

598. **A clock is a unique id per millisecond, and a fast test file spends many tests inside one.** CI failed on round 82's own new test with `expected [ …n, …n ] to include …n`, and the green run here proved nothing except that this container is slower. The file mints peer ids as `BigInt(Date.now() + 21)`, `+ 31`, `+ 41` — offsets picked to keep tests apart — and runs end to end in ~120 ms, so `Date.now() + 41` in one test IS `Date.now() + 21` twenty milliseconds later. On the runner they landed on the same number: «Chatni qo'shish» left an `include` rule on the peer the lookback test then indexed, `offerableMatches` correctly refused to offer an answered chat, and the assertion read as a defect in the code it was testing. Fixed with a frozen base plus a **counter** (`PEER_BASE + (peerSeq += 1)`), which is the same fix `login-lockout` needed one round ago (#592) after minting two users from the clock alone and getting one. Recorded as a rule because it has now cost two rounds: **an id a test needs to be unique must come from a counter; the clock may only make it unique across RUNS.** Reproduced before fixing, by forcing call 9 to return call 3's id — same test, same assertion, same shape as the runner's — and the collision-free version then passed three runs in a row. Also comment-fixed on the way: `offerableMatches`'s two `inArray`s are a cross product, wider than the question, and that is safe only because the answer is a SET keyed on the exact (manager, peer) pair — which matters in the normal case, not an edge one, because a Telegram peer id is global and two colleagues with the same customer share it.

599. **The drain now claims what it takes, which is the fix #594 wrote down and deliberately did not make.** `processPendingEvents` polled `processed_at IS NULL`, did the work, then stamped the batch — so two overlapping drains read the same rows and both acted. `claimNextEvent()` takes one event at a time with the idiom this codebase already uses for exactly this shape (`outbox.ts:claimNext`, `issue/approvals.ts`): the UPDATE stamps `processed_at` inside a `SELECT … FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED`, so a row belongs to whichever drain won it and the loser skips past instead of blocking. Red-proven with the lock removed: **1584 recipient rows against 792 distinct (event, user, channel) triples** — every event delivered exactly twice — and equal with it. **Two false red proofs came first and both are worth the entry.** The first used `InternalNote`, which has no recipients, so nothing was countable and the stripped version stayed green — a test that passes because the thing under test never appeared proves nothing (#494, for the third time). The second compared each event's count against the FIRST event's, which uniform doubling never exceeds; comparing `count(*)` to `count(DISTINCT …)` is the assertion that cannot be satisfied by everything being wrong equally. The trade is stated: claiming BEFORE the work means a process that dies mid-batch loses that event, where marking after meant duplicating it — and duplication is the common case (the per-minute sweep against the kick every CRM action fires) while a crash between the commit and the handler is not.

600. **A stranger's request must never tell the stranger anything.** Every public door here — the form, Meta's webhook, the bot — answers with ONE constant string whether a lead was created, joined onto yesterday's enquiry, recognised as a customer's number, or dropped at the cap. Answering differently for a number already in the client book turns a public page into a way to walk a list and learn who our customers are; it is the fence `ingestCalls` keeps for the calls app, and it is why `landInboundLead` returns its outcome to the SERVER only. The one exception is deliberate and is the opposite kind of answer: a complaint about the INPUT («write the whole phone number») is about the box the sender just typed in and says nothing about us, so it is specific and immediate. The landing order is the design — a known CLIENT first (their question belongs on the card that carries their cargo and their balance, not on a parallel lead nobody reads), then the ONE open lead on the same number within thirty days (joined, and the owner is left alone: whoever is working the enquiry keeps it), then a new lead. Lost leads are never joined onto: somebody who was lost and came back is a new enquiry, and a card closed with a written reason must not be reopened by a machine. Matching is on the PHONE only, never the name — `similarLeads` also matches an exact name when no phone was typed, which is right when a seller is reading and deciding and catastrophic for a machine, where `{name: 'Aziz'}` from a stranger would write into a real customer's card.

601. **«Navbat bilan hammaga», and the case that matters is when the rota is empty.** Who takes their turn is a COLUMN on the role (`roles.inbound_rota`, migration 0066), following round 23's `warehouse_scoped` — and deliberately NOT `salesManagerOptions()`, which answers «who may be shown in a dropdown» and re-adds people who have been deactivated, the last thing an advert lead should find. Whose turn: fewest inbound leads in the window, longest since their last one breaks the tie, `NULLS FIRST` so a seller who has never had one sorts to the front, which is what makes a new person's first day work. No stored pointer — a counter would have to be kept correct when somebody joins, leaves or is off sick, and the leads themselves already know. It ships OFF for every role, so on the morning of the deploy nobody is in it: an advert lead is then created with **no owner at all**, and that is the state everything downstream has to survive. `followUps` gained the `isNull(ownerId)` branch it never had — round 74's «Meniki means mine OR unclaimed», now on the call list too — so an unclaimed lead is on EVERY seller's `/bugun` instead of nobody's. The client half deliberately did not widen: a client follow-up is a date a person typed on a card, so it already belongs to somebody. Red-proven, and the rota's own guard too: strip the `inbound_rota = true` clause and all 275 active users enter the rotation, which took four tests red at once.

602. **Three doors, because each one fails in a different place.** The **public form** (`/ariza?manba=instagram`) needs nothing from anybody and works the minute it deploys — a Meta lead form has to be approved and connected and kept connected, and this works for a channel we have never heard of. The **bot** (`t.me/<bot>?start=ad_instagram`) asks only for the number, and Telegram itself proves it, which is a stronger identity than anything typed. **Meta Lead Ads** is the one that needs setting up, and two of its rules are load-bearing. `metaConfig()` returns null unless ALL THREE secrets are present and the route **404s on null**, because `undefined === undefined`: an unconfigured server comparing a missing verify token against a missing query parameter would agree it was us and hand out a subscribed webhook. And the signature is computed over `await request.text()` — the raw bytes — because `request.json()` and re-serialising cannot reproduce what was signed: `JSON.stringify` picks its own spacing, number formatting and key order. `timingSafeEqual` THROWS on a length mismatch, so length is checked first and every early return is a plain false; an unhandled throw in a webhook is a 500, and Meta reads a non-200 as «send it again». The webhook only ENQUEUES and answers 200 immediately for the same reason. Meta's payload does not say whether the advert ran on Instagram or Facebook, so the source is recorded as `meta` («Instagram/Facebook reklama») rather than guessed — writing «Instagram» on a Facebook lead would put a lie into `funnelReport`, the one screen that answers which advert is worth paying for; `ad_id` is kept in `ref` so the two can be separated later without losing this month.

603. **A «filled it too fast to be a person» timer was built here, and the e2e caught it eating a real submission.** The reviewed plan called for a honeypot AND a timestamp, and at three seconds from mount the browser test — which fills the form, is refused twice for a short name and a short phone, and then submits — was under the threshold and got the same cheerful «Arizangiz qabul qilindi» as everybody else while nothing was written. That is the exact shape of the worst bug this feature can have. The trade is not symmetrical: a junk lead is visible on the funnel and one tap from being lost, while an enquiry we paid an advert for and dropped in silence is invisible for ever — and a serious robot adds a delay in one line, so the check was costing real leads to stop nobody. It is out. What remains is the honeypot, which has no false positives at all, and the two caps in `landInboundLead` — three arrivals per phone per day, two hundred per source per day — which count what ACTUALLY ARRIVED rather than guessing at who sent it, and which count the dropped ones too, or the ceiling is escapable by making every attempt look like a duplicate.

604. **Making a column nullable is a change to every reader that joins on it.** Migration 0066 relaxed `crm_activities.created_by` so a machine could write a note — and `clientFeed`'s note branch had `JOIN users u ON u.id = a.created_by`, an INNER join, so an advert lead's first message was dropped from the lenta silently. The card rendered a lead with nothing on it while the enquiry sat in the database: the one thing the seller opens the card to read, invisible. Found by the browser test, not by reading, and not by any of the ten integration cases that had just been written about the landing — because every one of them asked the DATABASE what was stored and none asked the SCREEN what it showed. A sweep over the other author joins in the same file and across `src/` found none that need changing: `client_transactions.created_by` and `handovers.created_by` are still NOT NULL, and `leads.created_by`, which 0066 also relaxed, is read by nothing at all. The lenta case is now an integration test that goes red the moment the join goes back to INNER.

605. **The first migration-number collision, and the ledger repair it needed.** Two sessions minted **0065** on the same day — `0065_tg_chat_lead` and `0065_inbound_leads` — with the SAME journal `when` (1785190000034), because both counted up from 0064 without looking at what the other had pushed. Theirs merged first, so mine became **0066**; that part is trivial. The part worth writing down is the local database: drizzle's migrator applies every entry whose `folderMillis` exceeds the LAST APPLIED `created_at`, so after the rename my already-applied migration (recorded at ...034) would have made theirs (...034, not greater) silently SKIP while mine (...035) re-ran and failed on `CREATE TABLE lead_intakes` — and since drizzle wraps all pending migrations in ONE transaction, that failure would have rolled back theirs too. Repaired by moving the ledger row back to ...033, undoing my own DDL by hand and letting `pnpm db:migrate` apply BOTH in journal order, which is also the only real proof that they compose (68 rows, both objects present). RULE, now that the DECISIONS numbers have collided four times and the migrations once: **`git fetch origin main` and read the tail of `_journal.json` before minting either.**

606. **A flag is the wrong kind of protection for demo data, and the owner found out by deploying.** «Productionga chiqazganimda seed bilan birga ko'chirib qo'yyabti warehouse users va boshqa hamma demo datalarni.» The blocks sat in `seed.ts` behind `if (seedDemo)`, where `seedDemo` meant «the users table is empty» — which is true of a real installation exactly ONCE, on the day it is created, which is the day he ran the bootstrap. So the guard was correct, documented, tripwired, and useless: it let demo warehouses and eleven accounts with a published password into a live company, and `seed.ts` runs from the compose `migrate` service on every deploy thereafter. The fix is not a tighter condition but the absence of one — warehouses, demo users, example clients, the §6.9 FX rates and the canonical GS777 receipt moved to `scripts/seed-demo.ts`, which only CI and the local suite run. `seed.ts` no longer CONTAINS the code, so there is nothing left to get wrong; `seed-demo.ts` still refuses a database holding any account that is not one of its own, because a script that is safe only while nobody makes a mistake is not safe. The tripwire changed shape with it: it used to assert the flag was wired up and now reads `seed.ts` as TEXT, refusing the demo password, the demo phone numbers, the demo warehouse names, the canonical receipt and the word `seedDemo` — which is why none of those strings is written out in that file's own header comment. Red-proven by putting one demo warehouse back behind a flag.

607. **Removing the demo accounts removed the only way in, and the instructions went on promising them.** A freshly bootstrapped server now had every permission, role, setting, cost type and funnel stage — and nobody to sign in as — while `ops/bootstrap.sh` still printed `+998900000001 / demo1234`. `pnpm create-admin +998… "Ism Familiya"` mints one super_admin and prints a GENERATED password once: generated rather than taken as an argument, because an argument lands in the shell history and in every process list on the machine; generated rather than defaulted, because a default IS a published password wearing another hat; and drawn from an alphabet with no look-alike characters, because it is read off a terminal and typed on a phone. It refuses the moment any account exists — this is a bootstrap, not a way to add staff. Verified end to end on a throwaway database, including the refusal.

608. **A measurement that never ran is not a measurement.** The hisoblash clock (round 28) lost its last door in round 46, so for two months a five-minute sweep asked the database about rows nothing could create, `/bugun` could never receive a calc task, and the VED home printed a queue that was structurally zero. The owner's «hisoblatish degan hamma narsani yop» closed it — and then he narrowed it in the same breath, which is the part that mattered: the bot's 🧮 intake STAYS, and it was missing exactly one thing. Of his two asks one was already true (`landIntake` has always created the lead under the sender, so it already belonged to whoever sent it); the stage was not. `crm_calc_stage`/`deal_calc_stage` are settings with a picker on both funnel-settings screens rather than a name the code hunts for — the funnel is his to rename and reorder, which is the same reason `lead_sources` grew a stable key — and empty is both the default and a real answer. The move is FORWARD only by sort order (#392's rule: a card further along is somebody's work in progress, and a machine dragging it back because a second request arrived undoes a person's decision), goes through `moveLead`/`moveDeal` so the audit row, the stage event and any automation rule watching that stage fire as if a person had dragged it, and is FENCED, because a funnel misconfiguration must never lose the note the bot has just promised to save. `calc_requests` the TABLE stays: it holds real rows from before round 46, and a table is never dropped in the release that stops writing it. Red-proven twice — no move at all, and a move that is not forward-only.

609. **The off-site backup gets an S3 destination, and building a stand-in bucket found the bug that would have broken it.** The owner: «google drive emas boshqa joy yo'qmi buni zahira olgani». Drive's problems are all in its credentials — a refresh token that dies after seven days unless the OAuth app is published, minted by hand through a browser, and a bespoke exchange nobody else speaks — while an S3 key does not expire, is made in one screen, and this system already talks the protocol, because MinIO is where every photograph in it lives. `backupS3Config` needs all four values or answers null, so a half-configured destination reads as «not configured» and takes the local dump rather than failing silently; `forcePathStyle` is mandatory against any non-AWS endpoint, since virtual-host addressing would ask for `bucket.eu2.contabostorage.com` and fail as DNS, the hardest kind of failure to read. TWO destinations now, and S3 wins when both are filled in — deliberately NOT a fallback chain, because trying the second when the first fails means a night's backup landing where nobody is looking and «it worked» hiding a destination that has been broken for a month. **The find:** there is no bucket in this container, so the wire was spoken at a stand-in HTTP server of our own — and it immediately showed that since ~3.729 the AWS SDK computes a CRC32 on every upload and, for a STREAM body, delivers it as a trailer: `content-encoding: aws-chunked`, `x-amz-content-sha256: STREAMING-UNSIGNED-PAYLOAD-TRAILER`, **no `Content-Length`, and 4140 bytes on the wire for 4096 bytes of dump**. Contabo and several other S3 clones refuse that shape outright. `requestChecksumCalculation: 'WHEN_REQUIRED'` restores a plain body with a real length — measured both ways, and red-proven by taking the line out and watching three tests fail. Round 9's Drive path shipped without ever making one real request, which is exactly why nobody learned about the seven-day token until later; a stand-in that speaks the real protocol is the cheapest version of not repeating that. `dumpsToPrune` also refuses `keep <= 0`: through a plain slice that returns the WHOLE list, so one mistyped environment variable would delete every backup on the night it was set — nothing is a safer answer than everything.

610. **A funnel's most expensive card is the one nothing happens to, and that produces no event.** Phase 7 could hear a stage move and a warehouse event; the owner's own example of what was missing was the opposite — «lead 3 kundan beri shu bosqichda turibdi, javob yo'q → eslat» — and a forgotten lead is silent by definition, so no trigger built on events could ever reach it. `lead_stale`/`deal_stale` (migration 0067) are swept on a clock instead. «Sitting still» is deliberately `updated_at` older than N days and NOT «entered the stage N days ago»: reading when a card entered a column means reading `audit_log` per card, and the honest question is not how long it has been in this column but how long since anybody did ANYTHING to it — an edit, a note, a price. A seller who touched the card yesterday has not forgotten it, whatever column it is in.

611. **«Once» has two meanings and only one of them is useful.** A move-triggered rule fires on an event, which happens once; a time trigger fires on a CONDITION that stays true every hour until somebody acts, so without a record the sweep opens the same task every hour — which is precisely how people learn to ignore the thing meant to save them. `automation_fires` carries a unique index on (rule, entity) and the sweep INSERTS BEFORE IT ACTS, so a crash after the claim costs one reminder instead of duplicating it, and two overlapping sweeps cannot both speak (#599's lesson in the shape this table can carry). But «once ever» is the wrong once: a lead reminded in March, worked on in April and forgotten again in May deserves a second reminder. So the claim is an UPSERT that is re-won only when `fired_at < updated_at` — the card has been touched since the rule last spoke. **That is why there is no hook in `moveLead` or anywhere else to clear anything**: the record's own timestamp is the fact, every write path already moves it, and a design whose correctness depends on remembering to call a cleanup is a design that will be wrong the first time somebody adds a fourth way to edit a lead. Red-proven twice — insert-only (`DO NOTHING`) kills the second reminder, and no claim at all takes three tests red at once, because a sweep with no memory poisons every later assertion in the file. A claim the action then declines (a rule assigning to «the responsible person» on a card that has none) stays SPENT — retrying it hourly only repeats the same warning for ever — but it is deliberately NOT counted, because `fire_count` is the one number that tells the owner a working rule from a typo, and a count that outruns the tasks it opened tells him nothing.

612. **A condition is a question about a card, so a rule that has no card may not ask one.** `CONDITION_FIELDS` is a fixed curated list per board, and that is the whole safety of the feature: a rule is written by an admin and evaluated by the server against a record it did not choose, so an open field name would be a way to read whatever the row happens to carry — `isConditionField` refuses `password_hash` for the same reason it refuses `source` on a deal. A warehouse event belongs to no funnel, so a condition on one is refused **at the save** rather than accepted and never fired: a rule that looks saved and quietly never runs is the worst of the three possible behaviours. Inside the evaluator two rules are load-bearing and both mean the same thing — an answer nobody can compute is «this rule does not apply», never «fire». An UNKNOWN field is false rather than skipped (a rule naming a field the record lacks is written wrong, and firing it on everything would be the loudest possible way to be wrong). And `gt`/`lt` refuse a value that is not a number on EITHER side, so «5 > katta yuk» is false rather than whatever JavaScript decides. The two boards share the names of the four things both carry (round 71 gave a lead its own quote) so one loaded record answers either, and a field the board does not have is ABSENT from that record rather than null — because `conditionsMatch` reads absence as «does not apply» and null as «empty», and asking a deal whether its phone is empty must not answer yes.

613. **The same sentence must mean the same thing whichever trigger woke it.** «Open a task for the responsible person» has to behave identically for a stage move and for three days of silence, so both paths meet in ONE `applyRuleAction` — the alternative, a copy per trigger, is how the two halves drift until only one of them fills `{ism}`. The stale path passes `actorId: null`, because nobody CAUSES a silence: a rule assigning to «whoever did it» then finds no one and is skipped loudly, and the form does not offer that option for a time trigger in the first place. `{ism}`/`{kod}`/`{narx}`/`{kub}`/`{kg}`/`{etap}` are the canned Telegram replies' vocabulary extended, not a second one — two placeholder languages in one app is one too many for the person who writes both — and the unknown-placeholder rule is copied verbatim: a placeholder nobody defined is LEFT ALONE (braces in somebody's text are their text) while an empty VALUE blanks itself (a nameless lead must not send «{ism} bilan bog'laning» to a colleague). Numbers print the way a person writes them: postgres hands back `200.00` and `2.500`, and neither is what anybody would put in a task title. The card is loaded ONLY when the rule needs it — `ruleNeedsRecord` asks whether there is a condition or a placeholder — so the event path, which runs on every drain, still costs no extra query in the common case (#432).

614. **A disabled input posts nothing, and this time the gap moves somebody else's answer.** The condition rows post as three parallel lists zipped by position, and the two operators that ask about the box itself (`empty` / `not_empty`) have nothing to type — so the value box goes away by being **hidden**, never `disabled`: a `display:none` input still submits, a disabled one does not, and one silent gap hands the next row the previous row's value. This is #171's fifth appearance and the first where the damage is silent rather than a visibly emptied field. The browser half of the e2e proves it, and the ORDER of its fixture is the test: the valueless condition goes FIRST, because with it last the missing entry falls off the end where nothing notices. Red-proven by swapping one word — `hidden` for `disabled` — and reading the saved rule back as «Сумма больше » with the 500 gone. Two other things only the 360 px screenshot said: two selects side by side clipped the field to «Источни…» when half the list starts with the same word, then clipped the operator to «рав…», so each control takes its own line (#421); and greying the value box out was tried first and read as an ordinary empty box, which is why it is gone rather than dimmed.

615. **A sweep that can wake somebody up runs during working hours.** The threshold is measured in DAYS, so a sweep every minute buys nothing, and the two things a rule can do are open a task and send a Telegram message — a message at 04:00 is worse than the same message at 09:00, and nobody reads a task before they wake. `20 4-14 * * *` is the Tashkent office day (09:00–19:00, UTC+5) written in the UTC the container runs on, and the conversion is stated in the code because working it out from a colleague's night-time notification is the expensive way to learn it. Nothing is lost by sleeping: a card that went quiet at midnight is just as quiet at nine, and the candidates are ordered by how long they have been sitting. `STALE_PER_SWEEP` is 200 with the bound stated — the first time somebody writes such a rule against a funnel holding ten thousand forgotten leads, the alternative is ten thousand tasks in one minute — and what is left over arrives on the next pass rather than being lost. There is deliberately **no prune** of `automation_fires`: deleting a row RE-ARMS its rule against that card, so a housekeeping job would quietly re-open old reminders, and the table is bounded by (rules × cards that have gone quiet) anyway.

616. **The funnel had two doors and only one of them knew the law.** `moveLead`/`moveDeal` have refused a move into a lost stage without a written reason since the day they shipped, and cleared that reason on the way back out. `updateLead`/`updateDeal` — the ✏️ form on both cards — wrote `stage_id` and did NEITHER, and the form renders a `<select>` of every stage, so this was an ordinary press and not a forged post. Two consequences, both reproduced before anything was changed: a lead could be dropped into «Yo'qotildi» with nobody's reason on it, which is the one fact the business always wants about a closed job; and a REVIVED lead kept the reason it had been lost for, which the card prints in red above an open card. The fix is one function both doors ask — `stageWrite(targetKind, typedReason)` returns the `lost_reason` a move must write, or refuses — and the elegance is that **the form passes no reason**, so the same rule that lets the board through refuses the form without a second condition existing anywhere. #528's shape, again: a pair rule enforced in one direction only. Only on an actual MOVE, because an ordinary save on a lead that is already lost must be neither a refusal nor a wipe — a test pins that third case, and it is the one a careless fix breaks. Unified on the stricter of the two thresholds (`< 2` characters, the lead's) since the point of one law is that there is one. Red-proven three times.

617. **A screen must not offer what the service refuses — and the obvious way to do that revives the record.** With the law in place the ✏️ forms would have listed «Yo'qotildi» and answered a press with a refusal, so `formStages` drops lost stages from the four create/edit pickers. The trap it exists to avoid was found while writing it: filtering the current stage OUT of a `<select>` makes it fall back to its FIRST option, so opening the form of an already-lost lead to correct a phone number and pressing Save would silently move it back onto the board — a worse bug than the one being fixed, and invisible to anything but a test that puts a lost record in front of the form. The record's own stage therefore stays in the list. The board's `StageMover` is untouched and still offers every stage: it is the door that asks why. Verified in a browser at 360 — the form lists eight stages and not the ninth, the fold still lists all nine, document 360 wide.

618. **An existing test broke, and that was the proof.** `m9v-automation` picked `stageValues[stageValues.length - 1]` for its rule target and then moved a deal there through the edit form. Every seeded funnel puts LOST last (#577), so the spec had been exercising the defect all along — it went red the moment the defect was fixed, which is stronger evidence than any assertion I could have written. The spec now chooses `option:not([data-kind="lost"])`, and the rule picker stamps `data-kind` for it: a rule ON a lost stage is legitimate («when a deal is lost, tell me»), so the picker still offers everything, and what the attribute buys is that a reader can tell which is which without counting positions in a funnel the owner names himself. That is #59's own rule, broken by a spec written after it: **choose a stage by kind, never by position.**

619. **m8 invented a funnel column and a custom field on every run and removed neither.** #183 calls configuration worse than data in an e2e — it changes what every screen renders — and this file had been adding a stage and a field per run since it was written. On this container's long-lived database the funnel had grown eight extra columns, which is also the ground truth behind #601: a spec that indexes into the stage list is reading a list that gets longer every time anybody runs the suite. Cleanup is a final TEST and not an `afterAll`, because a page opened in a hook has neither baseURL nor a session and the cleanup silently does nothing (round 57 shipped exactly that lie). Three locator lessons in it: a field is rendered as its own EDIT FORM, so its label is an input VALUE and `getByText` finds nothing; the fields of an object sit inside a collapsed panel, so nothing in there is clickable until it is opened; and the first version reached the stage's row through `locator('li, tr, div').filter({hasText}).last()`, which **passed alone and failed in the full suite** — how much div soup matches depends on what else the screen is showing that run, so `StageTools` rows carry a `data-testid` now. A cleanup step verified only in isolation is a cleanup step verified in the one condition it will never run in. Proven by counting the rows afterwards — the residue left behind was exactly the runs that predate the new test, which is the accumulation problem stating itself.


620. **The universal advert door already existed, and nobody had been told its address.** «Reklama ozimizni qolimizda … tiktok hamma platformalarda berishimiz mumkun» reads like a request for an integration per platform, and it is not: `/ariza?manba=tiktok` has worked since round 83 and needs no account, approval, key or review from anybody — it covers TikTok, YouTube, a website, an Instagram bio, a printed QR and any channel that has not been invented yet. What was missing was not a mechanism but the ADDRESS: the owner cannot set up a campaign without the exact URL, and telling him in chat means he loses it the next time he scrolls. So the round's first deliverable is a directory on `/crm/kelganlar` printing, per source, the form link, the bot deep link and (when switched on) the webhook — each in a `readOnly` input that selects itself on focus, because he is pasting these into Google's settings on a phone, where selecting text inside a paragraph is a fight and a long-press on an input is not. Nothing is invented for the bot's name: `getBotUsername()` is the cached `getMe` the client card has always used, and minting an env var for a name the bot can be asked for is a second source of truth.

621. **One endpoint for every platform that posts to a URL, because they differ in exactly two ways.** Google Ads lead forms (which is also how a YouTube lead form arrives — a YouTube advert IS a Google Ads advert), a connector standing in front of TikTok, and the owner's own website all send a lead to a URL we choose, and the only real differences are where they put the secret and what they call the fields. So: `POST /api/leads/in/<source>`, one shared secret PER SOURCE on `lead_sources.webhook_secret` (migration 0068), and two readers — Google's `user_column_data`, and a plain `{name, phone, note}` for everything else. Per source rather than one global key, so switching a channel off is one button and touches no other channel; and the key is GENERATED, never typed, because it gets pasted into a third party's settings page where it will outlive everyone's memory of it. The secret is deliberately accepted from the BODY as well as a header: Google's lead form extension has no way to set a header, and refusing its shape means refusing Google. Rules inherited whole from the Meta door: **404 when the source has no secret** (an unconfigured door must not exist — the `undefined === undefined` failure in another costume) and 404 rather than 403 on a bad key, since a wrong key and no door are the same fact to anybody who should not be here; **never throw**, because every platform reads a 500 as «send it again»; and **one constant answer** for created, joined, recognised-as-a-client and dropped alike (#600). A retired source's door closes with it, red-proven — otherwise a key goes on accepting leads into a channel nobody looks at.

622. **Keeping the whole payload is right, and Google puts the password in it.** `lead_intakes.ref` stores the sender's body verbatim so a platform we have never seen still leaves a trail somebody can follow — but Google's lead form posts `google_key` INSIDE that body, so storing it as sent would write the shared secret into a table the arrivals screen renders and into every nightly backup, where it can never be recalled. `refWithoutSecret` drops `google_key`/`key`/`secret`/`token` case-insensitively, because the sender chooses the spelling and a secret that survives on a capital letter is not dropped. Caught by reading the route back before it shipped, red-proven by narrowing the list, and then proven in the real request path: the stored `ref` of a live Google-shaped POST carries `lead_id` and `campaign_id` and no key. That last step mattered — a service-level test of a form-fed path proves the service, not the system (#531, third appearance), so the round's endpoint was exercised over HTTP with curl before it was called done. It also found the one thing no unit test could: `psql -tAc` on `UPDATE … RETURNING` prints the command tag too, so the probe's first «wrong» answer was a newline in an HTTP header, not a defect.

623. **Instagram and Facebook are different adverts with different money behind them, and the split was one field away.** Round 83 recorded every Meta lead as `meta` and STATED that telling the surfaces apart would need resolving `ad_id` through the Graph. It does not: the lead object itself carries `platform`, and the read that already happens can simply ask for it — `fields=…,platform`, one word. Absent or unrecognised stays `meta`, because «a Meta advert» is still true and guessing would put a lie into the one report that answers which advert is worth paying for. **That report turned out not to exist.** `funnelReport` has computed a per-source conversion since the CRM shipped and NOTHING renders it — the only consumer reads `funnel.stages` for a won count. So «qaysi reklama pul keltiryapti» was unanswerable, not under-answered. It is now a block on the arrivals screen carrying both halves, which answer different questions and neither replaces the other: ARRIVALS counts what the advert actually sent, including the twenty copies of one number that became nothing, and WON money counts what it earned — many arrivals and no leads is a broken form, few arrivals and good money is a channel to spend more on. The money is `sum(quoted_amount)` over WON leads only (round 71 gave a lead its own quote and this is the first report to read it): a quote on an open lead is a hope, and on a lost one it is a price somebody refused.

*(Numbering note: #620-623 above were written as #616-619 and renumbered on
merge — the other session's round 87 minted the same four the same day. That is
the fifth DECISIONS collision and the second week running; the migrations did
not collide this time because round 84's rule was followed and `origin/main`'s
journal tail was read before 0068 was minted, but main's #616 had not landed
yet when I read it. **Read the tail AND the open pull requests.**)

624. **A truck is loaded by weight and space, and the screen counted boxes.** The owner, standing at a truck: «kamera ochilganda yuklash payitida hozir gs code harif va nechtadan nechta ortilgani ko'rinyabti, shu yerda iloji bo'lsa kg kubi va sredniy vesini ko'rsatishni ixcham qilib.» The totals line had carried the running kg and m³ since the plan editor's density work, but the LIST — the thing the loader actually reads, one row per lot with its code, its goods and its N-of-M — carried nothing but a count, so «how heavy is the next one» could only be answered by walking back to the plan. Each row now carries a SECOND line, `kg · m³ · kg per box`, and the totals line a fourth figure, `ø` for the average box on board. Second line and not a wider first one, because the first is already full: measured at 360 px the row is 302 px — code 84, goods 177 and already truncating, count 25 — so three more numbers would have left the goods name 43 px, and a crate row, whose «goods» is the list of what is inside it, nothing at all. The per-box weight is the number a person standing at the tailgate acts on: 24 kg goes by hand, 80 kg waits for the forklift, and no total tells you that. **The divisor is boxes that carry a weight, never all of them.** An unweighed box adds nothing to the sum, so counting it in the denominator reports a lighter average the more of them there are — the number would degrade exactly where the data is worst, which is the one place a person might trust it. It is also the only count a QUICK batch has: `doneCount` reads the plan, a quick batch has none, and the average would have vanished on precisely the ad-hoc load — the CEO's urgent cargo — that this line is most useful for. Row spacing went 1 to 2 in the same change: a row is two lines now, and at the old spacing the gap INSIDE a row equalled the gap between rows, so the grey weights read as belonging to the code beneath them.

625. **The fourth number is the one that would have rescaled the page, and only a made-up truck said so.** The totals line was one flex row of four un-wrappable spans. At the fixture's numbers it measures 328 px inside a 328 px box and looks perfect, which is exactly the trap — the numbers on a real truck are bigger. Forced to a 12,500 kg / 105 m³ load the row runs past the viewport, and a row wider than the viewport makes mobile Chrome zoom the WHOLE page out (#400) — on a SCANNING screen, where every tap target then sits somewhere other than where it is drawn, with a camera open and a queue behind you. `flex-wrap` costs nothing and removes the class of failure rather than the instance; each «·» moved INSIDE the span that follows it (round 78's rule) so a wrap can never leave a separator hanging at the end of a line. Measured all three ways: realistic numbers one line at 20 px, an absurd load two lines at 40 px, document 360 px in both. The lot rows needed nothing — a paragraph wraps by itself. LESSON, and it is the reason this one was caught: **measure the widest value the field can hold, not the value the fixture happens to have.** A layout that fits the seed data is a layout nobody has tested.

*(Numbering note: #624-625 were written as #620-621 and renumbered on merge —
the other session's advert round minted the same numbers the same evening. That
is the SIXTH DECISIONS collision. My own round-84 rule already says to read
`origin/main` before minting; what this one adds is that reading main is not
enough when the other session's work is sitting in an unmerged pull request.
**Read the tail AND the open pull requests.**)

626. **The scanner picked its decoder by whether an API EXISTS, not by whether it works — and the Kashgar warehouse found out.** The owner: «kamera ochilyabti lekin qr codelarni oqimayabti», and then the sentence that settled it — «menda qr code ishlayabti lekin qashqar skladchimizda ishlamayabti». One codebase, two phones, so the difference IS the defect (#476's rule: when a user reports two things and one WORKS, the working one is the control group). `if (DetectorCtor)` was a bare presence test on `window.BarcodeDetector`, and the zxing fallback was reachable ONLY in the else-branch. On Android the global is there while the reading itself is done by a platform module delivered through Google Play Services — which a phone bought in China does not have. There it constructs without complaint, never throws, and returns an empty list for ever: a perfect live picture that decodes nothing, with no error anywhere to notice. The documented feature test, `BarcodeDetector.getSupportedFormats()`, was never called (`grep` returned nothing). Three changes, and the third is the one that actually saves the warehouse: the format list is asked for and an answer without `qr_code` disqualifies the detector; a `detect()` that THROWS disqualifies it immediately (a call that errors will error again for the same reason); and a detector that has not read one code in **25 live frames** — about four and a half seconds — is handed over to zxing anyway. That last rule is deliberately unable to tell "broken" from "not pointed at a label yet", and resolves the ambiguity toward the decoder that needs nothing from the platform: being wrong costs some CPU, and being wrong the other way costs a warehouse that cannot scan. The zxing chunk is now fetched at scanner START rather than at the moment of failure — these are the offline screens, and a chunk that must cross the warehouse wifi exactly when the decoder gives up is a chunk that will not arrive. Both decisions live in `scan/decoder-choice.ts` because the component needs a camera and a browser to run and a decision does not (#166). Proven end to end, not by reading: `scripts/dev-scan-decoder-probe.mjs` plays a Y4M of a real box's QR through Chromium's fake camera at the real unload screen — Kashgar's phone in a bottle is a `BarcodeDetector` that reports `['qr_code']` and returns `[]` for ever. **Red-proven by raising the trial threshold to `Number.MAX_SAFE_INTEGER`: 166 detect calls over thirty seconds and the counter never moved; restored, it hands over at call 25 and reads.** The stand-in is a fixture and not a race (#278) because nothing on the page loads a BarcodeDetector of its own.

627. **Every way the camera can fail looked exactly like every other way: a black square.** One `catch` covered the whole start-up and its comment said "camera unavailable (denied / desktop without cam) — HID still works", which is true and useless to a warehouse operator holding a phone. Permission refused once, another app holding the camera, an insecure origin, and the decoder above all rendered the identical black rectangle with no text — so the person who could tell us which one it was could not tell it apart himself, and neither could I: the first three rounds of this investigation were spent asking him to describe a square. The component now carries a `cam` state and says which: «HTTPS kerak» when `navigator.mediaDevices` is undefined (which is what a plain-http origin looks like from JavaScript — measured, not assumed: `isSecureContext:false`, `mediaDevices:"undefined"`, `getUserMedia` throws a TypeError), «ruxsat bering» on `NotAllowedError`/`SecurityError`, and a plain "did not open" otherwise. Plus the quiet one: a camera that has been live for twelve seconds and read NOTHING now points at the manual-entry button that has been sitting under it since M3. **The `http://` reading was investigated and REFUTED as an explanation for this outage** — the session cookie is `secure: NODE_ENV === 'production'`, so on an insecure origin nobody gets past the login screen at all and the state "scanner dead, everything else normal" cannot exist. The message stays, because the day somebody does open the plaintext port that round 81 left published, the app should say so instead of showing a black square.

628. **One foreign QR jammed a phone's whole outbox for ever, and the truck's cargo was then declared missing.** Found by the audit round, not by the owner — it is the more expensive of the two defects. `/api/scan/sync` validates the body as `z.array(itemSchema).min(1).max(200)` with `code` bounded to 40 characters, `flushScans` sent the ENTIRE queue in one request, and any non-200 threw. So one supplier's own QR on a Chinese carton — a `tmall.com` URL of 45 characters, which the unload screen queues ON PURPOSE because reality wins at unload — made the server refuse the whole body, and the retry sent the same poisoned body every fifteen seconds for ever. The same shape at 201 queued scans after a long offline stretch. The screen said **📴 offline while the phone was online**, because the caller treated a refusal as a network failure; the counter kept climbing green; and `finishUnload` then flagged every box still `in_transit` as `missing_in_transit`, so cargo physically standing in the warehouse was recorded as lost in transit, the client was not told it had arrived, and it could not be issued. Four changes: the flush slices at `MAX_PER_SYNC` (the server's own ceiling, restated where the reader can see it); a 400 BISECTS the slice until the rows the server actually refuses are alone and only those are dropped, so one bad code no longer costs the good scans behind it; a 401/403 does NOT drop anything (a session that expired makes those rows sendable again after a login, and discarding a real scan because of it would lose cargo) and is reported as itself; and `isSendableCode` refuses the unsendable code at the door with a sentence — «bu bizning kod emas» — instead of queueing it. Deliberately NOT done: widening `/api/scan/sync`'s unload gate to accept `scan.load` at the origin the way `/planned` does. `/planned` is a READ and the sync route is the WRITE, and turning a refusal into an acceptance is an access change wearing a bug fix's clothes; the refusal is now visible, which is what was actually missing.

629. **A photograph belonged to the desk the goods were received at, not to the cargo — so it went dark the moment the box moved.** The owner, testing as the Kashgar warehouse operator: «bazi tovarlarning rasimlar ochmayabti». Some, and the some is the point. The `receipt` and `receipt_lot` branches of the attachment gate asked `inScope(actor, receipt.warehouseId)`, and a receipt's warehouse is where the goods were RECEIVED — which in a China→Uzbekistan consolidation business is almost never where they are. Measured on his own data the same hour: of **4,403** goods photos, **3,041** were still at the receiving warehouse, **737** had moved to another one and **625** were in transit or already issued — so **1,362, thirty-one per cent, could not be opened by the operator standing next to the carton**, which is exactly who photographs are for. The rule now widens to where the cargo IS, and the widening is a restatement rather than an invention: `wms/search` and the bot's lookup already judge a box by the warehouse it is standing in or, while it is in transit and standing in none, by the TWO ends of the truck it is riding. `cargoNearActor` filters in SQL and not over a fetched page, because a receipt can carry hundreds of boxes and a `limit` here would answer "not yours" about the one box that is. It is deliberately NOT a permission change: nothing new is readable to anybody who could not already open the same receipt card and the same stock row. Red-proven by making the helper return false — the two new cases (moved, and on the truck) go red while "a third warehouse the cargo never touched" stays refused, which is the assertion that says the widening has an edge.

630. **The wrong way to take a feature from one warehouse is to take it from a role.** «Qashqardagi skladchidan tezkor yuklashni olib tashla.» A quick batch is a truck with NO plan — no agent verdict, no approved line list, nothing for the customs paperwork to be built from — which is the right tool for an ad-hoc internal move and the wrong one where a truck crosses a border. The button reads `batches.depart_close`, so the obvious fix is to untick that grant; measured on his data, that grant is held by admin, logist, super_admin and warehouse_manager, and **`warehouse_operator` does not hold it at all**. Unticking it would have (a) taken quick loading from Yiwu too, since a role is company-wide, and (b) taken DEPART and CLOSE with it, which is most of the job it is meant to allow. So the flag is per WAREHOUSE — `warehouses.allows_quick_batch`, migration **0069**, additive and DEFAULT TRUE so nothing changes anywhere until he unticks the box — following `issues_to_clients`, which round 68 made a per-warehouse setting for exactly this kind of reason. Three places must agree and a tripwire says so: the form posts it (with the hidden `off`, #171's fifth appearance), the action parses it defaulting to true for a row that predates the column, the screen offers only origins that allow it, and **the service refuses it anyway** — a hidden control is not a rule, and a hand-posted form would otherwise still create the truck (#531). The DESTINATION list is untouched: the flag is about who may skip the plan, not about where cargo may go.

631. **An unguarded cleanup hook hides the failure that caused it.** Writing #629's fixture, the whole describe failed with `UNDEFINED_VALUE: Undefined values are not allowed` — a postgres.js bind error with no table and no column in it. Twenty minutes went into the inserts. The real error was three lines away in `afterAll`, which deletes by ids the FAILED `beforeAll` never assigned: postgres refuses the undefined bind, vitest reports the hook's error, and the actual failure — `duplicate key value violates unique constraint "clients_code_unique"` — is nowhere on screen. Guarding the deletes made it appear instantly. And the duplicate itself is **#598 for the second time**: two fixtures minted from `Date.now()` in the same millisecond. The counter that fixes it has its own trap, which cost the next run — `` `AC${STAMP}${seq}`.slice(0, 12) `` puts the counter where the truncation eats it, so both rows came out identical anyway. **A discriminator goes at the FRONT of a bounded string.**


632. **A health check that has never been run on a fresh install is a health check nobody has tested.** `ops/bootstrap.sh` curls `http://localhost:3000/api/health` on the HOST, and round 81 removed the `3000:3000` port mapping five rounds earlier — it had been serving the login form over plain HTTP straight off the VPS's address, outside TLS and outside the proxy. So since that round every healthy fresh install has ended with «App 120 soniyada ko'tarilmadi» while the app sat there answering perfectly. Nobody noticed because nobody bootstraps a new server often; the first person to hit it was the owner, mid-move, on the one machine where a false alarm is most expensive. The probe now runs INSIDE the container. The rule this joins: a change that closes a door has to be followed to every caller of that door, and the callers nobody exercises weekly are the ones that will be wrong when it matters.

633. **The container was listening on one address and everything was probing another.** Next's standalone `server.js` binds to `process.env.HOSTNAME || '0.0.0.0'`, and Docker sets `HOSTNAME` to the container id — so the app bound to its bridge address alone. Caddy reaches it either way through `app:3000`, which is exactly why this survived a year unnoticed, and its own startup log states it in a form that reads like a typo: `Local: http://7c2424fa61ee:3000`. What does not work is `127.0.0.1` from inside the container, so a health probe or a debugging session gets ECONNREFUSED from a process that is running fine — and the first fix for #624 got the container right and the address wrong, which cost another round trip during the move. `ENV HOSTNAME=0.0.0.0` in the runner is the root cause; it is not a widening, because the port is still unpublished and only the compose network can reach it. The probe also asks the container's own hostname, so it is correct on images built before that line existed — which is every image already deployed.

634. **The migration count after a restore is not the source server's, and the checklist said it was.** The VPS move restores a dump taken from the old server, and that dump carries the old server's schema AND its `drizzle.__drizzle_migrations` — about 33 rows against a code base that needs 69. The verification step in `docs/DEPLOY.md` said the count should EQUAL the old server's, which followed literally either reads a correct move as broken or accepts a half-migrated database as fine; the second is round 52's failure exactly, where the client book, the stock table and `/o/<code>` read `list_views` at render with no catch and show the error page. It now names the number and says why it differs, with an explicit `docker compose run --rm migrate` beside it — `docker compose up -d` does run it through `depends_on`, but that service is `restart: 'no'`, so a failure leaves the app serving on the old schema with nothing on any screen to say so. Also learned the hard way and written down: after a `git pull` the images must be REBUILT, because migrations are `COPY`ed into them and `run --rm migrate` on a stale image applies nothing and reports success.

635. **The move's own lessons, which are about operations rather than code, and cost the most time.** Lower the DNS TTL a day ahead: the record carried 14440 (4 hours), and that single fact reorders the entire procedure — build and verify the new server while the old one serves, change DNS while the old one STILL serves, wait for propagation, and only then take the five-minute switch with a fresh dump. Stopping the old app before DNS moves takes the system down completely while the domain still points at it; that happened, and the recovery was to start it again and rethink the order. Verify DNS with `dig` against the domain's own nameservers, never the registrar's panel — the panel showed the new IP in its form, printed «Problem occured!» twice, and had saved nothing. And the second dump earned itself: the client count went 327 → 328 between the two, so switching on the first one would have lost a customer somebody entered that hour. What the owner sees last is his own router: `nslookup` answered from `192.168.1.1` with the old address long after the world had moved, which is a cache to reboot, not a fault to debug.

636. **The fifth clock-collision, and this one had a measurable rate.** CI went red on `staff-bot.integration.test.ts` — a file this branch never touched — with `duplicate key value violates users_phone_unique`. The fixture minted `+99893` + `Date.now() + random(0..999)` sliced to seven digits, and the file runs end to end in ~130 ms, so `Date.now()` barely moves and the whole of the uniqueness rests on that random offset. Measured rather than reasoned about: replaying the exact formula for the eight employees the file mints, **576 of 20,000 runs contained a duplicate — 2.9 %**, which is precisely why it passed for rounds on end and then failed on a run that changed nothing. Fixed the way #598 and #592 were: a frozen base plus a **counter**. `padStart(7,'0')` before the slice, because `Number()` on a STAMP with a leading zero returns a shorter phone — unique, but not the shape the rest of the app matches on (the last nine digits, everywhere). **The rule is now five for five: an id a test needs unique must come from a counter; the clock may only make it unique across RUNS.** Worth stating the general form too — a fixture whose correctness is probabilistic is a fixture that will fail on somebody else's change, and be blamed on it.
637. **The three-second tick was a floor, and it was being read as the pacing.** The owner, sending a reply from the CRM with his own Telegram open beside it: «bir oz Queued bo'lib turib keyin jo'nadi, shuni tezlashtirishning iloji bormi». Two waits, neither of them a rate limit. The listener polls `tg_outbox` every 3 s and sends ONE message per tick, which is deliberate — a queue that drains as fast as it can is the burst that gets a Telegram account flagged — but the POLL and the PACING are different things and only the second one is load-bearing. And the screen refreshed every 10 s (round 25's `AutoRefresh`), so even after the message left, «navbatda» stayed on it until the page next asked. Up to thirteen seconds of a delivered message looking undelivered. Now `queueReply` fires a postgres `NOTIFY` on a channel the listener `LISTEN`s on, and the knock pumps immediately — **measured at 1-2 ms** across two connections, against the 2 s a naive probe reported, which was the listener's own connection setup and not delivery. `MIN_SEND_GAP_MS` (1200) keeps the pacing: however loud the knocking, two messages never leave closer together than that, and a knock inside the gap is ignored because the tick already has it. The 3 s poll STAYS underneath — a listener that only reacts to NOTIFY loses everything queued while it was down, which is the whole reason the queue is a table and not a socket. Both ends fenced: a database that will not take the NOTIFY must not fail a reply, and one that will not take the LISTEN leaves the listener exactly as it has always been. The screen half is one expression — `ms={queued.length > 0 ? 2_000 : 10_000}` — so it polls fast only while there is something to watch and costs an idle thread nothing. `OUTBOX_CHANNEL` is exported and both sides import it, with a tripwire that refuses a string literal in the listener: the two ends are separate processes, so a typo would be silent on BOTH and everything would still work, three seconds slower, for ever.

638. **`finance.view` was a door and was being read as a licence over every client's money.** The owner, logged in as a seller: «men sotuvchi accountidan kirdim va financelar ko'rinib yotibti, menga boshqa clientlarning financi ko'rinyabti — unga faqat o'zini ko'rinishi yetarli». Measured on his grants: `sales_manager` holds exactly `clients.view_own`, `crm.leads`, `finance.debt_override`, `finance.view`, `reports.own_clients` — two of which say «own» out loud — and `/finance` called `clientBalances()` with no scoping at all, so a seller read every client's charges, payments and balance plus the company's total debt at the top of the screen. `/finance/reestr` and its XLSX were the same, and `/finance/[clientId]` was one typed uuid away. `finance/scope.ts` states the rule once: `seesAllMoney` is `finance.manage` (the accountant, the owner) or `clients.manage` (whoever administers the book), and everybody else carries `moneyOwnerFilter` = their own id — deliberately NOT `finance.view`, which is the grant that made this a bug.
