Two answers and no third, the shape `warehouseScope` settled on for the same reason (#199): a filter that can quietly become «no filter» fails in the permissive direction. Four places, because a list scoped with an open URL beside it is not scoped: the balances query, the register's ROWS **and its total** (a «jami» over the company above one seller's payments is a screen contradicting itself), the XLSX (a file carrying rows the screen hid is a leak with a filename — #490 one size up), and the per-client ledger, which `notFound()`s rather than refuses so the address bar cannot be used to ask whether a client exists. **The cost is real and was stated to him**: 1,402 of his 1,692 active clients carry NO sales manager, so a seller now sees only what has been assigned. That is the deferred item CLAUDE.md has carried since the access pass — «needs the 17 logins first, or it hides nearly every client from sales» — and he has answered it: hiding is the point. Red-proven by unscoping the balances query and watching the leak test go red while the accountant's unscoped read stays green.

639. **Merging two managers' chats produces a conversation that never happened.** «Chatni ichiga hamma odamni chatini qo'shib tashlayabti all deb.» Two sellers talking to one client from two personal Telegram accounts are two SEPARATE conversations; interleaving them by `sent_at` puts a question next to an answer neither person ever saw and presents it as a record. «Hammasi» is now a choice (`?hodim=all`) and no longer the default: `defaultThreadManager` opens the thread on whoever spoke most RECENTLY — not the busiest historically, because a supervisor opening a client is asking about the live conversation — and returns null when there is nothing to choose between, so a single-manager chat is untouched. `threadManagers` grew a `max(sent_at)` for it. Everyone below the supervision view was already scoped to their own account (round 20) and sees no change at all.

640. **Three coloured bars for three phones is the whole top of the screen.** «Ulangan ishchilar ro'yhati juda katta joy olyabti bunday bo'lmasin» — with a screenshot of `/suhbatlar`: three full-width banners above the search box, one per connected Telegram account, on a screen whose job is the conversation list. The component is now one summary line — worst state, and a count — with the per-account detail one tap away, the fold idiom this codebase already uses for the chat menu and the manager chips. It opens ITSELF only when an account is `signed_out`, because that is not news, it is a job: a person has to go and log in again, and a job behind a fold waits a week. «Not answering» stays folded — the listener catches up by itself and the summary already says how many. New key `bridgeAccounts` ×4.

*(Numbering note: #637-640 were written as #632-635 and renumbered on merge —
the other session's VPS-move round minted the same numbers the same day. The
SEVENTH collision, and the first where both sides had already read `main`
before starting: theirs landed while this branch was being verified. The rule
stands and gains a clause — read the tail, read the open pull requests, and
expect to renumber anyway when a round takes hours.)

641. **The facts panel was painted over the history, and the first measurement said it was not.** The owner: «lentadagi history log telefonda eng pastda turibti, ammo pcda o'ng taraf menu orqasiga o'tib qolyabti pastga tushgan sari». Round 78 had put the history in the `tail` slot at `md:col-start-2 md:row-start-2` — under the sticky facts rail, in the same 24rem column — with the stated reason that nothing about the desktop should move. Measured at 1280×900 on the local card the two never touched, and that measurement was WRONG in the way a measurement can be: the local lead's history is 46 px tall, so it can never rise high enough to reach a rail pinned at the top of the viewport. With a short lenta and a long history — the ordinary case on a real client — the tail and the rail **overlap by 303 px**, and `elementsFromPoint` at the top of the history returns the FACTS PANEL. A sticky item's travel is not confined to its grid row here, and a positioned element paints over a static sibling, so the history scrolls up underneath the rail exactly as he described. Even where they miss, the panel was stranded at the bottom of the narrow column with ~450 px of nothing above it. The tail now renders INSIDE the main cell, under the lenta, where reading order and column agree. **The rule: a layout defect needs the content that provokes it — a fixture that cannot reach the failing geometry is evidence about the fixture** — and it had to be learned twice in one round. Giving the tail a grid row of its own measured identically *while the lenta was the taller column*, which is the lead card; on the DEAL card the rail is taller, a grid row two starts below the tallest thing in row one, and the history opened **548 px of nothing** above itself, sitting at y=1129 in a 1723 px page against y=597 in a 1191 px page inside the cell. Half a screen of extra scrolling to reach the thing he said he could not reach, measured only after an adversarial pass asked which column was taller. The CLIENT card was carrying both halves of the same complaint unfixed — its history was the last thing in the RAIL, which is the middle of the page on a phone and the covered column on a desktop — and it moves to the same slot.

642. **Taking the only door out of the app makes the page it moves to load-bearing.** «Logoutni profil ichiga kirgaz.» The app bar's rightmost 44 px ended the session, one thumb from the theme toggle, on every screen. It is on `/profile` now, in the identity card, beside the name whose session it ends — and `nav.logout` is reused rather than a new key minted, because a second key for the same button is how one locale ends up without it and every screen in that language throws at RENDER time (#163). What the move costs is that `/profile` is now the ONLY way out, and the one morning this codebase has watched a page 500 was a deploy where the schema was ahead of the code (#472) — which is exactly the morning somebody wants to sign in as somebody else. So the five loads under the name (devices, the Telegram link, call devices, the APK, the mute list) are each wrapped: the error still goes to the log under `[profile]`, only the panel is lost, and the name and the button render from the session alone. Source-shape tripwire in the `card-facts` tradition, because both arrangements WORK and nothing about behaviour can tell them apart: the layout must not mention `logoutAction` and the profile must. The bar is down to five controls at 360 px, which is the width round 60 measured when seven of them silently became 33 px; the language switcher deliberately stays on `/profile` on a phone, since taking it back would spend the room that was just recovered on a control most people set once.

643. **«Which truck brought this to Kashgar» is a question about `box_movements`, and four of the nineteen causes answer it wrongly.** The owner, on the Excel a load plan sends to the Chinese agent: «date va qaysi partiyada Qashqarga kelganini chiroyli shaklda qo'shib ber, Qashqarga kelgan partiyasi bo'yicha sort bo'lgan holda». A plan out of Kashgar consolidates what several trucks from Yiwu and Guangzhou brought there, and the agent judging the load reads it truck by truck. The rule is one sentence — **a lot's arrival is the newest movement, over the lot's boxes standing there NOW, that moved a box INTO that warehouse; if that movement is an unload, the truck is named** — and every clause of it is load-bearing, which the live database says out loud. «Landed» (`to_status <> 'in_transit'`) keeps `batch_departed` out: that row is written with `to_warehouse_id = destination` the moment a truck LEAVES, so a rule trusting `to_warehouse_id` alone announces every arrival days early. Asking instead where the cargo is STANDING answers the same question, and was the first version of this clause — wrong for a reason no unit test could show: `departBatch` nulls `current_warehouse_id`, so the moment the plan's OWN truck left, every box fell out of the answer and the file the agent already held re-downloaded saying every carton was walked in, with no truck and no date. **A document that has left the company must not change its claims when it is fetched again**, and the test for it has to depart the truck. «Moved INTO» (`from IS DISTINCT FROM to`) keeps `plan_approved`, `load_scan`, `crate_packed` and nine others out: they carry the same warehouse on both sides because they are status changes, not journeys — and `plan_approved` is NEWER than the arrival for exactly the cargo this sheet is about, so without it every row would name the truck it is being planned ONTO. That is the red proof: strip the clause, re-download the sheet after the agent approves it, and the arrival column answers with the outbound batch. «If it is an unload» keeps `found_at_origin` from naming a truck it never rode, and `found_here` is IN the set — that box did ride the truck, it was simply never scanned off it, and after round 89 it is how a warehouse whose scanner failed clears a whole manifest. One grouped query for the whole sheet, never one per line (#432, #526); measured against his fullest warehouse with no lot filter at all, 57 ms cold and 11 ms warm.

644. **A date on a document is a fact about a warehouse, not about a server.** The agent sheet prints when each block landed, and `docDate` read the instant in UTC. Every Chinese warehouse runs on UTC+8, so a truck unloaded at 07:00 in Kashgar happened at 23:00 UTC the day before: the sheet would date the arrival to a day nobody was working, and the agent is being asked to approve cargo against it. It takes the warehouse's own `timezone` column now, falls back rather than throwing on a zone it cannot parse — a date is better than a crash in the middle of a document — and stays dd.mm.yyyy as TEXT, which is the house rule the packing-photo sheet has printed since it shipped and is the reason the two documents the same agent holds for the same cargo agree. Three more things the design review earned before any of it shipped: the block headings are NOT merged cells, because Excel refuses Sort and Filter over a range with unequally-merged rows and the heading spills across the empty cells beside it anyway; the frozen pane gained `xSplit` so that reaching the third photograph no longer scrolls away the client code that says whose goods it is; and the English half of the two new headers pointed the wrong way round — a column headed «Arrived on» holding a batch CODE, one cell from a column headed «Received» holding the date.

645. **The adversarial pass earned its place a second time, after the code was already written.** Round 92's three designs were judged by four lenses BEFORE any code, and that pass produced `found_here`, the `plan_approved` trap, the warehouse timezone and two Excel details. Re-run against the SHIPPED branch it raised 35 objections; 26 died under per-finding verification and the nine that survived included three defects already committed. (a) The arrival query asked where the cargo was STANDING, and `departBatch` nulls that column — so the agent's sheet answered differently once the truck it describes had left (#643). (b) `foldArrivals` dated a lot from its earliest row of ANY kind, so a lot half walked-in in June and half trucked in July printed «KAS-012 · June» and, because a block heading is the minimum over its rows, back-dated every other client's cargo in that block. (c) `groupByArrival` keyed on the JOINED code list, so a lot that came on two trucks formed a third block of its own and KAS-012's cargo appeared in two places with two subtotals — on a sheet whose entire promise is one block per truck. All three are one shape: **a rule that is right about the common case and silently wrong about the case the business actually has.** The pass's most valuable finding was not a defect but a measurement — that my own layout evidence had been taken at ONE scroll offset and ONE column ratio, and that the broken layout reads zero overlap at rest. Hence `m9zl-card-history.desktop.spec.ts`: it pads the history first, sweeps the scroll, asserts its own preconditions (page height, tail actually on screen) and checks `elementFromPoint` as well as the rectangles, because a panel can be readable and unclickable. Red-proven by restoring the old column — and that proof is impossible with an at-rest assertion, which is the point.

646. **The tray's gate was right where it was asked and wrong for who needed the door.** The owner, the day his sellers connected their Telegrams: «hodim o'zi tanlashi lid/client/shaxsiy deb tanlanadigan adminda chiqyapti, lekin hodimda ko'rinmayapti». He was right, and the data was never the problem — `/suhbatlar/qaysi` scoped its rows per manager from birth; the DOOR demanded `clients.manage`, round 22's deliberate «deciding what the company keeps is not a sales job», which read well until the people whose accounts actually HOLD the unknown chats could not open the screen their own tray fills. The gate is `mayDecideChats` now — the manager's own connected account (`status <> 'signed_out'`; a signed-out row has no session and no chats to answer for), or `clients.manage` for the administrator — asked by the screen, by the door on /suhbatlar, and by all THREE actions, because a screen is a view and an action is a door. What each person SEES is untouched: own rows only, `admin.settings.manage` for everybody's. Red-proven (own-account clause stripped → integration test red) plus a source-shape tripwire that counts the predicate at all four entrances — the defect's exact shape was a correct predicate asked in the right places, wrong by one clause.

647. **Connect-time history is the catch-up mechanism pointed at a window, with one verdict refused.** «Iloji bormi sotuvchilarimiz ulangandan keyin 1 haftalik tarixi bilan tushsin» — yes, and the machinery mostly existed: the listener already re-reads missed messages through the same decide-and-store path a live message takes, idempotent by the unique index. The backfill (migration **0070**, `history_backfilled_at`) walks the dialogs on listener start when the stamp is NULL, takes the last `BACKFILL_DAYS = 7` (capped `BACKFILL_PER_CHAT = 500`), and stamps only at the END — a listener killed mid-pull owes the walk again, and `saveAccount` clears the stamp on every connect so a RECONNECT recovers the week the bridge missed. The one deliberate difference from live (`backfillStep`): the `openLead` verdict is a STOP, not a store — live it mints one lead the moment a stranger writes to a work number, but replayed over seven days of history it would mint a lead for every stranger of the week in one silent burst, and the owner asked for history «hozirgi bor clientlari bilan». A client's `empty` service row is a SKIP, not a stop: the chat identity lives in the peer, and one «call ended» marker must not truncate a client's week. Stated honestly, as it was at the first import: coverage follows Telegram's phone visibility — a client whose number is hidden is not matched, and the tray remains the door for those. NOT verifiable here (no Telegram in this container): the first real connect is watched in `docker compose --profile telegram logs -f tg-listen`, which prints `ulanish tarixi: N ta suhbatdan M ta xabar olindi`.

648. **The calls selector filters what the viewer already holds, which is why it cannot be a leak.** «Telefonda gaplashgani ham userlar aro tanlash imkoniyati telegramga o'xshab.» The thread's own fold (`ThreadManagers`) is reused on the calls panel — chips of takers with counts, «Hammasi», `?chodim=` beside the thread's `?hodim=` on all three cards, each link built by one `cardHref` that carries the OTHER filter (#514: a literal string drops it). The scoping argument is different from the thread's and simpler: `conversationFor` honours `managerId` only under `viewer.all` (round 34) because the filter reaches the QUERY; here the fetch is viewer-scoped FIRST and the chips narrow the fetched rows afterwards, so a hand-typed `chodim` can only ever shrink what the person was going to see. Red-proven at the layer that matters: the viewer fence stripped from `callsForClients` → the seller-sees-own-only test red. The selector is offered exactly when the thread offers its own — supervision view, more than one voice — because for anybody else the rows are their own calls already and a picker would be buttons that change nothing. «Hammasi» stays the default for calls where round 91 made it a choice for chats: interleaving two typed conversations forges a dialogue; a call log is one voice per row and interleaves nothing.

649. **«Javob kutmoqda» could only say two things, and it was computed four times.** The owner, at a truck: «habar javobsz qoldi deb warning berishni chatni ichiga kirgandan keyin tohtatish — negaki klient chatga nuqta qoygandur, misol uchun ok yokida agree yokida got it, shunda bunga sales manager javob bermaydi lekin warning turibti … javobsz qoldi degan narsa juda yomon korinyabti». The mark meant «the newest stored message came IN», and that sentence was restated by hand in FOUR independent places — `listConversations`, `chatBadges`, `salesFlowCounts`, `unansweredChats` — which is #513 broken four ways: a predicate that decides what a screen shows belongs in one place or the screens disagree. It is `crm/waiting.ts` now, pure and unit-tested, with THREE states and no others: **new** (the client wrote and nobody has seen it — the alarm), **seen** (read, no answer written; «ok» needs nothing from anybody), **answered** (we replied, or a reply is on its way out). `chatNeedsAnswer` is `state === 'new'` and is the only thing the red badge and the Telegram nudge ask. The cost is stated rather than hidden: a manager who reads a REAL question on their phone and then forgets it now leaves no alarm behind, which wants a «remind me» button and is deliberately not in this round — one new idea at a time.

650. **The «seen» signal is Telegram's own, because inventing a second one would have been a new fact about an EMPLOYEE.** Opening a dialog on a phone sends a read receipt and Telegram tells every other device, including our listener (`UpdateReadHistoryInbox` — a raw update, since gramjs models `NewMessage` and `EditedMessage` and nothing else). So the system copies a fact that exists whether it stores it or not, instead of recording who opened whose customer's chat and when. Migration **0071** is `tg_chat_reads`, primary key (manager, peer) — the pair the listener already knows and the only pair the state needs. `GREATEST` in the conflict clause, and not as tidiness: Telegram redelivers updates out of order after a reconnect, and a stale one arriving late would move the pointer BACKWARDS and resurrect an alarm the manager had already dealt with — which is the exact behaviour this round exists to remove. Our own thread screen and the 💬 dock set the mark too, because to the owner both screens are the chat — but under the same law, and the law is a WHERE rather than a check anybody could forget: `markThreadRead` counts the rows whose `manager_user_id` IS the reader, so a supervisor (round 33 `seesAllTg`) opens the same screen, matches nothing and silences nobody. Red-proven by deleting that one clause. It is a POST from an effect and never a write in the server page: App Router prefetches the thread link from every hover on the list, and a prefetch that marked chats read would clear warnings for conversations nobody opened.

651. **Two live defects, both found by writing the predicate down rather than by anyone reporting them.** (a) `tg_outbox` was never consulted at all, so a reply TYPED in the CRM left the alarm standing until the listener delivered it — and for ever while company-wide sending is off, which is how `tg_sending_enabled` ships. A typed answer is an answer; the queue is where it lives until the socket agrees, so `queued`, `sending` and `sent` all count and `failed`/`cancelled` deliberately do not — those are precisely the cases where the customer is still waiting and nobody knows. (b) `salesFlowCounts` carried its own hand-written `DISTINCT ON` with **no `client_id IS NOT NULL`**, and postgres groups all NULLs together: since round 82 gave lead-owned chats a null `client_id`, every such chat in the company collapsed into ONE phantom «waiting» on the seller's home screen — a number openable nowhere and clearable by nothing. It reads `chatBadges` now, which is the fourth restatement deleted rather than repaired. The two facts the pure rule needs are fetched with two GROUPED queries over the rows already on the page, never a subquery in the `DISTINCT ON` projection — round 74 measured that shape at 916 ms and had to take it back out of this very function.

652. **The word cost 71 px of the client's name, and that is the row nobody has to act on.** The list gained «✓ o'qildi» for `seen` so the state is visible rather than merely true, and measured at 360 px it took the name from 209 px to 138 — worse than the alarm badge, on the row that matters least. Below `sm` the tick carries it alone and the word returns where there is room (`hidden sm:inline`, the idiom #522 settled on when «Yuborish» became ➤), which puts the name back at 201 px. The dock shows the alarm only and no tick: it is three-quarters the width, and **showing less is not disagreeing** — both surfaces compute from the same `state`. Screenshotted at 360×800 with all three states seeded, then the fixture deleted from the local database, because a conversation left behind is also a number on somebody's home screen.

653. **A regex that states the wrong contract passes until the database is old enough.** `m3-planning` asserted `^M3STO-\d{3}$` on a batch code, and `nextBatchCode` pads to three digits without capping there — so a warehouse's thousandth truck is `-1000`. This container's database has run the suite past 999 and finally said so; a fresh one, and CI, would have gone on agreeing with the assertion for years. Fixed as `\d{3,}`, which is what the code actually promises. The general form is worth keeping beside 67b's lesson about physical row order: **a long-lived local database is not a worse CI, it is a different oracle** — it finds the assumptions that only volume can break.


654. **A document was never «not asked for» — it was the same hole the voice notes left.** Owner, 2026-08-11: «5 ha sen aytgandek klientlar ham bizga fillar jonatishadi. eng togrisini bajar». `tgMediaPlan` fetched photos, then voice notes, and printed «📎» for everything else — so an invoice, a packing list or a clip of the pallet reached the manager's Telegram and reached the CRM as a paperclip. It takes documents now on the photo branch's own terms: the size read from the message BEFORE any I/O, an unknown size refused, a 20 MiB ceiling (the files service's own non-photo limit, and the same number a voice note gets). **What it does NOT do is keep a second list of acceptable types** — it asks `isStorableType`, exported from the files service for this, so a mime STORAGE would refuse is never fetched and never logged as a failure; two lists that must agree is the shape this codebase keeps paying for. Deliberately still a paperclip: stickers (a wall of them is not a record, and their own attribute names them), round video notes (nothing here plays one), link previews. Sending widened the same way — `queueReply`'s `kind !== 'photo'` refusal is gone and the cap is asked PER KIND at both doors, so a photo keeps the photo ceiling and a document gets the document one; `forceDocument` on the way out, because gramjs offers an image-shaped document to Telegram, which re-encodes what it decides is a picture and drops the filename — a PDF the client has to open would arrive as an unnamed blob.

655. **A quoted message is held as Telegram's own id, never as a foreign key.** Owner's item 2: «reply qaysi habarga bo'ldi». Migration **0072** adds `reply_to_tg_message_id` to `tg_messages` and to `tg_outbox`, and both are plain bigints. An FK to `tg_messages.id` fails twice over: a client can answer a message older than anything imported, so the FK would refuse the whole ROW and lose the reply rather than lose the quote; and `purgeExcludedChat` deletes `tg_messages` while deliberately keeping `tg_outbox`, so the FK would either block the purge or cascade a hole into the queue. It resolves through the (manager, peer, tg_message_id) unique index the thread already has — `attachQuotes`, ONE query over the page, three `IN` lists consumed as a map on the exact triple (#598's reasoning, third outing). **An unresolvable quote is normal and renders as an empty strip rather than as nothing**: «this is an answer to something» is still the fact the reader needs, and silence reads as a bug. The reply target rides the queue row so the listener can pass `replyTo`, and `recordSent` carries it onto the echo — a thread that quotes in one direction only reads as two different conversations.

656. **«Forwarded from» has three sources and an empty answer is a real one.** Owner's item 1: «telegram ilovasida forvarded deb accountusername turadi … agar u telefon contactida bolmasa menimcha kelgan habarda kimdan forward bolayotganini malumotlari mavjud». `fwdFromName` reads, in Telegram's own order of filling them: the sender or channel gramjs has ALREADY resolved from the entity cache this connection holds; the `fromName` header, which is what a chat with anonymised forwards sends INSTEAD of an id; and `postAuthor` for a signed channel post. **Never a network call** — resolving an unknown peer would be one round trip per forwarded message on a personal account, which is the traffic pattern that gets one limited. The return is `null` for «not a forward» and **`''` for «forwarded, source hidden»**, and the bubble tells them apart: null prints nothing, empty prints «↪ Yuborilgan: yashirin». One column, two facts, because a boolean beside a nullable name would be two columns that can disagree.

657. **Forwarding, narrowed by his own answer, is INWARD only.** He asked for «habarni forward qiliw» and then answered the question about it with «boshqa mijozga jonatilmaydi» — so nothing leaves for another customer's chat. `shareMessage` hands ONE message to ONE member of staff, in their own Telegram, with a link to the card it came from. That deliberately crosses round 20's fence (a conversation is private to the manager whose account holds it) and the crossing is the point: **the difference between a leak and a hand-over is that a person decided and the decision is written down** — audited on the MESSAGE with `action: 'share'` (its own verb, not `export`, which in this system means a spreadsheet leaving), naming both sides. Three refusals, each re-derived rather than trusted from the form that drew the button: the viewer must be able to READ that message (`shareableMessage` asks the thread's own question over the id, so a hand-posted uuid from somebody else's conversation refuses), the recipient must be an ACTIVE user, and it is never yourself. **The file is not sent** — the bubble's words and a link go, the attachment stays where it is, and the attachment gate keeps deciding who may open it instead of being routed around by a bot.

658. **Five hundred bubbles must not become five hundred React islands.** A ↩ and a ➦ on every message is two controls per bubble on a screen that renders up to 500 of them, and a client component per bubble would hydrate every one of them for a control almost nobody presses. The bubble stays a SERVER component with plain `<button data-reply-to>` / `<button data-share-msg>`, and the two client components that already exist — the composer and the share sheet — find them by **delegation**, one document listener each, in the CAPTURE phase so a bubble inside the dock's own form cannot have its click eaten first. Found while looking at it in a browser rather than by a test: the ↩ was drawn on every message while the composer is shown only when a reply can actually LEAVE, so on a screen with the bridge down it was a button that did nothing. `canReplyNow` is now the one predicate (#513) and the page passes an empty reply label when it says no — the ➦ stays, because showing a colleague works either way. Also from the browser: the share sheet had no panel at all (the `Overlay` scaffold owns the backdrop and nothing else, which is stated in its own comment and was still read as «it will look after itself»).

659. **Meta went live with zero code changes, and the whole outage was one wrong noun.** 2026-08-11, in chat with the owner at the server: round 83's door worked first try — what did not was Meta's side, and every check individually read green while no lead arrived. The token had been minted in Graph API Explorer for the WRONG page («Greenleaffamily», another page the same account administers), so `subscribed_apps` had bound the app to a page no advert runs on; the webhook registration, the signature, the queue and the worker were all sound. The tells, recorded in `docs/ADS.md` §3.5 for the next time: `leadgen_forms` answering `(#10) insufficient privileges` while `debug_token` says the token is a valid PAGE token with every scope — a permissions error that is really an identity error; and the Testing Tool stuck at «Pending» for ever, which also happens for its own reasons in Development mode and so proves nothing either way. The diagnosis that worked was #476's control-group rule again: ask `me?fields=id,name` — the token names ITS page, and every `me/…` call binds to the right object by construction, so the fix (and now the doc) uses `me` everywhere a page id could be hand-typed. Two more facts worth the ink: a page token derived from a LONG-LIVED user token via `me/accounts` is permanent (`expires_at: 0` — the .env token no longer dies in October and leads no longer stop silently on a date), and the decisive end-to-end test needs no cooperation from Meta's tooling: sign the webhook body with the app secret ourselves and POST it to our own door — it exercised signature → queue → Graph read → landing, and the real lead stood in the funnel a second later. The idempotency ledger makes that rehearsal safe: when Meta's stuck delivery finally fires, the database refuses the replay.

660. **The queue is made of people, and the streams are an ordered list read aloud.** Round 83 hung the rotation on a ROLE column because «navbat bilan hammaga» sounded like a statement about a job; the first real Meta lead landed on an admin and the owner corrected the noun: «bizning firmada hamma sotuvchi, lekin hamma lead bilan ishlamaydi». Migration 0073 moves membership to `users.inbound_rota` — backfilled from the role flag so deploy morning changes NOTHING, which matters because he switched the role flag on in production hours before this shipped — and the roles-screen checkbox plus `setRoleInboundRota` are DELETED rather than left accepting posts (round 70's rule; the roles COLUMN itself stays, unread, per the never-drop-in-the-same-release law). On top of the list sit `inbound_routes`: source and/or a case-insensitive word over name+note, members as a users-validated jsonb list, **read top-down with the FIRST match winning** — deliberately not "most specific wins", because a hidden ranking makes the screen lie about itself and the ↑↓ arrows already say whose hands the order is in. One fairness everywhere: `nextInboundOwner(pool?)` is the same fewest-first/NULLS-FIRST query whether the pool is a rule's members or the whole rotation, so a rule cannot be less fair than the queue it overrides. Two boundaries drawn on purpose: routes run ONLY for the new-lead branch (a client's question and a joined enquiry already have their people), and a rule whose members have all been deactivated falls BACK to the general rotation — a VIP stream must not quietly become leads nobody rings.

661. **A fixture helper ate its own distinguishing digit, and the crash surfaced two tests away.** The routing file's phone builder wrote `+9989` + a seven-digit stamp + a two-char tail and then sliced to 13 — cutting exactly the tail's second character, so every "different" phone was THE SAME number: the first arrival created a lead, the rest joined it, the fifth hit the daily cap, and the capped branch's undefined `leadId` reached `eq(leads.id, undefined)` as postgres.js's opaque UNDEFINED_VALUE in a test about rule ORDER (#631's shape — the truncation eats the counter — meeting #598's). The fix is a one-char tail and a comment, but the rule worth keeping is the diagnosis: when an integration failure makes no sense, print what the FIXTURE actually built before reading the code under test. Second armor in the same file: `users.inbound_rota` is GLOBAL where the old role-scoped rota was file-local, so the file snapshots every pre-flagged user, clears them, and restores in afterAll — a long-lived local database is a different oracle (#653), and an exact-winner assertion over a global pool is exactly what it breaks.

662. **The tarjimon: an advert form's own questions become lead fields, one decision per key.** «Bazan targetda yukingiz bormi, baza yukingiz necha kub yokida boshqa har hil savollar beriladi — buni qanday qilib leaddagi fieldlarga ulayman?» The answer is a MAP, not a parser that guesses: `lead_field_map` (0074) holds one row per question key — kub, kg, a lead custom field, or the stored decision «izohda qolsin» (a decision must be a ROW, round 82's include/exclude shape, or the unmapped list nags for ever about keys the owner already answered). Discovery is the arrivals themselves: `lead_intakes.fields` keeps each arrival's raw pairs (capped at 30×80×300 and with the secret's own key names dropped — a public door's storage must be bounded), and one grouped `jsonb_array_elements` query lists what the adverts actually sent, newest sample first; a MANUAL key row sits beside it because on deploy morning the seen list is empty exactly when the owner sits down to map his existing form. The mapped copy lands BESIDE the note line, never instead of it — the lenta stays the complete record of what the person wrote, which also makes every mapping reversible. Checkbox fields parse «ha/bor/да/是» and skip an unreadable answer rather than writing «no»; a mapping whose field was deactivated degrades to note-only rather than storing values no card renders; a mapped key that stopped arriving renders with ⚠ — an agency's form edit retires keys silently and nothing else says so.

663. **Nothing on the landing path may throw on a stranger's input — the review's blocker, made structural.** The three-lens design review (20 agents, 15 confirmed findings, 2 refuted) found the shape before any code existed: `landInboundLead` writes its `lead_intakes` ledger row LAST, so a structured write that throws between `createLead` and `record()` leaves a committed lead with no ledger row — and the replay fence and both daily caps read ONLY that ledger, so a poisoned mapped answer (a text field's pattern rule, a number past `numeric(12,3)`'s nine integer digits) would have turned webhook retries into uncapped duplicate leads. Three fences now: `parseMeasure` refuses anything ≥100 000 (a customer pasting their PHONE into the kub box is the ordinary case, not the attack), the structured writes sit in a catch of their own that degrades to note-only and logs, and the red-proof turns that catch into a rethrow and watches the ledger assertion go red. The same review caught a LIVE pre-existing leak: `secretFrom` reads the webhook key from the body's `key`/`google_key` — a documented, first-class way to authenticate — and `fromPlain`'s known-set did not include those names, so the shared secret fell through to the lenta note as «key: …», past `refWithoutSecret`'s fence which guards only `ref`. Same names, same case-insensitivity, both fences now, red-proven. And the drizzle-numeric rule got its third outing (#503, #608): `inbound_routes.min_m3` arrives as a STRING, and `'10' <= '9'` is true — `listRoutes` converts once at the edge so the pure `matchRoute` compares numbers; a rule with a volume window deliberately does NOT match an arrival whose volume is unknown, and the /ariza door is kept alive for volume rules by `textVolume`'s unit-anchored free-text read («25 kub»), which routes the lead but never fills the card — a guess is good enough to pick whose phone rings, not good enough to print as a fact.

664. **The board was ordered by «last touched», which is a rule about the DATABASE and not about the work.** Owner, 2026-08-11: «cartni boshqa etapga otkazganda ularni tartibi ozgarib qolyabti qaysi ketma ketlikda qoysa usha saqlanib qoladgan qilsa boladimi?» He is describing `ORDER BY updated_at DESC` on the funnel and `created_at DESC` on the deal board, and the symptom is worse than the one he named: moving A and then B put B above A, and an ✏️ edit, an owner change, a bulk sweep or an automation rule reshuffled a column nobody had touched. A card now carries a NUMBER (migration **0075**, `board_order double precision` on both tables) and a drop takes the MIDPOINT of its two new neighbours — one row written per drag whatever the column's length, against the obvious alternative of renumbering everything below the insertion point, which on the owner's fullest column is 4,500 UPDATEs for one gesture. Backfilled from the order the board shows TODAY (`row_number() OVER (PARTITION BY stage_id …) * 1000`), so the deploy changes nothing anybody can see and the first drag is the first difference. **NULL stays legal and is READ as «nobody has placed this», sorting FIRST** — which is exactly where a brand-new card has always appeared — so any write path that forgets the column degrades to the old behaviour instead of dropping a card somewhere arbitrary.

665. **The per-stage cap had to learn the same ORDER BY, or a card dragged low would VANISH rather than sink.** Round 74 caps each open column at forty with `row_number() OVER (PARTITION BY stage_id ORDER BY updated_at DESC) <= 40`, and the board then draws what it was sent. Leave that ranked by date while the board draws by hand-order and the two disagree about which forty cards exist: drag the newest card to the bottom of a fifty-card column and it is still in the FETCHED set (rank 1 by date) but drawn last — and the fiftieth card, which the fetch never included, is what the reader was looking for. Both boards now rank the cap and order the rows with the identical two keys, which is #513's rule wearing a slice's clothes: one predicate, consumed by the rows AND by whatever decides which rows there are. The closed half changed too and the reason is worth recording, because round 47 had deliberately sorted it by date to stop the twenty-card slice being «whichever leads sit in the earliest column» — the numbers are per-column RANKS, so ordering by them globally returns the top of every closed column instead of all of one, which is the same promise kept by different means.

666. **`place` is the drag's and nobody else's.** `moveLead`/`moveDeal` take an optional `{ beforeId }` — the id of the card the moved one landed directly above, or `null` for the end of the column — and **every other door passes nothing**: the one-tap next-stage button, the ⋯ sheet's stage list, a bulk sweep, an automation rule, the cargo trigger. Those get `topOfColumn`, which is «the card just arrived» and is precisely what the board did before any of this existed, so not one existing behaviour moved. The same split answers the question that would otherwise have needed a second flag: the board's `move()` still refuses a same-column press (the button's old guard) and allows a same-column DROP, because the drop carries a landing place and the press does not. **A pure re-order writes no audit row and emits no stage event** — a card dragged one place up its own column is a fact about how somebody reads the board, like the sidebar being collapsed; auditing it would write a row whose before equals its after, which the history draws as a change with no lines in it (#502), and announcing it would fire every «entered stage X» rule on a card that entered nothing.

667. **The arithmetic is pure because two things do it, and the second one is a browser.** `slotBetween(prev, next)` and `compareBoardOrder` live in `crm/board-order.ts` with no imports at all, so the SERVICE that writes the number and the BOARD that has to draw the card in its new place immediately both use the one rule. Uzbekistan→Germany is a third of a second (round 45) and a card that snaps back for that long reads as a drag that was refused, so the kanban keeps an optimistic `ordering` map beside its optimistic `placement` and fills it with the same midpoint the server is about to compute — the neighbours on screen ARE the database's neighbours, because the cap takes a prefix of this very order. Midpoints halve, so the rule also says when they have run out: under `BOARD_MIN_GAP` it answers `'renumber'`, and only the server acts on that (`renumberColumn`, one UPDATE over one stage) because only the server can write a column. That one function does double duty — a column whose gaps are exhausted and a column holding unplaced cards are the same sentence, «these numbers no longer express this order». The unit test plays the realistic worst case out: drop on the same seam until it gives, which takes far more splits than anybody performs by hand, and the answer when it gives is a renumber rather than two cards claiming one place.

668. **A drag that can reorder needs a door for the machine that has no mouse, and 32 px of the height it needed belonged to the layout.** The desktop drag is a mouse's alone and stays so (#510, the owner refused a touch drag twice), so the ⋯ sheet grew ↑ / ↓ above the stage list — disabled at the ends rather than hidden, because a control that appears and disappears under the thumb is a different button every time the sheet opens. Then his second ask: «etaplarning boyi balandroq bolsin pcda ular uzunroq koproq karta korinsin, 2 etapda karta kopayib ketsa scroll chiqib qolyabti yonidan shu korinishi kerak emas». MEASURED at 1280×800 before touching anything: the board ended **67 px above the window's bottom** on /crm and 69 on /bitimlar, and everything above it — app bar, toolbar, the deal board's fold, the drag hint — is content somebody asked for. Of that gap 32 px is the layout's own `md:pb-8`, which clears the phone's tab bar and on a desktop board clears nothing; spending it needs `md:-mb-8` on the board, the vertical twin of the `-mx-4` it already carries, or the page grows a scrollbar under a board built not to have one (#354). Together `10rem` → `6rem` + `-mb-8` = **588 → 652 px** per column on /crm and 540 → 604 on /bitimlar, a whole extra funnel card. The scrollbar is `.no-scrollbar` (`scrollbar-width: none` + the WebKit pseudo-element) and ONLY here: eight columns side by side means eight bars arriving and leaving between the cards, each narrowing its own column as it appears — and hiding a bar hides an affordance, which is why the page's own is untouched.

669. **The photo compressor fetched ITSELF from a CDN, on every photograph, in a company that works in China.** Owner, at a warehouse: «yuk qabul qilganda rasimni kirgizgandan keyin prixodga ruxsat chiqmayabti» — he adds a photo and the confirm button never goes live. `browser-image-compression` does its work in a Web Worker, and the worker it builds does not contain the library: it `importScripts` it from **`cdn.jsdelivr.net`, at runtime, every time**. So `addPhotos` awaits a 57 KB download from a third-party host before a single byte is compressed, and until it resolves there is no thumbnail, no error, and a confirm button that stays grey because `lotsValid()` wants `photoIds.length > 0`. **Measured in this container, which has no route to the public internet: 12.7 s to fail, then a silent fall-back to the main thread** — flat, independent of the photograph's size and barely moved by a 4× CPU throttle, which is what said it was a wait and not a computation. In Yiwu, Guangzhou and Kashgar — where every receipt in this business is actually created — jsDelivr is not reliably reachable at all, so on a warehouse phone the wait is however long that network takes to give up. The fix is one option: `libURL` pointed at our own copy in `public/vendor/`, which the build's service worker then precaches, so the compressor is not merely local but available with no network at all. **0.5 s end to end**, and no request leaves the origin. Red-proven by removing the line and watching cdn.jsdelivr.net and the 12.7 s come back.

670. **Four «known failing» e2e specs were this bug, dismissed for eleven rounds.** CLAUDE.md has said since round 57 that m1×2, m2×1 and m9h fail here because there is «no image service in this container», and every round since has read that sentence and moved on — including the rounds that added the note about how those failures make everything after them unverified locally (#509). Nobody re-read the failure. It is `img[src*="/api/attachments/"]` never appearing within the spec's 10 s timeout, which is the same 12.7 s wait the owner was standing in front of, and the same one the fix removes: **all four pass now**, and the suite went from five local failures to one. The claim was invented to explain a red test and then believed because it was written down. The rule this earns, beside #476's control-group rule: **an explanation for a failing test that nobody has re-run against the failure is a guess with a citation.** A test that has been failing for a year is not furniture; it is the only thing in the building that has been reporting this defect.

671. **A screen that is working must say so, or the operator taps again.** Even fixed, compressing a 12-megapixel photograph on a warehouse phone takes seconds, and the receive wizard said nothing at all between «I chose a photo» and «a thumbnail appeared» — no spinner, no text, the same 📷 tile before and during. That silence is what turns a slow path into a bug report, and it would turn ANY future slow path into the same one: a big photo, a bad upload, a busy server. `busyLots` is a COUNTER per lot rather than a boolean, because the 📷 takes several files at once and the last to land must be the one that clears it; the tile goes ⏳ and refuses another file, the sticky bar says «Rasm yuborilmoqda…» INSTEAD of «you have not added a photo» (both leave the button grey and only one of them is the operator's to act on), and the confirm is disabled while anything is in flight — filing the receipt now would file it without the photograph that is a second from landing on it. The decrement is in `finally`, or a refusal leaves the screen stuck on «working», which is where this round started. The other two upload screens already had an `uploading` state; the one that receives the cargo did not.

672. **The adversarial sweep round 97 launched found five more, all on the same screen and all the same shape: a transient failure made permanent, or reported as something else.** Forty-three agents over six lenses, per-finding verify-to-refute; it confirmed the CDN diagnosis independently — and added the fact that makes it worse than measured. **In this container the connection is REFUSED, so the library falls back after 12.7 s and merely gets slow. A connection that HANGS produces neither of `compressOnWebWorker`'s two exits — there is no timeout in it — so `await imageCompression(...)` NEVER RESOLVES**, proven by holding the route open: `{settled: "NEVER SETTLED", ms: 12040}`. A hung connection is the standard behaviour for a blocked host in mainland China, which is where every receipt in this business is created; and it explains «it worked before» exactly — the service worker's `StaleWhileRevalidate` bucket matches `/\.(?:js)$/i`, so a phone that had once fetched the script kept working from cache, and the deploy that reinstalled the worker is what took the cache away. The other four, each fixed here: **`ensureBucket`'s latch closed even when nothing answered**, so one storage blip during the first upload after a deploy made every upload for the life of the container skip the check — cured only by a restart, which is not a thing a warehouse does at seven in the morning; the latch now closes on an ANSWER (any HTTP status, including 403 and 404 — the store is up and said no) and not on silence. **A pg-boss hiccup 500'd an upload whose bytes and row were already committed**, so the operator was told the photo failed, took it again, and the receipt ended up with two — the thumbnail enqueue is logged and swallowed now, because a missing thumbnail is a slower list and not a lost photo. **Deleting one photo wrote back a render-time snapshot of `photoIds`**, so a photo that landed while the ✕ was in flight was deleted along with it; the write is functional now. **Six failures shared one message** — «check the connection» for a 401 on a deploy morning is a warehouse with perfect wifi being told its wifi is broken, so 401/403 and 5xx say what they are. And the upload had **no deadline at any layer**, which was survivable while the screen said nothing and is not now that it shows ⏳: a spinner that never stops is a worse lie than no spinner, so 120 s and a sentence.

673. **The customer got one «yukingiz keldi» per carton, and the cause was a transaction boundary rather than a slip.** Owner, watching his customers' phones as a truck was emptied: «mashinadan yuk tushganda yukingiz keldi deb har br karobka uchun habar jonatyabti». `ingestUnloadScans` walks the phone's scan queue **one input per transaction** — that is deliberate, and it is what makes the offline queue replayable — and the client's copy was emitted inside it, on the `ReadyForPickup` event. One carton scanned is one message; `unloadRemaining`, the «accept everything remaining» button, feeds one input per short code through the same door, so a single press on a two-hundred-box truck was two hundred. Measured on his data: 34 % of receipts are a single box, so a third of deliveries looked fine and hid it. The half he said was RIGHT is the control group (#476): the CHINESE arrival is one message per RECEIPT, because `ReceiptConfirmed` fires once for a confirmed receipt and carries the lots with it — «umumiy necha karobka necha kg necha kub qaysi tovarlari keldi» is what he asked for at the Uzbek end and what he already had at the Chinese one.

674. **A message that must be true LATER cannot be composed now, so the claim and the sending are two different facts.** Three things had to hold at once and no `if` holds all three. ONCE: the first landed box claims a row in `client_notices` with `ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING` on (client, kind, ref) **inside the movement's own transaction** — round 83's claim-before-work rule, so two scanners on the same truck at the same second cannot both win it, and a rolled-back unload cannot leave a claim that silences the real one following it. TRUE: a message sent on the first carton would say «1 karobka» about a delivery of thirty, so the claim reserves only the right to speak and the TOTALS are read when it is sent, by which time the truck has been scanned. DURABLE: waiting in memory means a deploy inside the window loses the customer's message altogether, which is worse than the noise it replaces — so the wait is a row and the worker retries it. The twenty minutes is a CEILING, not a delay: `finishUnload` pulls the pending notices forward the moment the truck is properly closed, and the window exists only for the warehouse that never presses that button, because a customer's message must not depend on a human habit. The totals are pro-rated by `landed / lot.boxCount` exactly as the batch card computes them, or a lot short-loaded in China is announced at the whole consignment's kilos; and membership is asked through `box_movements`, never `current_batch_id`, because landing NULLs that column and by send time every arrived box has forgotten its truck — #440's trap, red-proven here by three tests going red on the live pointer.

675. **The truck's identity is the company's, not the customer's.** Neither the arrival message nor the cabinet names a batch code, a plate or a driver, and `ArrivedSummary` has no field for one — a batch code is this company's throughput published to anyone who buys a single carton, and that lorry is carrying twenty other customers whose delivery dates it would leak. What the customer is told is a PLACE and a QUANTITY, which is all they asked for. The same rule shaped the cabinet's estimate: the date is printed with the place it is about («Andijan: taxminan 14.08 – 16.08») because a bare date on the Yiwu→Kashgar leg would be read as the delivery date, and the destination is named by warehouse NAME rather than code for the same reason the whole screen stopped saying «YW».

676. **His nine-rung ladder is DERIVED from three columns, not listed anywhere.** «htoyda qabul → htoy sklatdan yolga chiqdi → htoy qirgiz chegara sklatda → sklatdan yuklandi eksport bolti → transitda → ozbga kirdi → rastamojka → olib ketishingizga tayyor → olib ketdingiz». `warehouses.country` and `warehouses.type` already say WHERE — «qirgiz chegara sklat» is his Kashgar row, the only one of type `hub`, and nothing in the code hunts for a warehouse by name — `boxes.status` says WHAT, and the batch carries its two ends, its status and the operator's manual pin. So the ladder survives him opening a second hub or renaming a warehouse. **TWO of his nine are one rung here**: «ozbga kirdi» and «rastamojka» are the same recorded fact, the truck is in Uzbekistan and the cargo has not been released, because nothing in this system stamps the moment a declaration clears — splitting them means inventing a button somebody has to remember to press, and a stage that only advances when a human remembers is a stage that lies. Stated to the owner, and one tap on the batch card away whenever he wants it. The asymmetry inside the rule is deliberate and its reason is in the code: a truck that has ARRIVED at its Uzbek destination with the box still unscanned reads as `in_uz`, while the same shape on the Chinese leg stays `cn_transit`, because his ladder has a rung for the first and none for the second, and a box sitting on a lorry is honestly described as being on the road.

677. **The «necha kunda yetib keladi» he asked for was already in the repository, in his own numbers.** `tracking/map-data.ts` has carried a per-route schedule since the map shipped — Yiwu→Kashgar 144-168 h, «the truck waits at the Chinese border 1-3 days (sometimes more)», Osh→Tashkent 36-48 — and `engine.ts` already turns a departure stamp plus those ranges into what is left, re-anchored by the operator's checkpoint pin. It was written to place a dot on a map, so nothing but the map ever asked it. **No new table, no admin screen, nothing for him to fill in**, and the estimate self-corrects every time somebody pins «still at the border», which is the one leg no schedule can predict. That is the sixth time in this programme a «we lack X» would have been false, and the reason the assembly moved to `tracking/eta.ts` rather than being copied is #513: the anchoring rule replaces the elapsed clock with the time since the pin, which is subtle enough that a second copy drifts, and then the lorry on the operator's map and the date in the customer's phone disagree about the same truck. A date is shown only on a MOVING rung, only when a route exists, always as a range (a midpoint is a promise the office then has to explain), and not at all once the schedule is exhausted — «taxminan kecha» is worse than silence while somebody finds out.

678. **`Intl` renders a short month in Uzbek as «M08 14», and Uzbek is what his customers read.** Found by taking the screenshot, not by any test: Chromium ships no Uzbek month names, so `new Intl.DateTimeFormat('uz-UZ', {month: 'short'})` falls back to a machine format. The Russian one was fine, which is exactly how it would have shipped. The estimate prints numeric `dd.MM` now — no locale data at all, identical in all three languages, and the convention every printed document in this system already uses. The general rule: **a browser API that «handles localisation» handles the locales its build ships data for, and the one this business needs most is the one nobody's ICU build carries.** The zone is still resolved through `Intl` (Asia/Tashkent), which is data every build has, so a truck landing at 02:00 local is not announced for the day before.

679. **He took the trade, so «rastamojka» became a rung of its own — and the honest default is what makes it safe.** #676 folded «ozbga kirdi» and «rastamojka» into one stage and stated the cost: splitting them needs a button somebody has to remember to press, and a stage that advances only when a human remembers is a stage that lies. His answer was one line — «ha rastamojka tugadi tugmasini qo'sh» — so migration 0077 adds `batches.customs_cleared_at`, the customs panel on the batch card carries the tap, and the ladder is ten rungs. **NULL means «nobody has said», never «not cleared»**, which is exactly what every truck that departed before this column existed honestly is: they keep the single rung they had, and the split only ever appears on a truck somebody has answered about. It is a COLUMN and deliberately not a fourth `tracking_checkpoint` key, though the three position pins sit two panels away and look like the same control: those answer «where is the truck» and each of them re-anchors the map's clock through `CHECKPOINT_SEGMENTS`, while clearing customs is not a place and must move no estimate at all. The gate is `ved.docs` — the customs manager's fact, the same gate as the firm picker it sits under — and pressing again clears it, because a person who marked the wrong truck must be able to say so. The stamp decides between the two rungs for **all three** ways of knowing the lorry is in Uzbekistan (the pin, the batch's `arrived`/`unloaded`, an internal UZ leg), so a declaration cleared while the truck is still driving to Tashkent shows as cleared, which is what actually happens. The ✅ goes on the panel's collapsed FACE beside the firm's name — round 43's lesson, learned on this very panel: a fold with nothing on it is invisible whatever it holds. Its browser half rides in m9y, which already opens that panel on a real truck, and puts the stamp straight back (#183 — a customs answer is read by every screen that draws it, now including a customer's Mini App). The action itself cannot be integration-tested (it calls `authorize`, which needs a request scope), so the four halves are pinned source-shape in `customs-cleared-wire.test.ts` and the STAMP's effect is proven against the ladder — #531's rule for the third time: a service-level test of a form-fed path proves the service, not the system.

680. **An unlabelled progress strip means nothing, and the owner said so in one sentence.** «Sen qilgan etaplar timeline emasku hech nima korinmaydi u yerda — qaysi etap qacon nima bolganini koradgan qilish kerak.» Round 98's ten equal segments were a designer's abstraction: they encoded the ladder's position but named nothing, dated nothing and measured nothing, so to the person it was drawn for it carried no information at all. The replacement shows the two things he actually asked for, both of which the system already possessed. (1) **The dated history**: every carton's every move has been a timestamped `box_movements` row since M2 — received, departed, landed at the hub, exported, entered Uzbekistan, cleared, ready — and `journey.ts` turns those rows into the package-tracking list every customer already knows how to read (a dot, a thread, a date, a sentence). (2) **The road itself**: «yolni qanchasini bosib otganini korsatadgan» — the schedule's own `progress` figure, computed by `engine.ts` for the map since the map shipped, drawn as a named bar (Yiwu ——🚛—— Kashgar · 64 %) for BOTH legs, the Chinese one and the export one. The rule this earns: **a visualisation that cannot be read without the legend in its author's head is not a visualisation** — names, dates and numbers are the picture.

681. **History lines are EVENTS, not stages, and the two vocabularies must not be merged.** A stage answers «where is it now» and reads stative («yuklanmoqda»); a history line answers «what happened then» and needs a finished verb («yo'lga chiqdi»). Forcing the stg* labels into the history produced sentences like «03.08 — being loaded», so the journey has its own seven-key vocabulary (jrn*), and the two loading rungs deliberately have NO history line — loading is the moment between the shelf and the road, and the departure line carries the fact. Derivation details that matter: `batch_departed` stamps the DESTINATION warehouse (round 92's trap, used here on purpose — the destination's country is what tells the internal Chinese leg from the export leg); the EARLIEST box speaks for the lot (a 200-box unload takes an hour, and «reached the warehouse starting here» is the honest date); the operator's «in Uzbekistan» pin and the customs stamp join through the TRUCK, because the paperwork happens to the lorry while the boxes still sit on it; and the list is ordered by the LADDER, not the clock — two scanners can write minutes out of order, and a history reading «entered Uzbekistan» above «exported» reads as broken however true the clocks are.

682. **The road bar's percent is the map engine's `progress`, through the same one assembler.** `tracking/eta.ts` (#677) now hands the cabinet `est.progress` beside the window, so the truck on the operator's map, the date in the customer's phone and the percent on the customer's bar are one computation anchored by one checkpoint rule. The bar is drawn only on a moving rung with a known route — a percent over an invented straight line would be #677's refusal broken one field over — and it survives the schedule running out: an overdue truck shows a nearly-full bar and NO date, which is the honest picture of «it should be here about now».

683. **Round 98, the first six: a phantom CSS class, a fold the owner overruled, an archive that names its mistakes, a sticker that stops lying, and files that open.** (1) «Dark modeda yozishganda hech nima ko'rinmayapti» — `input-sm` sat on the chat composers, the mention textarea and the tray's controls WITHOUT EXISTING in any stylesheet: Tailwind ignores unknown classes, so those fields kept the browser's own white `field` background under the app's inherited ink — near-white on white the moment the theme went dark. The class exists now (tokens, like `.input`), and `style-cascade.test.ts` grew the general fence: every class in the house component vocabulary (`input-*`/`btn-*`/`chip-*`/`card*`/`label*`/`num`/`section-title`) that a `className` uses must be DEFINED in globals.css or print.css — red-proven by renaming the definition, and its first dry run found `label-frame`/`label-svg` living legitimately in print.css, which is why the check reads both files. (2) The bridge-status fold self-opened for a `signed_out` account (round 91's «a job must not hide»), and the owner's production has had one signed-out manager for days — so the panel stood open on EVERY visit, round 91's complaint reborn. His word wins: always folded, and the alarm survives in the summary line itself (red tone, broken-count). (3) The tray's excluded chats were already an archive — `tg_chat_rules` keeps every decision with a phone snapshot — what was missing was the SENTENCE: `excludedBookMatches` marks an excluded chat whose last-nine matches an active client's `phones`, «⚠ mijoz kodi: GS500», because saying no to a friend is normal and saying no to a customer is almost always a mistake. (5) Claiming unclaimed cargo nulled `unclaimedMarking`, so a box physically stickered `GS500MANIKEN-AL` was reprinted by every screen as `GS500-AL` — the label and the box disagreed from the moment somebody answered whose cargo it was. The marking is KEPT now; safe because «unclaimed» is `clientId IS NULL` everywhere and never the marking's presence (grepped before changing, not assumed); the stock table prints the marking with the client's code small beneath, and the label sheet lets the marking win. (6) `/api/attachments` never sent a `Content-Disposition`, so a PDF's fate depended on the browser's mood; now render-safe types (pdf/image/audio/video/plain text) are `inline` and open in the tab, everything else downloads — with the SAFETY half pinned by a test: nothing executable (html/svg/xml) may ever be inline, or a stored upload becomes a script running on our origin. Word/Excel deliberately stay downloads: the browser cannot draw them and a bundled viewer is its own round.

684. **The sales month on one screen, and «why did we lose them» becomes the owner's own list.** Round 98's item 8 («dunyo standartlarida qanday malumotlar tahlili bolsa hammasini hohlayman va … yopilish sababini listdan belgilaydigan qilishimiz kerak»), built on one schema fact the system did not have: WHEN a card was decided. `updated_at` moves on every edit and `created_at` says only when the enquiry arrived, so «what did we close this month» was unanswerable — migration **0076** adds `closed_at` to leads AND deals, stamped by `closedAtFor` (pure, in stage-law beside `stageWrite`, with a unit test pinning that the two functions agree about what a decision is) in every door that changes a stage: `moveLead`/`moveDeal`, both ✏️ forms, `convertLead`. Only on an actual MOVE — a drag inside the won column is a re-order and must not move the month a win counts in (tested), and a revival CLEARS the stamp exactly as it clears the lost reason. Backfill = `updated_at` for cards already sitting closed: an approximation, stated, and the honest stamp starts with the first move after the deploy. The analytics (`crm/analytics.ts`, one `Promise.all` of grouped queries — #432 at design time) reads TWO CLOCKS on purpose: «new» by arrival day, «won/lost» by decision day, which is how a sales report is read anywhere and is why a lead that arrived in June and closed in July counts once in each month's own column. The screen (`/crm/tahlil`, gate `crm.manage` like the arrivals ledger) is the seven-cell scoreboard, per-day bars (vazifalar's plain divs), the funnel snapshot deliberately OUTSIDE the period, sources and sellers merged from separate created-in-period and decided-in-period queries (a seller row for `owner_id NULL`, because an unclaimed lead hidden from the table makes the totals disagree with the scoreboard), the lost-reason breakdown, and the deals' half of the same period. The DICTIONARY: `lost_reasons` rows are offered by every picker — the kanban's new sheet (`LostReasonDialog`), the lead card's StageMover, the bulk bar's select — and `moveLead`/`moveDeal` REFUSE a reason not on the list once the list exists (`reasonAllowed`, red-proven), because free text made the breakdown unreadable: «narx», «narx qimmat», «qimmat dedi» are one answer three ways. Two boundaries hold it together: the stored value on the card stays TEXT — the label at the moment of choosing — so a rename never rewrites what somebody recorded and history older than the list keeps its words; and an EMPTY dictionary keeps the free-text prompt everywhere, so day one (and every spec written before the dictionary existed) behaves exactly as before — which is also what keeps the e2e suite green with no fixture changes. The integration file snapshots/clears/restores the whole table in beforeAll/afterAll (#653's oracle: one active row anywhere flips the company-wide behaviour) and parks its analytics fixtures in March 2020, a window nothing else in any database can reach.

685. **The analytics page learns every filter a lead can be sliced by — and the review earned its keep before a line was built.** «Filterlarni maximalna qoyish mumkun bolgan narsalarga qoyib ber, source sotuvchi va boshqalar» — the set is manba, sotuvchi, and the narx/kub/kg ranges the boards already speak, with 'none' a first-class value on BOTH pickers («—» is a real row in both tables: no source = hand-entered, no owner = unclaimed, so it must be a real filter too). A three-lens adversarial review of the DESIGN found two blockers the build then absorbed. (1) **The owner filter is attribution, not the board's shared inbox**: `leadBoardWhere`'s ownerId deliberately means «mine OR unclaimed» (round 74 — work routing), and copying that idiom here would inflate every seller's filtered scoreboard by the same unowned pile, disagreeing with that seller's own row in the table BENEATH it and double-counting «Egasiz» into every name; `leadFilterConds` is strict `eq`/`isNull`, the divergence stated in its comment, red-proven by swapping the or() back in. (2) **The deals query's OR must wear its own parentheses**: drizzle's `and()` embeds members verbatim, so ANDing filters onto the bare `open OR closed-in-period` fragment renders `(filter AND open) OR closed` — and the first red-proof for it stayed GREEN, because the test asserted the OPEN cell, which is the arm that keeps the filter; printing the generated SQL showed the CLOSED arm escaping, so the won cells count the whole company while the open cell looks filtered. The assertion that catches it is the won cell (#166's fixture rule: a red proof that will not go red is evidence about the fixture). The rest of the review, all built: the filter type is structurally unable to carry `createdFrom/createdTo` (the board's `dan/gacha` are a created_at range; HERE those names are the period on two clocks, and a smuggled created_at bound silently drops every lead that arrived before the period and closed inside it); the pickers render HISTORY (sources via `listSources(true)`, sellers = active options ∪ every owner in the data — a seller deactivated mid-year keeps a year of numbers, and a value the form cannot render is a value the form deletes, #171); the advanced half folds inside the ONE GET form (two forms wipe each other, #171) with a count badge and a chips row so an active filter can never hide; preset/clear/chip links all go through `hrefWith` over VALIDATED-then-reserialized values (round 65's literal-links lesson, plus: `raw` echoes garbage, so `?narx_min=abc` must not walk from URL to URL); and the source/seller table rows are themselves filter links — tapping a row narrows the page to it, which beats hunting a select on a phone and gives retired sources the door the select alone could not. Found underneath, live on BOTH boards since round 71: the date validator was regex-only and `?dan=2026-02-30` reached a raw `::date` cast as a 500 — and in `readPeriod` the same input was WORSE than a crash, because V8 rolls `2026-02-30` over to March 2nd and the period silently shifted; both now round-trip through Date (#514). Also hardened while in the area: both boards' `hodim` was permission-checked but never format-checked, so a hand-typed non-uuid was a 22P02 500 for a view_all holder. Refuted and recorded: a lost-reason filter (five of seven scoreboard cells zero by construction) and a stage filter (contradicts the two-clock design) — the breakdowns already answer those questions.

686. **`preventDefault` on a checkbox's click cancels the tick itself — so the selection worked for a year while every box drew unchecked.** Round 59's `SelectBox` stopped propagation AND prevented default, because the board card underneath is an anchor carrying drag handlers. But a checkbox's checked state is flipped by the browser's *activation behaviour*, which runs after the event and is CANCELLED by `preventDefault` — React's `onChange` had already fired and stored the id, then the browser reverted the visual state. Every m9zc assertion counted (`bulk-count`, `bulk-result`) and counting stayed green; the owner's «checked bolgani korinmayabti» is the first report from the only oracle that looks at the pixel. The handler keeps `stopPropagation` (the card must not open) and loses `preventDefault`; the toggle moved to `onChange`, where a checkbox's answer belongs. RULE: a click handler on a FORM CONTROL may stop propagation, never default — default IS the control. The e2e now asserts `toBeChecked()` on both boxes, which is the only assertion that can see this class of bug; red-proven by putting the `preventDefault` back.

687. **Whose-cargo cells now state one rule once: the marking wins, the client code sits small beneath (`labels/code-identity.ts`).** Round 98 made the stock table and the label sheet lead with the box's printed marking; the plan view, plan editor, loading screen, unload screen and batch card each still carried their own inline `clientCode ?? marking` — the OPPOSITE precedence, which is the owner's item 8 verbatim: «skladda korinyabti lekin plan berishda unday korinmayabti». The person reading those five screens is standing in front of a carton that says `GS500MANIKEN-AL`; a screen that says `GS500-AL` makes them re-derive the identity by hand at the truck. `codeIdentity(marking, clientCode)` → `{main, sub}` is pure and zero-import so the offline scan screens bundle it; all seven consumers (stock included, refactored onto it) draw `main-letter` big and `sub` in `text-2xs` beneath; the loading screen's manual-pick search matches BOTH compositions, because the operator may type what the box says or what the client's papers say. The `❓` placeholder replaced the plan screens' bare `?` — one vocabulary there too.

688. **What was HANDED rides on the BoxIssued event, in the arrival message's own wire shape.** The client's «yuk berildi» said a box count and a name — the owner's item 6 wants the goods, the kilos and the cubes, which is what the Chinese-arrival message has carried since round 10. `issueBoxes` now puts `lots` (ArrivedLot shape: letter/names/count/kg/m³) plus summed totals on the payload, computed in ONE grouped query inside the same transaction, share-of-lot exactly as the arrival notice does (a box carries no weight of its own). The cabinet renderer draws a handover the way it draws an arrival — same lines, same totals row, ru-name-preferred — and `Array.isArray(payload.lots)` keeps every pre-round-100 event rendering exactly as it did, because the events table holds years of BoxIssued rows with no lots and a replay must not crash or print «0 kg». The staff draft gained the same two numbers behind the same guard. Same round, same shape, item 2: `receiptContents()` is now the ONE grouped query behind both the deal card's picker AND its linked-receipt rows — the enrichment had been written into one of the two readers only, so linking a prixod cost it its goods/kg/m³ on screen («birlashtirib bolgandan keyin yana faqat id korinib qolyabti»).

689. **A lead born from a chat now shows that chat — read-only, behind the same fence.** Round 82 gave `tg_messages` a `lead_id` so an unknown chat could open a lead, and `chatRowsForLead` existed with no consumer: the card that existed BECAUSE somebody wrote showed no trace of what they wrote (item 12). `conversationForLead(leadId, viewer)` is `conversationFor`'s projection, scoping and ordering with the key swapped — own-account rows only, `viewer.all` widens, red-proven by stripping the filter — and `TelegramThread` gained a `leadId` branch used only when no CLIENT resolves, because a converted lead keeps its `lead_id` (rekeyLeadChats) and the same bubbles must not render twice. Deliberately read-only: the outbox is keyed to the client book and the conversation lives on the manager's own phone — a reply box here would be a promise the queue cannot keep.

690. **The History tab prints the NAME behind a recorded id, and only where it is sure.** An audit row records what the form posted — for a picker that is a uuid, so the tab showed «stage: 4f2a… → 91bc…» to the person it translates for (item 4: «qandaydur codelar emas odam tushunadgan nomlar bilan»). `AUDIT_FIELD_REFS` names which columns point into which domain; `collectAuditRefs` gathers both sides of every change INCLUDING the folded rows (an opened fold must say the same words as its summary line); `resolveAuditRefs` runs one `inArray` per domain touched — stage reads BOTH funnels' tables, client prints «GS777 · Name», a history of note edits asks nothing. Two honesty rules make it safe on a MIXED vocabulary: only a uuid-SHAPED value is ever looked up (older writers recorded `warehouse: 'YW'` in the same columns — a lookup keyed on those would print a wrong name over a right value), and a uuid the lookup cannot find prints as it is with no name invented (a deleted stage's id is still the truth about what happened); the resolved name keeps the raw id in the tooltip. The tripwire extended: every ref column must also carry a human label — and its dry run immediately found `dealId` missing one, which is the fence doing on day one what it is for.

691. **The client book pages its RENDER; SQL `OFFSET` was considered and refused for round 68's reason.** The book capped its fetch at 200 and told the owner «refine the search» about the other 1,492 (item 14: «nega hamma royhat korinmayabti»). The obvious fix — `LIMIT 200 OFFSET n` — is wrong HERE specifically because this screen sorts in memory over custom-field columns SQL cannot order by: each page would sort separately, round 68's «#513's lie in pagination's clothes». So /stock's split, verbatim: the FETCH now covers the book (cap raised to `CLIENT_EXPORT_CAP`, killing `CLIENT_LIST_CAP` — the screen and the XLSX finally cover the same set, which the module's own comment had claimed all along), the sort runs over the whole set, and the RENDER slices 120 rows with plain prev/next links that walk with the back button; `page` was already a control param, so a saved view cannot store a position. The refine-search warning survives for the day the book outgrows 5,000.

692. **The deal card's charge door is CLOSED, and the door that remains was widened before the old one shut.** The owner's answer to item 3 was one line — «sotuvchi narx qoyib qoyadi, buni adminga qoldir … yop bitimdan yop buni» — so `ChargeForm`, `chargeDealAction` and the panel are DELETED, not hidden (round 70's rule: a control removed from a screen while the action still accepts posts is hidden, not removed). The design review earned its keep here: the fallback door on `/finance/<client>` fed its deal picker from `openDealsForClient` — open stages only, capped 20 — while the door being closed was stage-blind, and round 26's cargo triggers walk a deal into its won column the moment the last box is handed over. So the commonest correction there is — a charge posted the day after delivery — would have found the picker empty and silently landed with NO deal, invisible to the #251 deferral netting and under-reported by dealProfit for ever. The ledger's picker is now `ledgerDealsForClient`: open ∪ decided in the last 60 days ∪ live deferral, capped 40. The receiving wizard keeps the open-only list — cargo arrives on open jobs.

693. **The lenta answers «what arrived» and «whose words are these».** (1A.) The cargo branch of `clientFeed` carried a box count and nothing else, so the entry read «YW_IN_260725-010 · 1 box» to a man who wanted the goods; one LATERAL over `receipt_lots` now rides in the branch's own jsonb META (the union is POSITIONAL past its first branch — a new top-level column would mean editing six branches at the same ordinal, meta is free), and the bubble gains one muted line: goods (ru-name preferred) · Σkg · Σm³.
