The note branch carries `authorId`, and the reader's OWN notes sit on the right like any messenger — `ml-auto bg-brand-50` REPLACING the tone rather than decorating it, because two background utilities on one element are resolved by compiled-stylesheet order, not className order, and `bg-warn/10` compiles after `bg-brand-50`: the review proved the appended version ships right-aligned but still warn-tinted, with the brand tint as dead CSS no behavioural test can see. Only the NOTE kind aligns (cargo and money are the record, not a conversation) and a machine's note (`created_by` NULL) is nobody's, so it stays left. The cargo test mints its OWN client — the shared feed fixture is read by exact kind-sequence assertions, and a confirmed receipt on it would have broken three of them as collateral (the review's first blocker).

694. **«Yo'lda» on /stock is its own query with its own scope, and `arrived` is deliberately NOT in it.** (5A.) The strip's membership is the LIVE pointer over `in_transit` boxes only: while a truck genuinely drives, `current_batch_id` is exact — and the moment it lands, unload scans start NULLing it, so an `arrived` batch counted this way shrinks towards «Σ 0» exactly when the owner looks, which is #440's recorded trap on the batch card and was on its way back in through this strip until the review named it. Scope is the batch's TWO ends (`warehouseScopeEither`) and the `?wh=` filter matches EITHER end — on the origin's screen the truck is «what left us», on the destination's «what is coming». kg/m³ are the riding boxes' share of their lots, coalesced in SQL and `Number()`ed once (drizzle numerics are strings); zero-box rows are dropped and the list is capped at 20 — this screen's recorded defect history is DOM row count. Deliberately OUTSIDE the Σ line, the table, the sort, the views and the XLSX: those agree with each other about the SHELF, and a truck is not a shelf.

695. **The truck follows the road: snap the fixes, weight the pacing, and let the map refresh itself.** (9a.) Three mechanisms, because «toglar ustiga chiqib ketyabti» had three causes. A driver phone's fix lands tens of metres off the drawn polyline as a matter of course and was painted RAW — `snapToRoute` puts a fix within 0.15° ON the corridor and leaves anything farther alone, because a genuine detour is information the map must not iron flat. `pointAlong` paced the dot with `Math.hypot` over raw DEGREES, under-weighting every east-west stretch by ~23 % at this corridor's latitude — `chordDeg` weights by cos(lat) IN RADIANS, and the unit test asserts the WEIGHT itself (cos 40° ≈ 0.766; a degrees-for-radians slip gives a negative number, so the assertion cannot pass by accident). The page carries `AutoRefresh` (60 s, force-dynamic) and the Leaflet layer redraws its markers from the new props into ONE `LayerGroup` — the basemap and the user's zoom stay put, which is why the group exists. Two real towns joined the spine, with a lesson attached: the Artux bend gets a landmark pin at 0.1° (the straight chord passes 0.225° away, so the pin can go red), and Tianzhu gets NO pin — it sits 0.055° from the chord it divides, it paces the Wushaoling climb rather than bending the drawing, and no honest threshold can see it. A red proof that will not go red is evidence about the fixture (#166), including when the fixture is a mountain town.

696. **A warehouse's dot is the owner's data; the empty box is the trap.** (9b, migration 0079.) `warehouses.lat/lon`, nullable, db-wins-else-dictionary — the whole point of the column is correcting a dot `WAREHOUSE_POINTS` put in the wrong place, so the code keyed dictionary stays as the fallback and the deploy changes nothing until he types. The review caught the recipe everybody writes first: `z.coerce.number('')` is 0, and 0°N 0°E is a real place in the Atlantic that passes every range check — the empty coordinate box would have become a STORED coordinate that then beat the dictionary. `coordField` decides emptiness BEFORE coercion ('' → undefined → NULL) and is red-proven by stripping the preprocess. Stated as deliberate cuts: the corridor drawing keeps its own geometry (coordinates move the MARKER and its popup, not the road), and a db-coords-only warehouse far off the drawing renders on the Leaflet basemap but clips off the SVG fallback's viewBox — his warehouses all live inside the corridor, and a second corridor is its own round.

697. **One person, both staff and client — the bot serves both, and the keyboard is re-derived, never remembered.** (13A.) Telegram reply keyboards are exclusive: sending the staff menu physically REMOVES the cabinet's buttons from the phone, which is why «hodim ham klient» was broken in four places, not one. /start now answers by `startMenuFor` (pure, one place) and a both-chat gets ONE merged keyboard; the staff text catch-all lets any cabinet button label through (`isCabinetText`, derived from the same dictionary as the keyboard because the labels ARE the router, #264), checked BEFORE the «Bajarildi» result capture so pressing «Balans» mid-capture serves the cabinet and leaves the capture armed; and the three replies that used to NAME a keyboard — the language switch, the calc-save, the staff-link success — ask `replyKeyboardFor(chatId)` instead, because the review traced the merged keyboard dying on the first ordinary button-press after /start. Found underneath and fixed in the same round: the `/start <code>` deep link wrote its chat id RAW, skipping the holder check the contact path has always had — a chat already held by another colleague hit the column's UNIQUE index, the throw vanished into `bot.catch`, and the person got silence; the deep link now goes through `linkStaffChat`, which refuses only a DIFFERENT holder (re-opening your own code from your own chat stays a re-link, the case the review demanded a test for).

698. **The batch-code counter walks past taken codes, because a rollback eats the counter's step but not the collision.** The owner's item 7 was a screenshot with digest #2832070603 and «YW va GZ skladlarga tezkor yuklashni belgilab qo'yilgan lekin hatolik berdi»; his server log named it: `duplicate key value violates unique constraint "batches_code_unique" — (code)=(YW-019)`, on repeat, every attempt the same code. The mechanism is the counters table's own transactionality turned against it: `nextBatchCode` bumps the `batch_seq` counter and the batch INSERT then hits the unique index — and the rollback takes the bump back WITH the failed insert, so the counter re-mints 19 for ever. How the counter fell behind the table in the first place doesn't matter to the fix (a restored dump, a hand-entered batch, any historical crash between bump and insert leaves the same disagreement) — what matters is that the OLD code turned a one-row disagreement into a permanently broken warehouse: quick loading died at YW and GZ the moment each prefix's counter reached a taken number. The generator now WALKS: bump, check `batches` for the code, and only a free code is returned — each loop step consumes a number, so the first successful mint carries the counter clear of the whole collision block and the walk never runs again for that prefix. Concurrency is unchanged (parallel minters serialize on the counter row's lock exactly as before, and the unique index stays the final arbiter), and the fix ships with no migration and no operator step: the healing IS the next press of the button. The integration test mints the production shape by hand — a fresh prefix whose `-001` is already taken — and asserts `-002` then a plain `-003`; red-proven by stripping the taken-check (the stuck version returns `-001` and the test dies on the same unique index production did). Also read out of the same log and answered in chat, not code: the `Server Reference ID did not match "x"` lines are stale browser tabs posting yesterday's action ids at today's deploy — benign, cured by a refresh, and the round-9 build banner already tells the phone its screen is old.

699. **The AI round's shape, and why the fences are the feature.** Owner: «biz sistemamizga AI ulay olamizmi bizni sistemadagi malumotlarni tahlil qilib savollarga javob beradgan qilsak», his three answers: BOTH tiers (every staff member + an analyst mode for him), BOTH surfaces (the staff bot's free text + an app screen), NO client-facing AI, and v1 answers only — the model can read, never write. The design went through the house three-lens adversarial review BEFORE any code (security-leak / regression / product-fit) and the review changed it in four load-bearing places recorded below — which is the round's real lesson: an AI door is a new READ PATH over everything, so it inherits every scoping rule this codebase ever earned, and the review found the two places it would have quietly failed to. Tier 1 (`platform/ai/tools.ts`) wraps the exact functions the screens already answer with — `botLookup`, `globalSearch`, `composeMyDayText` — actor threaded through, wms reached by dynamic import (the startBoss crossing); the toolset is built PER ACTOR, so a tool the person may not hold is not in the map and a hallucinated call gets an error tool_result, not an execution (red-proven: gate stripped → a staff actor's run_sql executed). Tier 2 is the super_admin/admin ROLE — round 21's shape, deliberately not a permission: run_sql reads every client, every conversation and all money, and a grantable permission would invite granting that breadth piecemeal. Stated as a reversible widening.

700. **Model-written SQL runs under the database's own ACL, and everything hangs on ONE connection.** The confidentiality boundary is not the prompt and not a string filter — it is `gsr_ai_reader` (migration 0080): NOLOGIN, granted an explicit ALLOWLIST (default deny — audit_log with its before/after blobs, settings, every credential table, `ai_questions` itself and anything a LATER migration creates are invisible by construction), column-granted on `users` (everything but the two password hashes) and `lead_sources` (everything but the webhook secret). Four fences stand in order, each probed live before shipping: the pure vet (single SELECT/WITH, no semicolons, no comment markers — postgres.js `unsafe` EXECUTES a multi-statement string, so the vet is what stops `…; RESET ROLE; …` climbing out of the role, and `RESET ROLE` regaining superuser inside the tx was PROBED, not assumed); the bound `LIMIT $1` parameter, which forces the extended protocol where the SERVER refuses a second statement (42601); `BEGIN READ ONLY`; `SET LOCAL ROLE` with a fail-closed `current_user` assert. THE BLOCKER the review caught (its one true blocker): `SET LOCAL` lives on a SOCKET, and `pgClient.begin` reserves one — a query issued through the module-level `db` runs on a DIFFERENT pooled connection as the app's own superuser, read-write, no timeout, and postgres.js raises no error about it. The runner therefore executes ONLY through the `tx` handle `.begin` hands it; red-proven both ways (role line stripped → sessions and password_hash readable; query moved onto `pgClient` → the same two leak AND the timeout stops firing). Also probed: inside the wrap's `SELECT * FROM (…) __ai` grammar a data-modifying CTE is not even parseable, so no statement that survives the vet can reach the READ ONLY flag at all — recorded in the test rather than faking a probe of it (#494). A restored dump carries the grants but not the cluster-wide role: `SET LOCAL ROLE` then throws and the runner fails CLOSED with a loud log — the fix is the standing restore rule, run the migrations.

701. **A money question answered from raw columns is confidently wrong, so money is FUNCTIONS, not SQL.** The product-fit lens's blocker: the owner's flagship analyst questions are money questions, and money in this system is not a column — it is `clientBalanceUsd`'s signed non-void sum, `cashFlow`'s partner-aware legs, `companyBalance`'s per-currency FX with retired-till honesty (#425/#426/#251/#532's lessons, all of them invisible to a syntactically perfect SELECT that returns a plausible number and no error, so the model's self-healing loop never fires). Three answers: the schema card FORBIDS totalling money from raw `*_usd` columns and says what to use instead; `cash_flow` and `company_balance` are TOOLS wrapping the real report functions; and the ONE money figure SQL may aggregate is the view `v_client_balance_usd` (0080), whose equivalence with `clientBalanceUsd()` is pinned by an integration test over a fixture that includes a voided charge — the test is what stops the view drifting from the formula. The card's table/column inventory is GENERATED from the Drizzle schema at module load (a hand-written card rots into hallucinated columns); only the trap prose is written by hand, and `ai-schema-card.test.ts` holds card, grants and schema to one claim — every card table exists in the schema, the migration's GRANT list matches the card's allowlist both directions, no excluded table in either, and the granted column sets equal schema-minus-secrets so a new `users` column fails the test instead of silently vanishing.

702. **The review found a live pre-existing leak: the bot's balance line never learned round 91.** `botLookup`'s client branch gated money on `finance.view || finance.manage` — the pre-91 rule — and then printed ANY client's balance, while `/finance` has scoped a seller to their own book since round 91 (`seesAllMoney` / `moneyOwnerFilter`). Latent since the calls for a year of bot lookups; what made it urgent is exactly what made it findable: wrapping `botLookup` as an AI tool turns a quiet per-code leak into a conversational interface over the whole client book. The gate now asks both questions — the page-entry permission AND (seesAllMoney OR the client's own sales manager) — red-proven by reverting it (a `finance.view`-only actor read an unassigned client's balance). The rule to keep: **every function an AI tool wraps must be re-audited against the CURRENT scoping standard the day it is wrapped** — a screen's old sins become an API's.

703. **Free first, paid last, and the cost follows the tier.** The regression lens: as designed, «GS777 qayerda» — the owner's own example of a staff question — would have gone to the paid model because it contains a space, for the model to call the free lookup and return the same answer slower. The bot's routing is now: the whole text through `lookupFromBot` (today's exact behaviour), then every code-shaped token (`codeCandidates`, pure — a token carrying both a letter and a digit, which no ordinary Uzbek word does), and only then the assistant — behind `staffForChat` (a customer's words never reach the model or the ledger) and behind `aiConfigured` (no key = exactly the day before this shipped). Models by tier: tier 1 runs on `claude-sonnet-5` with NO schema card — a lookup-wrapper conversation does not earn a frontier model — tier 2 on `claude-opus-5`, where fewer wrong queries do. The daily cap (setting `ai_daily_limit`, 40, 0 = off) is a LEDGER row, not a counter in memory: `ai_questions` (0080) is reserved by the INSERT that checks the cap in the same statement — two racing questions cannot both slip under it, a crash still spends a slot, and the ledger doubles as the audit of every question, every answer and every SQL text the model ran, Tashkent-day-bounded like every «today» the system shows. The menus offer `/ai` only when the key is configured and only to the office roles (the warehouse's questions are codes, already answered free); the route answers everyone, honestly.

704. **The loop is injectable, so CI proves everything but the wire.** `askAssistant(input, deps)` takes `deps.callModel` — the integration tests script the model and drive the tool loop deterministically: the tool round and its ledger row, the refused staff run_sql, the analyst's fenced run_sql, the atomic cap, the honest not_configured (asked BEFORE the ledger — an unconfigured ask writes nothing), and the round budget (a model that never stops asking for tools is cut at 6 with the partial answer kept). The refusals are CODES, not sentences — the bot answers them in Uzbek like every staff string it sends, the /ai screen through the `assistant` i18n namespace in the viewer's locale, and the ANSWER language is the system prompt's instruction (bot = Uzbek, web = the actor's app locale). e2e m9zn is the m9x precedent: CI has no key, so the browser test proves the honest half — the sentence on /ai, no dead chat box, no menu door — and the first live question is watched in docker logs like every external integration before it. Client-sent history is accepted as plain prior TEXT only; tool blocks are structurally unrepresentable in the action's input, so a hand-crafted tool_result cannot be replayed into the model.

705. **The pre-go-live audit: six lenses, verify-to-refute, six confirmed defects — and five of the six were a rule this codebase had already written down, applied in one place and not its neighbour.** Owner, the evening the AI round went live: «avval audit qilib chiq performance bug analis securitylarni tekshirib chiq hamma uje ishlatishni boshlaydi ertada». Fourteen agents over security-of-the-AI-round / auth-and-public-surface / RBAC-scoping / performance / money-and-races / route-by-route authz; every blocker and major then handed to a separate verifier told to REFUTE it, defaulting to refuted unless the failing scenario reproduced from the code as written. All six survivors were real; the sweep also correctly refuted nothing into the report, which is the value of the second pass — the first version of finding #5 was filed «major» and the verifier corrected it to minor with reasons (sessions survive, it self-heals in fifteen minutes), so the go-live gate stayed honest about what actually blocks. **The pattern across the confirmed set: not one was a novel mistake.** Round 91 had already decided that `finance.view` is a seller's own-book grant — the partner screens and the partner-receipt attachment branch never learned it. Round 30 had already decided that a document route gates like the screen it backs — `handovers/[id]/act` and `batches/[id]/manifest` asked only for a login while their four siblings asked for permission and scope. Rounds 48-49 had already decided that a transient send failure is retried and a permanent one is not — the arrival-notice worker settled every zero-delivery attempt as terminal `failed`, and `dueArrivalNotices` reads only `pending`, so one 429 during the burst that follows a truck landing meant the customer was never told, silently and for ever. **The lesson is about REACH, not about care: a rule earns its keep only where somebody remembered to apply it, and the way to find the gaps is to go looking on purpose, one lens per rule.**

706. **The AI round's own defect was not in the fence — it was that the answer held the door.** The SQL fence, the per-actor toolset and the money-view rule all survived the audit unchanged, which is what the design review bought. What nobody had looked at was the DISPATCH: grammy's built-in poller is sequential — one update at a time, each fully awaited — and the same bot serves the customers. `await assistantFromBot(...)` inside the handler therefore froze every cabinet tap, every `/start` and every «yukingiz keldi» flow for as long as one admin's question took: tens of seconds for an opus-5 tool loop over a sixty-table schema card, and up to the SDK's own ten-minute default on a hung connection, because `new Anthropic()` was constructed with no timeout and the loop's 120-second wall clock is only consulted BETWEEN rounds. The owner's own words are that 95 % of customer contact is that channel, so the shape of the bug was «the primary channel appears dead, for as many questions as the shiny new assistant invites people to ask». Fixed in two independent places, as a hang deserves: the answer is dispatched off the middleware and delivered by chat id when it lands (`answerWithAssistant`, everything caught — a background promise that rejects is the one way this could take the process down rather than merely fail one answer), and the model call carries its own 60-second deadline so a stalled socket can never outlive the loop that is supposed to bound it. **RULE: a slow feature added to a shared sequential channel is an outage for everything else on that channel, and the review that judged the feature will not find it — the question «what else is waiting behind this?» has to be asked about the transport, not the code.**

707. **A cap that has never been raced is not a cap, and the proof has to be measured.** `reserveQuestion` claimed in its own comment that «two racing questions cannot both slip under the limit» because the count and the insert were one statement. False, and the audit said so: under READ COMMITTED a statement's snapshot is taken when the statement STARTS, so every concurrent counter reads the same pre-insert world. The first fix — folding a `pg_advisory_xact_lock` into the same INSERT as a CTE — is the one that looks right and buys nothing, for exactly the same reason: waiting for a lock does not get you a new snapshot. **Ten parallel asks against a limit of three granted all ten.** The version that works acquires the lock as its OWN statement inside a transaction, so the count that follows runs on a fresh snapshot after the wait — three of ten, measured. Serialising per USER costs nothing real (one person cannot type two questions at once, so the lock only ever contends with their own duplicates). The test is now a genuine burst of ten `askAssistant` calls rather than a sequential loop, because a sequential test passes on the broken version — which is how the comment came to claim something nobody had ever checked. **RULE: a concurrency claim is a measurement, not an argument; write the burst.**

708. **Verify, then throttle.** `isRateLimited` was asked BEFORE the password was checked, so five wrong guesses against a known staff phone refused that account's own owner their correct password for fifteen rolling minutes — and staff phones are handed out and guessable, which makes the lockout something any bored person can aim at a colleague on the morning twenty people are signing in fresh. The fix keeps both fences and reverses only their order: the ADDRESS net is still asked first, because argon2 is deliberately expensive and this is one Node process, so an unauthenticated flood must be refused before any hashing happens; the ACCOUNT lock is consulted only once a password has actually failed. Round 81's unbounded guess stays closed — every failure is still recorded before the lock is read, so wrong guesses still accumulate and still cap; the only thing that now escapes the lock is a correct password, which was never the attack. The order cannot be integration-tested (`loginAction` reads cookies and calls `redirect`, #531's fifth outing), so the two predicates are proven for real and the SEQUENCE is pinned source-shape.

709. **A red proof that will not go red is evidence about the fixture — twice in one round, and the second one nearly shipped.** The document-route tripwire asserted `toContain('inScope')`; stripping the actual scope check left the IMPORT line behind and the test stayed green. Round 75 recorded this exact shape (`toContain('openDoors')` matching a surviving import) and it happened again the first time somebody wrote a source-shape assertion without thinking about what else in the file contains the word. Re-anchored on the CALL — `inScope(actor, row.originWarehouseId)` — and it goes red properly. The same round produced its mirror image: the bot-dispatch test asserted the file contains no `await assistantFromBot(`, which is false of the CORRECT version, because the off-middleware helper legitimately awaits it. That assertion was wrong about the code rather than the code being wrong about the assertion, and the fix was to scope the claim to the handler half of the file. **Both directions of the same rule: a source-shape test asserts a SHAPE, and the shape has to be the one that only the correct version has.**

710. **«Bugun qo'ng'iroq» filled up and never emptied, and the reason was a filter nobody had written.** Owner, the day the whole staff started: «my day da kelgan leadlar call today bolib yegilib turibti lekin ular bilan ishlash boshlagandan keyin ham usha yerda turibti». `followUps` selected leads by `next_action_at <= today` **and nothing else** — no stage filter at all — so a lead moved to WON or LOST kept its old date and sat on the call list for ever. Every advert lead arrives booked for TODAY (`inbound.ts` stamps it on landing), so the pile grew by itself and could only shrink by hand: the ONLY ways off the list were converting the lead or opening the card, unfolding the ✏️ form and re-dating it, and the day screen offered no action at all. Three changes, and the owner picked the shape of two of them: the list now joins `lead_stages` and takes `kind = 'open'` (the bug); a real stage MOVE clears the follow-up, because moving the card is the work and «bugun qo'ng'iroq qildim» is what it means (his answer: «bosqichni o'zgartirgan zahoti avtomatik tushsin»); and every row carries **«✓ Bajarildi»** and **«Ertaga»**, because a list you cannot close from is a list that fills up. Deliberately NOT done, his answer: overdue OPEN leads stay on the list — a call somebody has not made is late work, and hiding it is not the same as doing it.

711. **Two fixes that mask each other need a fixture that separates them, or the red proof lies.** The stage-filter test first closed its lead by calling `moveLead` — and `moveLead` now also clears the follow-up, so the row left the list for the OTHER fix's reason and the test stayed green with the filter stripped out. #166's rule, hit for the third time in three rounds, and this instance is the sharpest: both fixes were correct, both tests passed, and the test named after the filter was proving nothing about it. The fixture now writes the closed stage DIRECTLY with an UPDATE, which is also the truer test — what it pins is the state production is already in, hundreds of leads closed before this shipped and still carrying an old call date, and those rows are exactly what `moveLead` will never touch again.

712. **Ten to twenty photographs, uploaded one at a time, from China to Europe.** Owner, same morning: «htoydagi skladchilarimiz rasimlarni yuklaganda juda sekin yuklayabti». The first suspicion was the round-97 CDN defect returning, or thumbnails silently missing — the serve path falls back to the FULL-SIZE image when `thumb_200_key` is null, which would make every list heavy without a single error. **Measured on his database before touching anything: 401 of 406 photos have thumbnails**, so that whole theory was refuted in one query and the remaining answer was the obvious one nobody had looked at — `addPhotos` was a `for` loop that awaited each compress-and-upload in turn. Twenty photos cost the SUM of twenty round trips on a link where latency, not bandwidth, is the bill. `runPooled` (four at a time) overlaps them. Bounded rather than unlimited for two reasons that pull the same way: a warehouse Android compressing twenty 12-megapixel photographs at once runs out of memory, and twenty simultaneous uploads over a thin connection finish later than four do. All three upload paths in the wizard got it, because they are the same loop three times. **The lesson is the diagnosis order: the cheap measurement that can REFUTE the interesting theory comes before the interesting theory's fix.**


713. **The archive test was a claim about every other test file, and my round is what proved it.** CI went red on `funnel-archive.integration.test.ts` — a file round 102 never touched. Its assertion was «my third win is among the newest TWO closed leads», and the closed slice is ONE FLAT limit ordered `board_order ASC NULLS FIRST` across every finished column, while each column is numbered independently (0075: a move goes to the top of ITS column). So a lead another file has just LOST legitimately outranks a lead this file has just WON — the test was really asserting what every other integration file had done first, which makes it hostage to vitest's duration-cache file order (#380). Adding tests to `crm.integration` made that file slower, moved it ahead in the run, and its `Dubl B` lost-lead (board_order −6000) took both rows. Two fixes, and the second is the one worth keeping: my own fixture stopped closing a lead with a raw UPDATE that left `board_order` NULL — `NULLS FIRST` put it above everything in the shared slice — and the archive test now asserts its three wins appear **in their own order** within a wide slice, which is the property it always meant and is immune to what else is in the database. **Found on the way, and the sharper half:** stripping the `closedOnly` kind filter left the ORIGINAL test GREEN. Its «no open lead in the closed slice» check looked at the same two-row window, so it only bit if the open lead happened to outrank every closed row — another claim about the rest of the database wearing a query's clothes. Asserted over the wide slice it goes red properly. **RULE: a test that reads a SHARED, capped list must assert something true of its own rows — «mine are present, in this order» — never «mine are at the top», which is a sentence about strangers.**
714. **Ten people creating a client at the same moment froze the whole application, permanently — and the line that did it read like housekeeping.** Owner: «yangi klientga kod berishni korib chiq hatolik ketmayabtimi». `createClient` opened a transaction and then, from inside it, called `getSetting('client_code_prefix')` — which runs on the module `db` handle, i.e. the POOL. A transaction reserves one of the pool's ten connections for as long as its body runs, so that inner call needs an ELEVENTH. With ten such transactions open at once there is no eleventh and never will be: all ten sit `idle in transaction` waiting for each other, nothing times out (postgres.js queues indefinitely), and because the pool belongs to every page in the app, every screen for every person stops with them until the container is restarted. **MEASURED against the shipped code: five at once finished in 792 ms, nine in 121 ms, twelve never returned at all — and `pg_stat_activity` during the hang showed exactly ten backends parked on `begin`, waiting on ClientRead.** The fix is to read the setting BEFORE the transaction opens; the whole change is one line moving up eleven. Reachability, stated honestly: ten simultaneous client creations is not his Tuesday, so this had probably never fired — but the failure is total and unrecoverable rather than slow, which is what makes a rare shape worth closing. Swept the rest of `src/` for the same shape and **this was the only one in all 25 files that open a transaction**, which is why `tests/unit/tx-pool.test.ts` guards the RULE (every transaction body, every pooled name) rather than this function: the defect is not a client-code defect, it is what happens whenever somebody reaches for `db` while holding a `tx`. Source-shape deliberately — the behaviour that proves it is a deadlock, and a test that deadlocks the pool takes its own worker's remaining files down with it.

715. **The generator serialises against itself; what it cannot see is a code somebody TYPED.** `nextClientCode` takes `pg_advisory_xact_lock` as its own statement inside the transaction, which is the shape #707 had just proven necessary — and it works: ten simultaneous automatic creates produced ten different codes, while the same run with the lock removed left only two of the ten alive (the other eight died on 23505). The manual path takes no lock, correctly — a typed code is a fact, not a draw from a sequence — so it can still insert `GS516` between the generator's SELECT and its INSERT. The loser of THAT race was told «bu kod band» — a sentence with no meaning at all for the person who left the box empty, and whose only remedy is to press the button again. The auto path now retries (three attempts) and takes the next free number; a typed code that is taken is still refused first time and every time, because retrying there would silently hand somebody a different code from the one they have already written on the carton. Proven deterministically rather than by a burst: a second connection inserts the code the generator is about to mint and holds its transaction open, so our INSERT blocks on the unique index, then fails 23505 the instant the holder commits — red-proven, the pre-fix version throws `code_exists`. **Refuted while looking, and worth keeping:** the 50-gap grouping rule (#115) is sound on his data and on every edge tried — a lowercase prefix, a prefix ending in a digit, regex metacharacters, leading zeros, duplicates, junk numbers; and codes are never reused, because the scan reads inactive clients too. The generator was not the problem.

716. **A snapshot taken at the top of every test restores the test's own value.** The new race tests need `client_code_prefix` pointed at a throwaway, and the first version re-read the setting inside each test before overwriting it — which looks tidier and is exactly wrong: by the second test the «original» it captures is already this file's own `ZZR`, so the restore puts `ZZR` back and every screen in the database that mints a code inherits it (#183, and it takes the NEXT round to notice). Caught by checking the database after the run rather than by any assertion. Snapshot once, in `beforeAll`, before anything has touched it.
717. **The off-site backup had never run once, and the reason was a binary that is not in the image.** Owner: «systemani toliq audit qil, backup olishni systemasini oylab chiq». The nightly job shells out to `pg_dump`; the app image is `node:22-slim` and installs no postgres client. So every night since the VPS move: ENOENT, an alarm, a thrown job — and `runOffsiteBackup` sits BEHIND that failure in the same function, so not one dump has ever left the machine, whichever destination was configured. The alarm did not help either: `alertAdmins` writes a `notifications` row, and `sendPendingTelegram` settles it as **`muted` with «telegram not linked»** for any admin who never linked their staff chat — an alarm that reports itself delivered and reaches nobody. Meanwhile the compose `backup` service (a postgres:16 container, which does have the tools) has been writing a perfectly good dump to the very same volume the whole time; `restore-test.ts` even carries a comment acknowledging that the compose container «is the one that actually runs», which is the thought that stops one step short. **The fix is not to install anything** — a Dockerfile change adds an apt.postgresql.org dependency to the deploy, on the machine where a build failure costs the most (#472's morning), and it cannot be tested from this container at all. Instead `runBackup` ADOPTS a dump written in the last 26 hours when it cannot take its own, and says which of the two happened, because «the copy is minutes old» and «the copy is up to a day old» are different facts. That also gives the compose script the alarm it never had: if IT stops working, the app finds no fresh dump and raises the failure. `ops/backup.sh` additionally refuses to prune when its own dump comes out under 4 KB — a zero-byte dump with the right name and the right date is the one that deletes the last good copy thirty days later.

718. **The photographs leave the machine too, and the database outranks them.** The database is the SMALL half of this business: it grows about half a gigabyte a year while the object store grows by tens (round 74 measured it). Every cargo photograph, call recording, Telegram file and attached document lived in one MinIO volume on one disk with no second copy anywhere — so the failure the whole subsystem exists to survive would have taken every photograph of every carton with it, and no dump would bring them back. Three rules shape it. **One object is uploaded once**: tens of thousands of them and a backlog that drains over days, so «already done» must survive a restart and cost nothing to ask — a ledger table (0081), keyed by (key, DESTINATION) so pointing the backup somewhere new honestly reads as «nothing is there yet». **Originals only**: thumbnails are derived by `generateThumbnails`, so copying them would roughly double the bytes to buy back something a restore rebuilds (`pnpm restore-objects --thumbs`). **The dump outranks the photographs**: a free Drive is 15 GB shared with the owner's own Gmail, so 2 GB is held back and the photographs stop at it — loudly, with a Telegram alarm, because a backup that quietly stopped is precisely the failure being prevented. The ledger row is written only AFTER the destination reports the size it stored, so an unverified upload leaves no claim behind. Stated to the owner rather than solved in code: at his volumes 15 GB free will not last a year once the calls app rolls out, and the answer is Google One or the S3 bucket already built in round 85 — a fact about his wallet, not about the design.

719. **One photograph nobody can read blocked every photograph behind it, for ever.** The first version walked the pending list oldest-first and stopped a run when a whole batch failed — reasonable-looking, and wrong in exactly the way #221's outbox was: an attachment row whose object is genuinely gone from storage (a lost upload, a file deleted by hand) fails every single night, sits at the head of the queue, and nothing created after it is ever backed up at all. Found by running the integration suite against a database that happens to contain a hundred such rows — the e2e leftovers — and noticing that MY OWN three test photographs never moved. The window now skips past the count of failures already seen this run, so the queue drains around the dead rows instead of behind them; red-proven by restoring the old break and watching not one of the three be copied. **The general shape: a queue keyed on «what is not done yet» needs an answer for the item that will NEVER be done.**

720. **A fence made of a name list is not a fence — and it took two false red proofs to see it.** The first `tx-pool.test.ts` listed the pooled names it knew (`getSetting`, `db.query.`, …) and passed clean over `submitPlan`, which calls `availableByLot(...)`: an ordinary project function whose body happens to run on the pool, and therefore the same total-freeze defect as #714 sitting in the warehouse's plan-submit path. The audit's availability lens found it; the fence had not. It now derives the pooled set by READING the code — every function whose body touches the module handle — and refuses any transaction body that calls one. Two red proofs then stayed GREEN in a row, and each taught a different thing. Putting the violation back at `availableByLot` did not go red because parameterising the function (`handle: Db | Tx = db`) had removed it from the set — so the scan learned a second kind, «pooled unless this call passes `tx`», which is the real invariant. Putting `getSetting` back inside `createClient` did not go red because the declaration regex demanded `(` straight after the name and `getSetting<K extends SettingKey>` has a generic in between — the fence had never seen the function it was written for. Both are #166 again: the test is now anchored on `expect(pooled.keys()).toContain('getSetting')`, because a scan that finds nothing passes everything. **`availableByLot` was also a correctness bug, not only an availability one**: it answered «is there enough stock» from a connection holding none of the transaction's locks.
721. **The seven-lens audit: 26 candidates, 19 confirmed, and the pattern was «one door learned the rule, its neighbour did not» — again.** Owner: «systemani toliq audit qil … hatoliklar yoqligini oylab analyz qb chiq». Sixty agents over backup / money / access / cargo / availability / telegram / schema-integrity, every finding handed to two independent verifiers — a SKEPTIC told to refute it and a REPRODUCER told to walk it from a real entry point — and both had to fail to refute for it to survive. The five backup findings show in the run as REFUTED, which is the pipeline working: the verifiers read the tree after those fixes had already landed. **The confirmed set repeats #705's lesson almost item for item.** Round 91 decided `finance.view` is a seller's own-book grant — the client CARD still asked the pre-91 question, so a seller opened any customer and read their charged, paid and outstanding totals, reachable by clicking (the /suhbatlar row links straight there, and a chat is scoped to the manager's ACCOUNT, never to who owns the client). Round 101 gave two document routes permission + warehouse — the other three batch documents had permission and no warehouse, so a Yiwu operator could pull another country's whole cargo list with a batch id; all four now ask ONE `guardBatchDocument`, because fixing them one file at a time is what let them drift. `/transit` asked for a login and nothing else. Round 706 moved the assistant off the sequential bot poller — the hisoblatish analysis was still awaited on it, with an Anthropic client built with no timeout at all. **The most expensive single finding was #714's own shape one module over**: `confirmReceipt` → `priceControlOnReceipt(tx, …)` → `getSetting` on the pool, in the busiest button in the warehouse.

722. **«Every box must still be on the shelf» was right about cargo and wrong about cartons.** Voiding a receipt, moving it to another warehouse and returning unclaimed goods to their sender all asked that question, and all threw `box_not_in_stock` on the first box that failed it. The rule behind it is sound — cargo that has been loaded, has departed or has been handed over is resolved by the load/unload/issue machinery, not by a correction — but two of the eight box states are TERMINAL. A `lost` carton (a person's written loss) and a `void` one (the surplus from a 20 → 18 miscount, which `editLot` produces routinely) are never coming back to a shelf, so blocking on one is not «resolve it first», it is NEVER: one lost carton out of twenty and that receipt can never be voided, never be moved to the right warehouse, and unclaimed cargo can never be returned to the sender standing at the counter. `splitForCorrection` gives all three doors the same three sets — `act` is corrected, `blocked` refuses, and the terminal rest is left strictly alone, because overwriting a `lost` box to tidy up a correction erases a recorded loss, which is the worse of the two mistakes. `moveReceipt` now also reports the boxes that MOVED rather than the boxes the receipt holds.

723. **A voided receipt grew live boxes, and the guard that should have stopped it passed by being asked about nothing.** `editLot`'s structural lock reads «no ACTIVE box has left in_stock», and on a voided receipt every box is `void` — so the active list is EMPTY and `[].some(...)` is false. The grow branch then minted brand-new `in_stock` cartons with fresh short codes and answered «print 5 labels»: live, plannable, loadable, issuable cargo hanging off a prixod that officially never happened, reachable with no race at all by a colleague pressing Save on a tab opened before the void. One line — the receipt must be `confirmed` — and the red proof is the returned `labelsToPrint: 5`. **The general shape: a guard stated over a FILTERED set is silent about the case where the filter empties it.**

724. **Every path that marks a scan done needs a path that takes the mark back.** The unload screen fills `done` the instant a code is scanned, because the offline queue is the whole design. What was missing was the other direction: a queue flushed after the logist pressed «Tushirish tugadi» is refused row by row with `batch_not_unloading`, and the screen went on reading 150/150 with every lot line green — one dark toast, a single slot overwritten by the next, as the entire telling — while those 150 cartons stood in the warehouse recorded as `missing_in_transit` and the staff summary said «Yetib kelmadi: 150». Two halves, and the first was worthless without the second: `codesToUnmark` takes the mark back for `rejected` and `unknown_code` (never for `not_on_plan`, where the operator is being ASKED, nor `duplicate`, which means the box is already recorded) — and the server had to start naming the code it refused, because all seven unload refusals returned anonymously and the fix would have done nothing at all (#494's shape, caught by writing the test before believing the fix).

725. **A scan that reads comments as code reads the codebase's explanation of the bug as the bug.** The transitive version of `tx-pool.test.ts` reported the client-code generator, which is fixed and correct. The chain: my own sentence «ONE **function for** all four batch document routes» matched `function\s+(\w+)`, minted a pooled function named `for`, and every `for (` loop in `src/` then counted as calling it. Comments are stripped before anything is read as code now, and the anchor test refuses to see `for`, `if`, `while`, `switch`, `catch` or `return` in the pooled set. Two earlier versions of the same fence had each been proven by a red proof that stayed GREEN — one because a parameterised handle removed the function from the set, one because a generic signature (`getSetting<K extends SettingKey>`) hid it entirely — so the test now asserts what the scan MUST find before asserting what it must not.

726. **A red proof is undone by a string edit, never `git checkout` — recorded in #430, and I did it anyway.** Stripping the cost-allocation fix to watch the test go red, I reverted the file with git instead of editing the string back, and took two UNCOMMITTED fixes in the same file with it — the voided-receipt guard and the allocation follow. Caught by grepping for both fixes rather than by any test, because the suite was green either way: the terminal-box tests live in another file and the allocation test had just been proven red by design. The rule is not about tidiness; it is that a red proof deliberately puts the tree in a state where a revert cannot tell the strip from the work.
727. **The staff-notification drain has a claim now, and the two things that made it urgent are both dead.** «sendPendingTelegram has no claim» survived round 105 as a stated-open item because no two drains were supposed to exist; the audit had already shown two ways they did. A partially-failed boot re-ran `startBoss`'s registration list from the top — `boss.work` mints a fresh worker per call, so every queue registered before the failure got a second worker on the retry, under a comment claiming «registration is idempotent» that nobody had ever tested. And the tg-listen container's session-dead alarm called `enqueue`, which called `startBoss`, which booted the ENTIRE worker fleet inside the Telegram bridge — two processes working `notify.telegram` (every staff message twice), and the nightly backup running in a container with no backups volume, which is #253's «dump thrown away on every deploy» resurrected by an alarm. Three fences, one per layer: registrations are NAMED and a per-process set skips the ones that succeeded (unit-tested with a boss that fails on demand); `enqueue` calls `ensureListening()` — `boss.start()` and the queue upsert, never a worker (source-shape, red-provable); and migration **0082** gives the drain a real claim — `status='sending'` + `claimed_at` taken in ONE `UPDATE … WHERE id IN (SELECT … FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED) RETURNING`, with a ten-minute reclaim that puts a dead drain's rows back (attempt counted, terminal at the cap). MEASURED both ways: two exhaustive claimers overlap on zero rows; with the locking clause stripped, on 600.

728. **Deactivation now means removed — from every recipient list, and at the door.** The only removal the app offers is «Deaktivatsiya», and it killed the web session and the staff bot while every Telegram notification kept coming: `usersWithPermission` and `usersWithRoles` joined roles to people and never asked `users.active`, so a logist who left the company went on receiving «boxes missing in transit» and every `DebtApprovalRequested` — a client's name and debt figure on a phone the company no longer controls, indefinitely, with nothing on any screen showing it. The `user_roles` rows survive deactivation BY DESIGN (reactivation gives the job back), which is exactly why the filter belongs in the lists. Asked twice on purpose: the lists stop new rows being queued, and `sendPendingTelegram` settles a queued row as `muted / user deactivated` at delivery — the backlog of somebody deactivated yesterday must stop too. TEST NOTE: the test mints its own user wearing an existing role rather than deactivating a seeded one — `users.active` is global configuration, and a parallel file may be standing on it (#653).

729. **The audit's tail, closed: one lock session, the echo that survives, and the doors that existed three times.** (a) `takeListenerLock` reserved a pool connection PER account, out of the same pool of ten every pump and store in tg-listen uses — the 10th connected manager would have left zero, silently, while the screen read «live» because the Telegram heartbeat needs no database. Advisory locks are per SESSION and a session holds any number, so one reserved connection now carries every account's lock; postgres's reentrancy within a session would answer «yes, you already hold it» to a second take of the same account, so in-process exclusivity is restated as a held-keys set. The exhaustion itself is deliberately not pressed by the test — emptying the shared pool takes the worker's other files with it — so the fence is the diagnostics: N accounts, ONE session. (b) A db blip between `markSent` and `recordSent` lost the thread's only copy of a reply the customer already has (Telegram does not echo a same-connection send, #476): the echo is now BUILT before the first database write, rides inside `unsettled`, and the retry settles both halves. (c) The batch-reader door existed as a copy in `wms/search`, a copy on /transit, and NOT AT ALL on /trucks and /map — which served every truck's plate and route, and /map every warehouse's stock by client code, to any login. One list (`wms/batches/read-door.ts`), four consumers, the menu items promising exactly what the door checks (spelled out in nav.ts because platform must not import wms — the unit fence pins the two lists against each other), stock on /map scoped like the stock screen, trucks by their two ends. (d) The expected-arrival services take the writer's scope: a re-posted warehouseId could plant a promise on another country's list, and a promise id read off a shared screen could close another warehouse's promise — refused now at the SERVICE (#531's rule), judged by the ROW's warehouse, red-proven. (e) `inTransitBatches` counted by the live pointer — the last consumer of #440's trap: a half-unloaded truck drained 180 → 0 on the report titled «what is on the road». It counts `batch_departed` movements now, on the (ref_type, ref_id, cause) index that already existed.

730. **A test on a shared queue claims to exhaustion, or it asserts about strangers.** The first claim tests took «my forty rows are in the first thirty claimed» for granted and went red against gsr_ci's four-figure backlog of pending rows from earlier suites — #713's lesson wearing a queue's clothes. The tests now claim until the queue is dry, assert disjointness and completeness over THEIR rows only, and put every foreign row they parked back to `pending` in `afterAll`, because a claim is a state change other files can see (#183).

731. **Round 107 — the owner's six answered items, judged before built.** His list («yangi klientga kod berilganini bilish imkoni yo'q» / «yutdi deganda bitim ochilib ketsin, kod majburiy» / «bitimsiz yuk kelsa ogohlantirsin» / «admin glavnida dashboard» / «skladchi rasxodni xabar qilsin, logist kiritsin» / «ostatkada yashiklar ko'rinsin») with his answers 1b / 2a-har-biriga / 3 / 4-yetarli / 5-compact / 6-faqat-ekranda. The four bigger designs went through a twelve-judge adversarial workflow BEFORE any code, and the judges overturned or corrected fourteen load-bearing decisions — every one recorded in the entries below with its evidence. The two small items shipped straight: the quick «+» client modal stays open on a banner (big mono code, copy button, card link; `QuickClientResult` grew `code`), and the client card header carries a one-tap `CopyChip`.

732. **Winning a lead is a ceremony — one landing, every other door shut.** `winLead` subsumes `convertLead`: settle the client (mint, or attach by TYPED code), write `leads.client_id` FIRST, rekey calls+chats, ALWAYS `createDeal` carrying the lead's quote, then move through `moveLead` with an internal `viaConvert` no server action exposes (an action taking that flag would BE the bypass — a public action's arguments are the caller's to invent). Shut and red-proven: `moveLead` (`convert_required`), `updateLead` (change-gated, inside the round-87 law's own branch), `createLead` (born-won refused; `leadFormStages` drops won from the LEAD pickers, keeping the record's own — the deal pickers keep `formStages` untouched), `deleteStage` (a closed move-target refused — a mass move must not silently decide a column), the bulk bar (won options gone; the service refuses forged posts as counts). The judges' catches that shaped it: `Number(null)` is 0, so a blanket cast would have minted «quoted $0 by <actor> today» deals from every unquoted lead (per-column null mapping now, red-proven); the mint carries `salesManagerId: lead.ownerId ?? actor` or round 91's money scope hides the new client from its own winner; no wrapping transaction (createClient and createDeal each open their own — a wrapper is #714's freeze, and the client-first write order is what makes a mid-flow crash retry into the «already has a client» path instead of minting twice).

733. **The attach step echoes a NAME, and a re-win opens a second deal on purpose.** A typed-but-existing wrong code would re-key the lead's Telegram thread and calls onto the wrong customer's card, and nothing anywhere clears `leads.client_id` — so the dialog resolves the code server-side and shows «GS777 — kim» before anything is written, and an INACTIVE client is its own refusal (a retired code taking a new job is exactly the typo being caught). A lead already carrying a client (the Telegram tray's, or won-revived-won) confirms «yana bitim ochiladi» and gets a NEW deal per press — deliberate: a returned enquiry is a new job, and deals carry no lead pointer to dedupe against. Production leads already sitting in won WITHOUT a client keep the card's ConvertForm panel as their door (it renders exactly for that state and now wins properly, deal included). Stated, not built: the deferred lead-ownership gate got heavier — any `crm.leads` holder can win any colleague's lead and mint records doing so.

734. **«No deal» split into its two honest halves, and the prixod message says which.** `priceControlOnReceipt`'s deal-less branch now asks whether the client HAS open deals: yes → new `UnlinkedCargo` event («bitimga biriktiring», naming the open deal codes — the price already exists, the job is one tap on the receipt card's round-38 picker), no → `UnquotedCargo` as ever («narx qo'ying»). The ReceiptConfirmed staff text carries the owner's marker on the same fact (`dealLinked === false` strictly, so years of stored events render unchanged — #688's rule). Same recipient road (seller, admins when the client has no seller), alerts mute group, offered as a rule trigger — and a production rule listening to UnquotedCargo now hears one case fewer, which is the point and is said here.

735. **Rasxod xabari: the operator reports, finance CLAIMS.** Migration 0083 `expense_requests` (paired CHECK on rejection, partial unique on `expense_id`). The double-entry race is the common case — two accountants, one open panel — so entering claims first (`UPDATE … WHERE status='open'`), a refused expense RELEASES the claim with the typed inputs kept, a crash between claim and save stays VISIBLE as a done-with-nothing ⚠ row (never silently re-enterable), and `voidExpense` re-opens the request it answered (#528's pair rule, red-proven). The submit authorizes `receipts.create` AT the posted warehouse (#514); the chek photos pre-bind to a client-minted id (#180) and their read branch is uploader ∪ `finance.expenses` — deliberately NOT `finance.manage`, which the customs manager holds without being able to open the screen the file backs (round 91's door-matching rule); the allowlist entry and the authz branch landed in one commit (round 14's rule). Deliberately a queue + Telegram ping and not a Task: `createTask` takes ONE assignee and the deciders are a set — the DebtApproval pair is the shape. STATED to the owner: `finance.expenses` is the buxgalter/admin today; granting it to the logist role is his one-click call and it opens the whole expense book with it. Both new surfaces catch (#472) — a half-applied deploy must not take down RECEIVING for a status list.

736. **The stock screen's yashik layer is gated, predicated, and compares what it prints.** `crateStock`: one grouped pass per active crate — members = `crate_id` + the stock page's own four statuses + `current_warehouse_id = crates.warehouse_id` (round 31's short-loaded member keeps its crateId at the ORIGIN; the bare pointer counted a Yiwu carton into a Tashkent row — red-proven); INNER JOINs make empty/dissolved crates structurally absent. The strip renders only under `crates.manage` — every crate surface is so gated and /stock's own gate is a bare login, so an ungated strip was a company-wide crate read plus links that teleport sellers to the home page. The overflow ⚠ compares values AS PRINTED (kg integer, m³ 2dp): numeric arrives a string and `'300' > '1000.000'` lexicographically (#663), and raw floats can flag two numbers that print identically. `q` deliberately never reaches the strip (outside the sort/views/XLSX, outside the search — stated divergence); `wh` and scope live INSIDE the function; a malformed `wh` reads as absent, not a 22P02 page. Screen-only by the owner's word: no Telegram, no export.

737. **The admin home dashboard exists because the signals cannot be a link tile — and its numbers must not argue.** Mounted EXCLUSIVELY with the role flows (an admin who also carries a working role keeps that working home — round 15's narrowest-job rule, stated to the owner), gated on the AI tier's `isAnalyst` ROLE plus each block's DESTINATION permission (grants are editable data; a home cell must never show money behind a link that bounces). The judges' four overturns: `salesSnapshot`'s month counts moved to the DECISION clock (`closed_at` — `updated_at` re-counted a merely-touched won lead, so /dashboard and this screen printed two different «bu oy yutilgan»; the shared clock is pinned by a test that equates both consumers); the open-deals sum FILTERS to USD and names the leftover count (#701 — a CNY quote added at face value); the zaxira signal has THREE states («sozlanmagan» is the truth on a server that has not done chore #2 — a permanent red teaches the eye to skip the one cell that will matter); the «yuborilmagan» count is one exported `notificationProblemCount` that excludes the by-design `muted` settlements («telegram not linked», «user deactivated» — never zero on this production) and includes 0082's stuck `sending` claims. m9p's tile-pair fence is scoped to a new `home-tiles` testid — the dashboard also links /bitimlar and the spec's bare `.first()` measured the wrong element.

738. **Two process notes from this round.** (a) `pnpm typecheck | tail` and `pnpm lint | tail` report the PIPE's exit code — twice this round a red gate slipped past a `&&` chain and into a commit because `tail` exited 0; check `PIPESTATUS[0]`, or run the gate bare. (b) A test warehouse that any audited action touched can never be DELETED — `audit_log.warehouse_id` is a real FK and audit rows refuse deletes by database rule; deactivating is how a fixture warehouse leaves every picker (#183) without fighting the trail. The crate-strip test also re-met #713 in new clothes: asserting «my crate is on the unfiltered strip» fails on any long-lived database where fifty other crates sort first — the malformed-filter semantic is pinned by equating the garbage call with the unfiltered call, never by looking for one's own row.

739. **The admin dashboard's restyle was judged before it was drawn, and the judges killed the desktop 4-up.** His «chroyliroq ihcham» plan went to three lenses (geometry, regression/fences, house taste). The binding width is not the phone: `max-w-6xl` minus the `w-56` sidebar caps main at 896px at EVERY viewport, so `xl:grid-cols-4` is a 192px card inner for ever — «Контрагенты…» truncating on exactly his desktop — and the 2×2 band stayed; compactness came from the rows instead. What shipped: each block's HEADER is itself the link, carrying the block's headline figure — the kassa total to /accounting/balance (the screen whose exported function computed it, #737's no-argue rule), the stock m³ to /stock (which also un-doubled the 📦 a box-count hero would have printed beside the block's own icon), the open-deals pair to /bitimlar — so the two rows that only restated a header are gone. Three judge rules absorbed: the other-currency count CANNOT ride the Savdo hero (~337px in a 302px header is #400's page rescale) and survives as its own muted line, or round 107's «the USD sum names what it excludes» is silently un-decided; every nowrap value needs `min-w-0` chained through the section and the label span, or the grid's min-width:auto hands the overflow to mobile Chrome's page zoom; and ru «Бугун» is an Uzbek word in Cyrillic — the Russian bundle says «Сегодня», caught by a judge and by no tripwire, because the i18n fence checks existence, never language. The header style is `.section-title` — the vocabulary already existed and `text-[11px]` was about to be minted beside it. Found by LOOKING at the fixture's negative kassa: `usd()` printed «$-1,875»; buried in a row nobody minded for a round, as the HERO it is the first ink on the screen — the sign moved before the $ (call sites writing their own +/− pass magnitudes, so no sign doubles). Labels shortened in all FOUR bundles in the same commit — a missing key throws at render, and a stale long en value breaks the one-line guarantee only for whoever reads en, which is how it would have shipped unnoticed.

740. **Round 108, the third speed round — the slowness was queueing, and the queue was fed by a blind loop.** His «sistema sekin ishlayabti, ayniqsa CRMda» arrived hours after the 84 deploy, so the deploy was the suspect — and the measurement acquitted it: on a clone of his data every screen renders fast alone (/crm 130 ms, cards 60-140 ms, every SQL statement under 0.4 ms warm), but the ONE Node process saturates, and at 16 concurrent requests p50 is 1,070 ms — his sentence, reproduced. What feeds the concurrency all day is what a 14-agent hunt + verify pass confirmed: (a) every open card with a Telegram panel re-rendered its ENTIRE page every 10 seconds through `AutoRefresh`, (b) every 2 SECONDS while `pendingFor` returned rows — and `pendingFor` includes `failed`, which only a human's ✕ ever clears, so one bad reply pinned an open tab at half a heavy render per second FOR EVER (the comment above that line claimed the fast mode «costs nothing» once the queue empties; failed rows keep it non-empty for ever — the design's own cost claim was false), (c) `chatBadges` for a supervision viewer sorted the WHOLE `tg_messages` table per board render (DISTINCT ON, no bound, mixed-direction ORDER BY no index can serve), (d) `activeClientsByPhone` fetched and hydrated the ENTIRE client book per call, per phone, per tick, and (e) the notifications reclaim ran a full-table UPDATE-scan every minute (119 ms at 680k rows) with `notificationProblemCount` doing the same on the admin home (67 ms → 0.04 ms with the partial index). Fixes: migration **0084** (two tiny partial indexes on `notifications`; `tg_messages.edited_at`), `chatBadges(viewer, clientIds)` bounded to the clients the board actually drew (awaited AFTER the rows on purpose — one serial hop beats a full-table sort), `activeClientsByPhone` prefiltered in SQL on the last SEVEN digits (a strict superset of `phonesMatch`'s last-nine rule — equal last-n, n≥7, implies equal last-7, so the JS filter stays the exact arbiter and nothing can be lost), and the pulse (#741).

741. **The chat pulse: the surfaces still refresh themselves, but only when the thread actually moved.** `/api/chat/pulse` + `ChatPulse` replace all three `AutoRefresh` mounts on chat surfaces: the client polls a token — a few indexed counts computed behind the EXACT fences the page reads through (`conversationFor`'s for messages, `pendingFor`'s for the queue) — and calls `router.refresh()` only when it changed. Judged by three adversarial lenses BEFORE code, and the naive `max(sent_at)` token lost on six counts, each now a term and a red proof: `edited_at` (0084 — `applyEdit` rewrote `body` in place and no count moved; a corrected «100 kub» read «10 kub» until an unrelated message), the attachments pair (the listener stores the row first and the photo's bytes seconds later — the refresh the row triggered has already drawn the bubble without its picture), per-status outbox counts + `max(queued_at)` (queued→failed moves no total; dismiss+re-queue in one window nets counts to zero), the stuck bucket (round 48's alarm is a WALL-CLOCK fact — the server evaluates the clock so the count flips exactly once at the crossing), the account-state word (a signed-out account flips the composer with zero writes on the thread), and the server-computed BASELINE passed as a prop (a client-invented baseline swallows anything that landed between render and first poll, for ever on a quiet thread). The 2-second beat SURVIVES — round 91 was fought over exactly that — but `fast` is queued/sending only, never failed, and a 2 s pulse costs a few counts instead of a full page. A 120 s unconditional floor bounds every class the token cannot see. The lead branch re-derives the LEAD CARD's gate (`crm.leads` + ownership) — `canReadTg` alone is satisfied by a bare `ved.docs` grant whose holder can open no lead card, and a pulse must not narrate a conversation no page will show (#514). The card panel polls the CARD's raw client id and the ROUTE re-runs `threadClientFor` per tick (round 32 in the poller, resolution never trusted off the wire). The route joined `/api/version` in the service worker's NetworkOnly list — a cached «did anything change?» answers with yesterday. Eight red proofs by string edit; `chat-controls.test.ts` now pins `<ChatPulse` + the `initial={pulse.t}` prop the same element-anchored way it pinned `<AutoRefresh`.

742. **Three lessons this round paid for again.** (a) **#430 violated a THIRD time** (#726 was the second): the first red proof was reverted with `git checkout` and took the round's own uncommitted `chatBadges` fix with it — caught by grepping for the fix afterwards, not by any test, because the strip's test asserts about the strip. A red proof is undone by the REVERSE string edit, and this file now has that in muscle memory: every later proof in the round carried its restore as a paired `replace()`. (b) A red proof that fails under `vitest -t` for FIXTURE reasons is a false red: the bounded-badges test borrowed its sibling tests' messages, so filtered to run alone it went red with the strip NOT applied (the strip had silently failed to match). The test now mints its own row, and the proof was re-run green→red→green. (c) The stuck-bucket strip stayed GREEN first: the fixture aged a FRESH row, and `max(queued_at)` moved with it, masking the missing term — the row is now born below the threshold and older than its neighbours, so crossing the five-minute line is the only thing that can change (#166: a red proof that will not go red is evidence about the fixture).

743. **Round 109, item 1 — the dashboard he asked for was invisible to him, and the exclusivity was the whole cause.** «Dashboard menda korinmadiku super adminda nega unday bolyabti». Round 107 mounted `AdminDashboard` in the ELSE of `buildHomeFlow`, on the argument that an admin who also carries a working role should keep the narrower home (#737, stated to him at the time). But `buildHomeFlow` answers for ANY working role a person also holds, and his own accounts hold one — an advert lead landed on «bir admin va sotuvchi» the week Meta went live — so the branch that was meant to be the rare both-hats case was HIS case, and the screen he had just commissioned was drawn for everyone except him. It renders on its own condition now, below whatever flow the person has: the day's work first, the company under it. Nothing else moved — same `isAnalyst` role gate, same per-block permission gates, same testids — and the extra cost is paid only by admin/super_admin accounts, a handful of people, on top of a flow they were already paying for. Fenced by `tests/unit/home-dashboard.test.ts`, source-shape on purpose (#166's exception): both arrangements compile and both render for a PURE admin, so the only behavioural oracle needs an actor holding a working role, i.e. a role grant every later spec would then inherit (#183). Red-proven by putting it back in the else-branch.

744. **Round 109, item 2 — a crate is a PLACE, and it says so on both screens.** His «tahta yashikga tirgandan keyin uni sklad stock mashina spiskasida tahta yashikni mestasi kubi kg si korinsin, tegida ichidagi narsalarni soni umumiy kub kilosi kichkina korinib tursa». Round 107 built the /stock half as a chip strip; this round makes it a two-line ROW and gives the truck card the same list from the same component (`components/crate-rows.tsx`) — the two screens answer one question and must never answer it differently (#513's shape in a layout's clothes). The first line is what a loader plans and a forwarder is charged for: code · client code · «1 mesta» · the crate's OWN measured m³ and kg (unmeasured prints «—», never a zero somebody would plan against); the second, small, is what is inside. **Deliberately outside the screens' Σ** — the boxes inside are already counted by the table above, and a crate row joining the total would double every crated cube on a number the tannarx also reads. His answer on the warning («agar ogohlantirish … spiskani tepasida tursa boladi, bolmasam shar etmas») is one sort key: over-capacity first, then code order, in the shared mapper so both screens obey it. The truck's list is `batchCrates`, membership through `batchMemberFilter` and never the live pointer (#440) — an unloaded truck's boxes point at nothing, and the crates would vanish from the document exactly when somebody looks up what came; round 31's short-loaded member, which kept its crateId at the origin, is excluded by the same filter. Gated `crates.manage` like every crate surface, because the row is a door to a crate card that redirects whoever may not open it.

745. **Round 109, item 3 — the road is DOWNLOADED once, not computed, and a through truck drives it too.** His «mapni ham mashina yoli boyicha route qoyish kerak … navigatsiyada yurganda yol boylab chizib beradiku shunday qilish kerak, buni nima trade offlari bor», then his choice: «B — shunday yolni bir marta yuklab olish kerak hamma yonalish boyicha, and tashkent yonalishi boyicha ham». `scripts/fetch-road-geometry.mjs` asks a public routing engine ONCE per corridor leg, simplifies at 0.02° (Douglas–Peucker) and writes `tracking/road-geometry.ts` — 8 legs, ~12 KB, no key, no quota, and nothing in the running app ever calls a routing service. `road(key)` falls back to the hand-drawn corridor if a leg is ever missing, so the file is an improvement and never a dependency. The border legs are fetched SEPARATELY (KA→Irkeshtam, Irkeshtam→Osh): no routing engine drives a lorry through a closed customs post, and the schedule has always modelled the wait as its own stationary leg — which needed one fix, because the wait must sit on the ROAD's last point and not on our own Irkeshtam dot, or the two-point leg creeps across the border for three days (route-shape's stationary fence caught it). **THE MISTAKE WORTH RECORDING: my first fetch used a hand-typed Irkeshtam coordinate 0.95° off the app's own**, so the Chinese leg stopped 80 km short of the post and the Kyrgyz leg started inside China; the endpoints now come from a `P` block copied verbatim from map-data.ts, and a new fence asserts every stored leg begins and ends within 0.15° of the place the app says it is. A second fence states his own correction as a rule: the China corridor never climbs above 43.5°N — «YW GZ dan ketadgan yol urumchiga kirmaydi, togri qashqarga ketadi». And since he asked for «every direction», a truck booked China → Uzbekistan in ONE batch is now composed of the same legs (`cnLeg` + `ka2uzLegs`) instead of falling through to the honest straight line: same road, same border wait, hours added rather than invented — and it gains the three checkpoint segments, so «где машина» has something to re-anchor on a through trip. The remaining fallback is one-way traffic only (nobody drives cargo back to Yiwu), and the existing test that used YW→TAS1 as its straight-line example was rewritten with that reason, not deleted.

746. **What the map screenshots said that no test could.** Two of his three map complaints were about a marker, not a route: «iconlarni yonidagi soni icondan uzoq bolib ketgan» (the warehouse count badge floated away from its icon — it is anchored to the icon's own box now, in both renderers) and «mashina iconini truck iconi bn ozgartirish kerak» (the SVG fallback still drew a generic dot where Leaflet had had a cab-and-trailer since round 98 — one marker in both now). Worth knowing for the next map round: **the renderer he sees depends on the server, not the code.** With `.data/basemap/corridor.pmtiles` absent the page draws the SVG schematic, and that is the file round 98 told him to fetch on the new VPS and nobody has confirmed he ran — so if the road still looks straight on his screen after this deploys, the answer is `docker compose --profile basemap run --rm basemap`, not a bug in the geometry. Both renderers read the SAME `routePoints`, so the corridor is right either way; only the basemap under it differs.

747. **Round 109's follow-up, from the deploy: the lorry, and the label that was standing 24 px away from it.** His «mashinalar iconkasini ozgartirmabsan va mashinalar iconi bn soni alohida turibti, skladlarniki yaxshi chiqibti» — and the last clause is the diagnosis (#476: when one of two things WORKS, the working one is the control group). The warehouse read correctly because round 109's badge fix had wrapped its icon in an inline-block span; the truck's had no such wrapper, and **Tailwind's preflight sets `svg { display: block }`** — so inside the marker's 80 px `text-align:center` box the label was centred as TEXT while the lorry stayed pinned to the wrapper's left edge, and the label being a BLOCK above the icon pushed the icon out of the box Leaflet had anchored. MEASURED in a browser: the lorry was drawn **24 px left and 18 px below its own coordinate**, with the code floating over open map — «alohida turibti», exactly. Fixed by giving the truck the warehouse's shape: the label is `position:absolute` (it cannot displace anything), the svg sits in the icon's own 38×28 box, and `iconSize`/`iconAnchor` agree with it — re-measured, every marker's icon centre now equals its point to the pixel. And the ICON itself: round 98's «truck» was a coloured rounded SQUARE with a 12 px silhouette inside it, so at map scale the eye read a square — a warehouse's twin, which is why he said it had not been changed. It is a lorry SILHOUETTE now, facing left because every loaded truck on this corridor drives right-to-left, with a white halo (`paint-order: stroke`) so it survives a dark road on the real basemap; drawn identically in BOTH renderers, since which one a person gets depends on the server's basemap file and not on anything they chose. `tests/unit/map-markers.test.ts` pins the shared path, the absolute label and the three numbers that must agree, source-shape because the Leaflet half runs in no test here or in CI. CONSEQUENCE, and a real one: a truck that has just departed stands ON its origin warehouse and its mark takes the tap (Leaflet gave it `zIndexOffset: 1000` deliberately; the schematic draws it last) — the bigger lorry made that overlap likelier and m9c went red clicking a covered pin. The spec now picks a pin nothing is covering, by asking `elementFromPoint` rather than trusting `.first()`, and still fails loudly if every pin is covered.

748. **The VED queue, phase A: a calculation request stops being a card's state and becomes a JOB.** `docs/VED.md`'s first phase, and the shape of it was decided by a five-lens adversarial review that ran BEFORE any code and overturned four of the design's own decisions. The biggest: v1 said a second submission would JOIN the open request on that card, held by 0052's partial unique index. But the landing rule sends every repeat client to their newest OPEN deal, so «one open request per card» would have merged Monday's monitors with Thursday's chairs into one row — and the freight band is computed from TOTAL kg ÷ TOTAL m³ (the owner's own tariff table), so the merge would have silently mispriced BOTH consignments, while `requested_by` stayed with whoever asked first and the second seller never heard back. The index is DROPPED in 0085 and a card may now carry several open requests; each is a job, with its own section, route, weight, volume, goods (`calc_request_items`, 0..1000 — his range) and materials. Three races died with it: two submissions both seeing «no open request» and the loser getting a 23505 error page with its note already written; a read-modify-write on a jsonb note list silently losing one seller's material; and a join landing on a request completed a millisecond earlier, where it would never be looked at again. **The queue owns assignment** (round 28 made the seller pick): a request is handed to whichever `ved.docs` holder carries the fewest open ones — the taqsimot rota's counting rule, restated because that one answers «whose lead» and this one «whose calculation» — so it has an owner from second zero and the clock accuses a person; any VED person may take it over, and the claim IS the UPDATE (`WHERE … assignee_id IS DISTINCT FROM me … RETURNING`), because a read-then-write leaves the loser holding an open timed task no request points at, which `completeCalcForTask` then cannot close. **The clock's three ends** are `'lines'` (the deal's goods were saved — hooked into `saveLines` AFTER its transaction and only on a non-empty save, since wiping a deal's lines is a deletion), `'task'` (finished by hand, now carrying the ANSWER: amount, currency, note) and `'returned'` (handed back with a mandatory reason). **Every speed figure filters `completed_via <> 'returned'`**, written into the migration comment and into the one function that computes it: a bounce-back takes ninety seconds, and counting it as an answer makes the person who hands everything back the fastest calculator in the company.
